Zusammenfassung

In dieser Dissertation erweitern wir die neuartige Theorie der stochastischen modifizierten Gleichungen für stochastische Gradientenverfahren. Diese Theorie verbindet Ideen aus der Numerik von Differentialgleichungen mit Methoden der stochastischen Analysis und der Optimierung, um Fragestellungen aus dem maschinellen Lernen zu untersuchen.

Die Arbeit besteht aus zwei Hauptteilen. Im ersten Teil (Kapitel
3
-
5
) untersuchen wir modifizierte Gleichungen für stochastische Einschrittverfahren, darunter stochastischer Gradientenabstieg (SGD) ohne Zurücklegen bei unendlich vielen Daten, und SGD mit Zurücklegen. In Kapitel
3
untersuchen wir zeitinhomogene stochastische Differentialgleichungen (SDEs), die von einer Brownschen Bewegung getrieben werden und deren Drift- und Diffusionskoeffizienten eine Entwicklung in der Schrittweite besitzen, wobei der führende Term im Drift durch das Einschrittverfahren bestimmt ist (und höhere Terme frei wählbar sind).
Unter bestimmten Regularitätseigenschaften beweisen wir für diese SDEs eine schwache Approximationseigenschaft erster Ordnung und wir bestimmen ihre linearen Fehlerterme explizit. Darüber hinaus beweisen wir eine schwache Approximationseigenschaft zweiter Ordnung für eine spezifische Familie von SDEs. In Kapitel
4
instanziieren wir unsere Resultate für SGD. Wir arbeiten das Beispiel der linearen Regression vollständig aus und nutzen es in Kapitel
5
, um die linearen Fehlerterme des Gradientenflusses mit zwei häufig verwendeten stochastischen modifizierten Gleichungen erster Ordnung für SGD zu vergleichen.

Im zweiten Teil (Kapitel
6
und
7
) führen wir eine neuartige Diffusionsapproximation für SGD ohne Zurücklegen (SGDo) bei endlich vielen Daten ein und untersuchen diese. In Kapitel
6
motivieren und definieren wir den Begriff einer epochenweise zusammengesetzten Brownschen Bewegung. Wir argumentieren, dass Young-Differentialgleichungen (YDEs), die von solchen Prozessen getrieben werden, als kontinuierliche Modelle für SGDo dienen - und zwar für jede Mischungsstrategie, deren induzierte Permutationen im Grenzwert großer Stichproben gegen ein deterministisches Permuton konvergieren. Ferner beweisen wir fast sichere Konvergenz dieser YDEs im streng konvexen Fall. Zudem bestimmen wir eine asymptotische obere Schranke für die Konvergenzrate, die mindestens so scharf ist wie bisher bekannte Resultate für SGDo.

In Kapitel
7
untersuchen wir Grenzwerte von Familien skalierter zufälliger Irrfahrten, die bis auf eine (möglicherweise zufällige) Permutation dieselben Inkremente haben. Wir zeigen schwache Konvergenz unter der Annahme, dass die Folge dieser Permutationen gegen ein deterministisches (höherdimensionales) Permuton konvergiert. Diese Permuton bestimmt die Kovarianzfunktion des Gaussprozesses im Limes.
Umgekehrt zeigen wir, dass jeder Gaussprozess mit einer Kovarianzfunktion, die durch ein solches Permuton bestimmt wird, als schwacher Grenzwert skalierter zufälliger Irrfahrten mit gemeinsamen Inkrementen auftritt. Schließlich wenden wir unsere Konvergenztheorie an, um zu zeigen, dass epochenweise zusammengesetzte Brownsche Bewegungen als Grenzwerte skalierter zufälliger Irrfahrten mit endlich vielen verschiedenen Inkrementen entstehen.

Abstract

In this thesis, we extend the recently introduced theory of stochastic modified equations for stochastic gradient optimization algorithms. This theory combines ideas first developed in the field of numerics of differential equations with stochastic calculus and optimization in order to study problems from machine learning.

This thesis consists of two main parts. In the first part (Chapters
3
-
5
) we study modified equations for stochastic one-step methods, including one-pass SGD and SGD with replacement. In Chapter
3
we study time-inhomogeneous SDEs driven by Brownian motion whose drift and diffusion coefficients admit an expansion in the step size, with the leading-order term for the drift determined by the one-step method (and higher-order terms free).
For these SDEs we prove a first-order weak approximation property and we compute their linear error terms explicitly, under certain regularity conditions. Further, we prove a second-order weak approximation property for a specific family of SDEs. In Chapter
4
we instantiate our results for SGD. We work out the example of linear regression explicitly. We use this example to compare the linear error terms of gradient flow and two commonly used first-order stochastic modified equations for SGD.

In the second part (Chapters
6
and
7
) we introduce and study a novel diffusion approximation for SGD without replacement (SGDo) in the finite-data setting. In Chapter
6
we motivate and define the notion of an epoched Brownian motion. We argue that Young differential equations (YDEs) driven by such processes serve as continuous-time models for SGDo for any shuffling scheme whose induced permutations have a well-defined large-sample limit (i.e. they converge to a deterministic permuton). Further, we prove almost sure convergence for these YDEs in the strongly convex setting. Moreover, we compute an upper asymptotic bound on the convergence rate which is as sharp as, or better than previous results for SGDo.
In Chapter
7
we study scaling limits of families of random walks that share the same increments up to a (possibly random) permutation. We show weak convergence under the assumption that the sequence of permutations converges to a deterministic (higher-dimensional) permuton. This permuton determines the covariance function of the limiting Gaussian process.
Conversely, we show that every Gaussian process with a covariance function determined by a permuton in this way arises as a weak scaling limit of families of random walks with shared increments. Finally, we apply our weak convergence theory to show that epoched Brownian motions arise as scaling limits of random walks with finitely many distinct increments.

Chapter 1
Introduction

In the last decade or so, the importance of
machine learning
has increased dramatically.
Since the release of AlexNet in 2012, training
neural networks
in particular has enjoyed rapidly growing attention.
Most models nowadays referred to as AI are in fact variants of neural networks trained with some
stochastic (gradient) optimization
algorithm. While the field of AI seems to find success after success in applications, many aspects of the theoretical foundations of AI are poorly understood. Implementing a stochastic optimization algorithm is reasonably straightforward. Modern machine learning frameworks use automatic differentiation in backward mode (a.k.a. backpropagation) to compute gradients. Aside from computing gradients, the algorithms are usually rather simple.

However, it is not simple to see why they should compute anything useful on all but the simplest toy problems. At least for
overparameterized
models, that is models where the number of parameters greatly exceeds the number of data points available. In practice we can effectively learn overparameterized models, although the reason why remains mostly elusive.

Consider, for concreteness, a
d
-dimensional stochastic optimization algorithm
\chi
with dynamics given by

\displaystyle\chi_{n+1}^{h}=\chi_{n}^{h}-h\nabla R_{\bm{z}(n)}(\chi_{n}^{h}),\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},h\in(0,1),

(1)

where
(R_{z})_{z}
is a family of differentiable functions from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}
and
(\bm{z}(n))_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is an i.i.d. sequence of random variables in some measurable space
\mathcal{Z}
. We interpret
(\chi_{n}^{h})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
as the sequence of estimated parameters when applying a
stochastic gradient descent
(SGD) method for minimizing the function
\mathcal{R}(x)=\mathbb{E}[R_{\bm{z}(0)}(x)]
with constant
step size
or
learning rate

h
. The function
\mathcal{R}
itself can be interpreted as
empirical risk
(that is training error) or
population risk
.
We refer to
h
as the learning rate and
R_{\bm{z}(n)}
as the risk due to the
n
-th data point (or mini-batch: a small set of data points).
We also denote by
\Sigma(x)=\operatorname{Cov}[\nabla R_{\bm{z}(0)}(x)]
the covariance matrix of
\nabla R_{\bm{z}(0)}(x)
.

Investigating the behavior of the discrete SGD dynamics directly is generally very difficult.
To make the SGD process tractable with methods from mathematical analysis one frequently approximates the SGD dynamics with an ODE, usually referred to as gradient flow (GF), given by

\dot{X}_{t}^{0}=-\nabla\mathcal{R}(X_{t}^{0}),\quad X_{0}^{0}=\chi_{0},

(2)

One can show, under certain regularity conditions on
\mathcal{R}
, that Equation (
2
) is then a first-order approximation of SGD in the learning rate, that is for all
T>0
and sufficiently regular test functions
g
we have

|\mathbb{E}g(\chi_{\left\lfloor T/h\right\rfloor}^{h})-g(X_{T}^{0})|=\mathcal{O}(h),\quad h\downarrow 0.

Here, first-order refers to first power
h^{1}
of
h
on the right-hand side. In other words, the global truncation error of
\chi
converges to
0

linearly
as we let the step size
h
go to
0
.

Gradient flow dynamics are deterministic and hence ignore the randomness in SGD. Therefore, in recent years analytic approximations in terms of stochastic differential equations (SDEs) have become popular. They generally take the form

dX_{t}^{h}=-\nabla\mathcal{R}(X_{t}^{h})\,dt+hb(X_{t}^{h})\,dt+\sqrt{hD(X_{t}^{h})}\,dW_{t}.

(3)

Here,
W
is a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion and
D(x)
is a symmetric and positive semi-definite
d\times d
matrix.
In particular,
32
introduced an approximation of the form
(
3
)
with
b=0
and
D
constant in space such that
D\approx\Sigma(x)
in a
“region of interest”
(e.g. around a stationary point of
\mathcal{R}
).
27
studied approximations with a state-dependent diffusion coefficient, in particular
b=0
and
D=\Sigma
.
All approximations of SGD with
b=0
and sufficiently regular
D
are in fact (weak) first-order approximations, just like gradient flow, and generally not approximations of higher order
(see
27
, Appendix C, Remark 4)
.

We can gain further insight by exploring higher-order approximations for
deterministic

\chi
. In this case
\chi
is simply the (forward)
Euler method
applied to the gradient flow ODE. Indeed, the Euler method for given ODE is generally a first-order approximation of that ODE. In the literature on numerics of differential equations it is well-known that modifying the (drift) coefficient by setting
b=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}\nabla\mathcal{R}
(and
D=0
) in Equation (
3
) yields a
second-order approximation
of
\chi

(see
20
, Section IX.7.2)
. Here,
\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}
denotes the Hessian matrix of
\mathcal{R}
. The resulting family of ODEs is called a
second-order modified equation
of gradient flow. We also call it a second-order modified equation of the discrete process
\chi
.

27
;
28
showed that if we combine the drift modification
b=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}\nabla\mathcal{R}
with the state-dependent diffusion coefficient
D=\Sigma
, then Equation (
3
) becomes a genuine second-order approximation of
\chi
in the stochastic case for sufficiently regular
\Sigma
.

Accordingly, we call Equation (
3
) a first-order stochastic modified equation (SME) of
\chi
if
b=0
, and a second-order SME of
\chi
if
b=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}\nabla\mathcal{R}
and
D=\Sigma
.

Stochastic modified equations have been used as simplified models to study the dynamics of (variants of) SGD. In particular, SDE approximations have been used to optimize hyperparameters
(
32
;
33
;
27
;
31
;
44
;
36
, see)
, to analyze the long-term behavior of SGD processes
(
14
;
25
;
41
, see)
, to study the impact of normalization schemes
(
29
, see)
, to analyze the runtime until convergence
(
22
, see)
, to study the transition between stationary points
(
43
;
45
;
42
;
23
, see)
, to study the implicit bias and regularization properties of SGD
(
3
;
37
;
30
, see)
, and to study the effect of running SGD in parallel
(
4
;
10
, see)
.

In most of these articles, the authors prefer to work with first-order approximations, in particular
(b,D)=(0,\Sigma)
. However, upon closer inspection in some applications a constant diffusion coefficient is also frequently used, for example for computing optimal hyperparameters.
Given that order of approximation is the same as that of gradient flow, it is unclear whether first-order stochastic modified equation are more accurate. To compare first-order approximations including GF, we can aim to compute the linear error term, that is, the constant
C
for which

\mathbb{E}g(\chi_{\left\lfloor T/h\right\rfloor}^{h})-\mathbb{E}g(X_{T}^{0})=Ch+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),\quad h\downarrow 0.

27
note that, in principle, the constant
C
can be computed using the method by
40
for the expansion of the global error of numerical schemes for stochastic differential equations. The underlying hope is to show that SMEs have a smaller approximation error compared to GF.

The first part of this thesis focuses on precisely this question. In Chapter
3
we prove a general approximation result for stochastic modified equations driven by Brownian motion and compute their linear error terms using a method inspired by
40
. In contrast to previous works, we show regularity of the global truncation error in the initial condition. Additionally, we allow for time-inhomogeneous dynamics
\chi
and do not assume a gradient field structure. Instead we work with a random, time-dependent increment function
f
. The time-inhomogeneous dynamics allows us, for example, to incorporate step size controls
u:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
, that is step sizes that change over time. Another option we can consider is volatility controls, which leave
\bar{f}=\mathbb{E}f
unchanged but change the covariance matrix
\Sigma=\operatorname{Cov}f
over time.
We show that by choosing
(b,D)=(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\intercal}\bar{f}\bar{f}+\partial_{t}\bar{f},\Sigma)
, the equation

dX_{t}^{h}=\bar{f}_{t}(X_{t}^{h})\,dt+hb_{t}(X_{t}^{h})\,dt+\sqrt{hD_{t}(X_{t}^{h})}\,dW_{t}

(4)

becomes a second-order SME of SGD. Here,
\nabla^{\intercal}\bar{f}
denotes the Jacobian matrix of
\bar{f}
. Moreover, we show that the linear error term for the general Equation (
4
) quantifies how much our chosen coefficients
(b,D)
deviate from the coefficients
(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\intercal}\bar{f}\bar{f}+\partial_{t}\bar{f},\Sigma)
of the second-order SME.

Chapter
4
is a transition away from the numerics of differential equations towards stochastic gradient optimization. In particular, we consider mini-batch SGD and its approximation by stochastic modified equations. We discuss two main examples, namely SGD with replacement and one-pass SGD.
In SGD with replacement we draw our sequence of samples
(z(n))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
i.i.d. from a finite data set
\{Z_{1},\dots,Z_{N}\}
of size
N
. In contrast, in one-pass SGD we draw an i.i.d. sequence directly from the population, i.e. the true real-world distribution of our data. Focusing on the one-pass case, we work out the example of linear regression for observational data explicitly.

Using the linear regression example, in Chapter
5
we perform a detailed comparison of first-order (stochastic) modified equations for SGD. We demonstrate that in this case, stochastic approximations are indeed usually better than gradient flow due to presence of residual noise in the data. Moreover, we show that the ranking of the different approximations is tightly linked to quantities like the
batch size
and the
kurtosis of the data features
.

So far, we have always assumed that the SGD iterations (
1
) use an i.i.d. sequence of data points, which corresponds either to SGD with replacement or one-pass SGD. Neither of these algorithms is actually used in practice. The first one is considered inefficient, since it can take a long time until all data points are sampled. The second one is impractical. After all, the abundance of data is limited, and if that is the case, then it seems wasteful to train on every data point only once. Instead, in practice one always uses SGD
without replacement
(SGDo). That is, we use one-pass SGD until our finite data set is exhausted and then reuse the same data in subsequent training periods called
epochs
. It is then up to us to define a new ordering for the data points in later epochs. Typical choices include using the same order as in the first epoch (
single-shuffle
) or shuffling the data uniformly and independently across epochs (
random reshuffling
). But in principle the options are endless.

Because we are reusing data points, the Markov property is longer satisfied for SGDo.
Thus, no version of SGDo is covered by the current theory of stochastic modified equations. In the second part of this thesis, we take a step towards a theory of SMEs for SGDo. In Chapter
6
we introduce a novel approximation of SGDo using a family of Young (or rough) differential equations which are driven by a process we call an
epoched Brownian motion
(EBM). An EBM is a Brownian motion up to some time point
T>0
, and is then given by repeating the same Brownian path on the intervals
[jT,(j+1)T],j\in\mathbb{N}
, perhaps up to an
“infinitesimal permutation”
of the increments. We focus on approximations with state-independent diffusion coefficient, similar to
32
in the i.i.d. setting.
While establishing a rigorous approximation theory is beyond the scope of this thesis, we demonstrate the usefulness of EBM-driven approximations via an application.
Specifically, we show that given a step size control of the form
u_{t}=\frac{1}{(1+t)^{\beta}},\beta\in(0,1)
for the SGDo iterations and a strongly convex objective function
\mathcal{R}
, the solution to the approximating EBM-driven equation converges almost surely. Further, we compute an asymptotic upper bound on the convergence speed which at least is as sharp as previous results by
19
on the convergence of single shuffle SGDo. Moreover, in the case of general random permutations, our results suggest markedly better upper bounds than the best results known for random reshuffling.

Finally, in Chapter
7
we clarify our heuristic idea of considering the same Brownian path up to an
“infinitesimal permutation”
when approximating SGDo by an EBM-driven differential equation.
We establish weak approximation results for epoched Brownian motion by random walks. In particular, we consider families of random walks that share the same increments, up to a (random) permutation. We show the existence of Gaussian scaling limits of these random walks under natural assumptions on the sequence of permutations. The central assumption is the convergence of the sequence of random permutations to a deterministic
permuton
, i.e. a probability measure on the unit square with uniform marginals. Further, we show that the covariance function of the limiting process is given exactly by the distribution function of the limiting permuton (a so-called
copula
).
This covers the case of EBM with two epochs. More generally, we consider higher and infinite-dimensional permutons and copulas. This allows us to realize EBMs with arbitrarily many epochs as scaling limits.

Chapter 2
Preliminaries

Before starting, let us briefly introduce notation and some basic properties that will be used repeatedly throughout this thesis.
Additional notation is introduced along the way as needed.

Words and multi-indices

We write
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,\dots\}
and
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.
Given a set
A
denote by
A^{*}
the set of words over
A
, i.e.

A^{*}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}A^{n},\quad A^{0}=\{()\}.

We define the concatenation of two words
a\in A^{m}
and
b\in A^{n}
by

(a_{1},\dots,a_{m})(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})=(a_{1},\dots,a_{m},b_{1},\dots,b_{n})\in A^{m+n}.

The empty word
()
is an identity for the concatenation operation.
We also define the length of
a\in A^{m}
by
|a|=m
.

Given
d,e,l\in\mathbb{N}
write

d\times e=\{1,\dots,d\}\times\{1,\dots,e\},

and

d^{\times l}:=\underbrace{d\times\dots\times d}_{l\text{ times}}.

The words over the natural numbers may also be viewed as
ordered multi-indices
.
Given
d,e\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
we write
d\leq e
if
|d|=|e|
and
d_{i}\leq e_{i}
for all
i\leq|d|
.
We set

\Pi d:=d_{1}\times\dots\times d_{|d|}.

In particular, for a natural number
d\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\Pi(\underbrace{d,\dots,d}_{l\text{ times}})=d^{\times l}.

We also consider
unordered multi-indices
which we typically simply call
multi-indices
. An unordered multi-index is a function
\alpha:\{1,\dots,d\}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Its size is defined by

|\alpha|=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\alpha(k).

We also define

\alpha!=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\alpha(k)!,\quad x^{\alpha}=\prod_{k=1}^{d}x^{\alpha(k)}_{k},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Placeholders

We write
\cdot
to also denote a placeholder (if it cannot be confused with multiplication). For example, a norm
B\to[0,\infty),x\mapsto\|x\|
on a normed space
B
may be denoted by
\|\cdot\|
. For another example, consider a function
f:A\times B\to C
. Then
f(a,\cdot)
denotes the function

f(a,\cdot):B\to C,b\mapsto f(a,b),

for all
a\in A
.

Uniform bounds

Suppose we are given functions
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}:A\to\mathbb{R}
. Then we write

f_{1}\lesssim\dots\lesssim f_{n},

or

f_{1}(x)\lesssim\dots\lesssim f_{n}(x),\text{ uniformly over }x\in A,

if there exist constants
C_{1},\dots,C_{n}>0
such that

C_{1}f_{1}(x)\leq\dots\leq C_{n}f_{n}(x),\quad x\in A.

We also write
f_{1}\asymp f_{2}
if
f_{1}\lesssim f_{2}\lesssim f_{1}
.
We can also mix
\lesssim
and
\asymp
with
\leq
and
=
as needed.

If
(E,(\|\cdot\|_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}})
is a vector space equipped with a family of seminorms (or just one norm), and
(x_{j})_{j\in J}
is a family (of elements in some space
F
with
E\subseteq F
), then we write

x_{j}\in E,\text{ uniformly in }j\in J,

if
x_{j}\in E,j\in J
, and
\sup_{j\in J}\|x_{j}\|_{n}<\infty
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Graded Fréchet algebras

A Fréchet space is a complete metrizable Hausdorff topological vector space
F
such that its topology is induced by a
grading
. We call a countable family of seminorms
(\|\cdot\|_{p})_{p\in\mathbb{N}}
a grading if

•

\|\cdot\|_{p}\leq\|\cdot\|_{q}
for
p\leq q
, and

•

\bigcap_{p\in\mathbb{N}}\{x\in F:\|x\|_{p}=0\}=\{0\}
.

Thus,
x_{n}\to x
in
F
if and only if
\|x_{n}-x\|_{p}\to 0,p\in\mathbb{N}
.
Given
x\in F
we define

\|x\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{p\in\mathbb{N}}\|x\|_{p}\in[0,\infty].

A Fréchet algebra is a Fréchet space
F
together with a continuous operation
\cdot:F\times F\to F
making it also an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra.
A graded Fréchet space is a Fréchet space equipped with a grading.
A graded Fréchet algebra is Fréchet algebra with a grading such that for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a
q\in\mathbb{N}
with

\|xy\|_{p}\lesssim\|x\|_{q}\|y\|_{q},\text{ uniformly over }x,y\in F.

We call the grading, and the graded Fréchet algebra,
Hölder-type
if for all
m\in\mathbb{N},p,q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
with

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{q_{i}}=1,

we have

\|\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\|_{p}\lesssim\prod_{i=1}^{n}\|x_{i}\|_{pq_{i}},\text{ uniformly over }x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in F.

Here, we define
p\cdot\infty:=\infty
for
p\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\frac{1}{\infty}:=0
.
One simple example for a Hölder-type graded Fréchet algebra is
\mathbb{R}
with the grading
\|\cdot\|_{p}:=|\cdot|,p\in\mathbb{N}
.
Another less trivial example is
L^{\infty-}(\Omega,V)=\bigcap_{p\in\mathbb{N}}L^{p}(\Omega,V)
, the space of
V
-valued random variables with finite moments. Here,
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{\Omega},\mathbb{P})
is a probability space and
V
a closed linear subspace of some Banach space. It is a Fréchet algebra under the standard operations, and the grading is given by the family of
p
-norms.

Arrays

Let
F
be a Hölder-type graded Fréchet algebra.

Let
d,e\in\mathbb{N}^{*},k=|d|,l=|e|
.
An element of
F^{\Pi d}
is a function
\Pi d\to F
, that is a
k
-array
(or
“tensor”
) with dimensions
d_{1},d_{2},\dots,d_{k}
and values in
F
.
Given
A\in F^{\Pi d}
we define

\|A\|_{p}=\max_{i\leq\Pi d}\|A_{i}\|_{p},\quad p\in\mathbb{N}.

Here, the maximum runs over all ordered multi-indices
i
with
i\leq\Pi d
.
This a seminorm and
(F^{\Pi d},(\|\cdot\|_{p})_{p\in\mathbb{N}})
is a graded Fréchet space with addition and scalar multiplication defined component-wise.
Given
e\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
B\in F^{\Pi d}
and
C\in F^{\Pi e}
we define the
outer product

B\otimes C\in F^{\Pi(de)}
by

(B\otimes C)_{ij}=B_{i}C_{j}=B_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}C_{j_{1}\dots j_{l}}\quad i\leq d,j\leq e

and we set

B^{\otimes j}:=\underbrace{B\otimes\dots\otimes B}_{j\text{ times}},\quad j\in\mathbb{N}.

For example if
\Pi d=d
and
\Pi e=e
, then
B,C
are vectors and
B\otimes C=BC^{\intercal}\in F^{d\times e}
is a matrix.
The identity array
1_{\Pi d}\in F^{\Pi d}
is defined by

(1_{\Pi d})_{i}=\begin{cases}1,&i_{1}=\dots=i_{k},\\
0,&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Here,
1
is the multiplicative identity in the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
F
.
For
k=1
we have
1_{d}=(1,\dots,1)
. For
k=2
we get the
d\times d
-identity matrix.

Given
d,e,f\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
with
l=|d|
,
A\in F^{\Pi(de)}
and
B\in F^{\Pi(df)}
, we define
\langle A,B\rangle_{l}\in F^{\Pi(ef)}
, the
l
-th contraction
of
A
and
B
, by

(\langle A,B\rangle_{l})_{jj^{\prime}}=\sum_{i\leq d}A_{ij}B_{ij^{\prime}},\quad j\leq e,j^{\prime}\leq f.

(5)

If
e=()
or
f=()
then we simply write
\langle A,B\rangle=(\langle A,B\rangle)_{l}
. In particular, if
e=f=()
, then
\langle A,B\rangle\in F
. In the case
\Pi d=d
we recover the dot product of vectors and in the case
\Pi d=m\times n
we get the
Frobenius inner product
of matrices, that is

\langle A,B\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(A^{\intercal}B)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{l=1}^{n}A_{kl}B_{kl},\quad A,B\in F^{m\times n}.

If we are given
A\in\mathbb{R}^{\Pi(de)}
instead (or alternatively a real-valued array
B
), Equation (
5
) still makes sense: We can either view the product
A_{ij}B_{ij^{\prime}}
as a scalar-vector product, or we can view
\mathbb{R}^{\Pi(de)}
as a subset of
F^{\Pi(de)}
by identifying
A
with
A^{\prime}\in F^{\Pi(de)}
given by

A_{i}^{\prime}=A_{i}1,\quad i\leq\Pi(de).

Both choices yield the same result.
For
A\in\mathbb{R}^{\Pi d}
we also define
Frobenius norm

|A|=\sqrt{\langle A,A\rangle}.

Of course, it is equivalent to the norm
\max_{i\leq\Pi d}|A_{i}|
considered above, that is

|A|\asymp\max_{i\leq\Pi d}|A_{i}|,\text{ uniformly over }A\in\mathbb{R}^{\Pi d}

However, the Frobenius norm is often nicer to work with when it is available. For matrices
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
we further define the
spectral norm

\|A\|_{\operatorname{op}}:=\max_{x\neq 0}\frac{|Ax|}{|x|}=\max_{|x|=1}|Ax|.

We have

\|A\|_{\operatorname{op}}=\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(A^{\intercal}A)},

where
\lambda_{\max}(B)
is the largest eigenvalue of a square matrix
B
.

Note that the following properties hold true:

•

\langle A,B\otimes C\rangle=\langle\langle A,B\rangle,C\rangle\in F,\quad A\in F^{\Pi(de)},B\in F^{\Pi d},C\in F^{\Pi e}
.

•

\langle A,B\rangle\langle C,D\rangle=\langle A\otimes C,B\otimes D\rangle,\quad A,B,C,D\in F^{\Pi d}
.

•

\langle A,u\rangle=(u^{\intercal}A)^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n},u\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

•

\langle A,u\otimes v\rangle=u^{\intercal}Av,\quad A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n},u\in\mathbb{R}^{m},v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

|\langle A,B\rangle|\leq|A||B|,\quad A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{\Pi d}
.

•

|A\otimes B|\leq|A||B|,\quad A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{\Pi d}
.

Further, for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

\|\langle A,B\rangle\|_{p}\lesssim|A|\|B\|_{p},\text{ uniformly over }A\in\mathbb{R}^{\Pi d},B\in F^{\Pi d}
.

•

\|\langle A,B\rangle\|_{p}\lesssim\|A\|_{pq}\|B\|_{pr},\text{ uniformly over }A,B\in F^{\Pi d}
, for all
q,r\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
with
\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{r}=1
.

•

\|\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\|_{p}\lesssim\prod_{i=1}^{n}\|A_{i}\|_{pq_{i}},\text{ uniformly over }A_{i}\in F^{\Pi d_{i}},i=1,\dots,n
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}^{-1}=1
.

Smooth functions

Given
d\in\mathbb{N},e\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, an open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and a function
f:U\to\mathbb{R}^{\Pi e}
, we write
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,\mathbb{R}^{\Pi e})
if
f
is
l
-times continuously differentiable (component-wise). We also allow
U
to be a more general set. Given
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
we write
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,\mathbb{R}^{\Pi e})
if
f
is in
\mathcal{C}^{l}
on the interior of
U
, and
f
as well as its derivatives can be uniquely and continuously extended to the boundary.

An arbitrary subset of
U
can be very degenerate. The subsets of interest to us are finite Cartesian products of the form
U=I_{1}\times\dots\times I_{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-m}
, where
I_{1},\dots,I_{m}
are bounded intervals.
If a function
f:U\to\mathbb{R}^{\Pi e}
is in
\mathcal{C}^{l}
, then it can be extended to a function
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{\Pi e})
(see also Section
24
in the Appendix). At the boundary of
U
, the derivatives of
\tilde{f}
must then coincide with the continuous extensions of the derivative of
f
. In particular, at the boundary of
U
, the derivatives of
\tilde{f}
are uniquely determined. Thus, for example, for the purpose of applying Taylor’s theorem to
f
, boundary points of
U
can be treated as interior points.

Function spaces

For (families of) function spaces such as
\mathcal{C}^{l}
we write
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l},\mathcal{C}^{l}(U),\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,V)
depending on the level of detail required. If
f
is introduced as a function
U\to V
, then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U)
means
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,V)
. If the codomain of
f
is not directly specified, then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U)
means
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,\mathbb{R})
. If neither the domain nor the codomain of
f
is specified and we write
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}
, then this means that there exist sets
U,V
with
f:U\to V
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,V)
.
Similarly, we treat other families of function spaces.

Chapter 3
Modified equations

In this chapter, we consider a stochastic one-step method
\chi
and study continuous-time approximations of
\chi
called (stochastic) modified equations. We show first- and second-order weak approximation results, including regularity in the initial condition, and compute the linear error term for first-order approximations explicitly.
This chapter is inspired by and expands on
6
.

1
Introduction

In this chapter, our goal is to study the convergence and global error of a stochastic
one-step method
given by

\chi_{n+1}^{h}=\chi_{n}^{h}+hf_{nh}^{h}(\chi_{n}^{h}),\quad\chi_{0}=x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(6)

when approximating a differential equation.
Here, we consider a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{\Omega},\mathbb{P})
and a random
increment function

f:\Omega\times(0,1)\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},(\omega,h,t,x)\mapsto f_{t}^{h}(\omega)(x).

The value
h\in(0,1)
can be interpreted as
discretization parameter
or
step size
.
We assume that the finite family
(f_{nh}^{h})_{n\leq\left\lfloor T/h\right\rfloor}
is independent for all
h\in(0,1)
.

Typical choices for increment functions satisfy

f_{t}^{h}=u_{t}H_{\bm{z}(\left\lfloor t/h\right\rfloor)},\quad h\in(0,1),t\in[0,T].

Here,
u:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
is sufficiently regular function,
(\bm{z}(n))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
an i.i.d. sequence of random variables in some measurable space
\mathcal{Z}
, and
H:\mathcal{Z}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is such that the random function
H_{\bm{z}(0)}
is sufficiently regular. In particular, we assume
H_{\bm{z}(0)}(x)
has finite moments for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Assume that
\mathbb{E}f_{t}^{h}(x)
exists and that it does not depend on
h\in(0,1)
.
Define

\bar{f}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},(t,x)\mapsto\mathbb{E}f_{t}^{h}(x),\quad h\in(0,1),

and consider the ordinary differential equation

\partial_{t}X^{0}_{t}=\bar{f}_{t}(X^{0}_{t}),t\in[0,T],\quad X_{0}^{0}=x.

(7)

If
f
is non-random, we have
f=\bar{f}
and (
6
) is simply the
Euler method
with step size
h
applied to (
7
). Then, under certain conditions, the Euler method is known to be a
first-order approximation
of (
7
). That is, for all
T>0
there exists a constant
C>0
such that

|\chi_{T/h}^{h}-X_{T}^{0}|\leq Ch,\quad h\in(0,1),T/h\in\mathbb{N}.

If
f
is random, one may think of (
6
) as a noisy version of the Euler method that does not require us to calculate the full expectation
\bar{f}
at every iteration. Several reasons come to mind for considering (
6
):

•

The expectation is given as an average over a very large number of realizations and is therefore expensive to compute.

•

The expectation is taken with respect to some continuous distribution and no explicit formula is known or useful.

•

The underlying distribution is not known at all, so no expectation can be computed.

In Section
3
we will show that the stochastic one-step method or
noisy Euler method
(
6
) is still a
first-order weak approximation
of the ODE (
7
), in the sense that for all
T>0
and sufficiently regular functions
g
there exists a constant
C>0
such that

|\mathbb{E}g(\chi_{T/h}^{h})-g(X_{T}^{0})|\leq Ch,\quad h\in(0,1),T/h\in\mathbb{N}.

It is somewhat remarkable that this is true despite the fact that
f
may be a very crude estimator of
\bar{f}
.
Thus, the quality of the approximation is really captured in the constant
C
.
Consequently, we determine the linear error term

\frac{1}{h}(\mathbb{E}g(\chi_{T/h}^{h})-g(X_{T}^{0}))

precisely as well.
Finally, we also investigate the regularity of
C
as a function of the initial condition
\chi_{0}=x
.

The one-step method (
6
) exhibits random effects that the ODE (
7
) does not have. To better explain these effects it can be useful to approximate (
6
) using a family of stochastic differential equations, driven by a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
, of the form

dX_{t}^{h}=\bar{f}_{t}(X_{t}^{h})\,dt+b_{t}(X_{t}^{h})\,dt+\sqrt{hD(X_{t}^{h})}\,dW_{t}.

Here,
b
and
D
are sufficiently regular functions, and
D
takes values in the space of positive semi-definite symmetric
d\times d
-matrices.
We study these so called
stochastic modified equations
(SMEs) in Section
5.1
. We show that they are (at least) weak first-order approximations of (
6
), in the sense that for all
T>0
and sufficiently regular functions
g
there exists a constant
C>0
such that

|\mathbb{E}g(\chi_{T/h}^{h})-\mathbb{E}g(X_{T}^{h})|\leq Ch,\quad h\in(0,1),T/h\in\mathbb{N}.

(8)

and we compute their linear error terms as well. We also derive a second-order SME, meaning a particular family of SDEs where we can replace the
Ch
on the right-hand side of (
8
) with
\tilde{C}h^{2}
for some constant
\tilde{C}>0
.

Even though we manage to compute the linear error terms for both stochastic and deterministic differential equations, the calculation does not immediately make clear whether first-order stochastic approximations are indeed better (according to their linear error term). We perform a thorough comparison in a special setting in Chapter
5
.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Growth conditions

In this section we discuss various regularity conditions that help us streamline the arguments in Section
3
.
We state some properties here without proof. We go in much further detail in Section
4
as preparation for discussing the more general theory of stochastic modified equations in Section
5.1
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N},e\in\mathbb{N}^{*},V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m},W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{\Pi e}
,
g:V\to W
be a continuous function, and
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We say
g

has (at most) polynomial growth of order
\kappa
if

|g(x)|\lesssim 1+|x|^{\kappa},\text{ uniformly over }x\in V.

In this case we write
g\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
.
More generally, let
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
g\in\mathcal{C}^{l}
.
Then we define

\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}}=\max_{|\alpha|\leq l}\sup_{x\in V}\frac{|\partial^{\alpha}g(x)|}{1+|x|^{\kappa}}.

Here, the maximum ranges over all (unordered) multi-indices
\alpha
up to size
l
. We write
g\in\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}
if
g\in\mathcal{C}^{l}
with
\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}}<\infty
. In particular,
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}=\mathcal{C}^{0}_{\kappa}
.

Polynomial growth conditions are stable under various elementary operations, as the next lemma shows.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
l,\kappa,\lambda\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then the following properties hold true:

(i)

If
\lambda\leq\kappa
, then
\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\lambda}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}
with
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}}\lesssim\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\lambda}}
.

(ii)

\|cf+g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}}\leq|c|\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}}+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}},f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}
.

(iii)

\|\langle f,g\rangle\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa+\lambda}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}}\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\lambda}}
, uniformly over
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}
and
g\in\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\lambda}
.

(iv)

\|f\otimes g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa+\lambda}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}}\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\lambda}}
, uniformly over
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}
and
g\in\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\lambda}
.

(v)

\|g\circ f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa(\lambda+l)}}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\lambda}}(1\vee\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}}^{\lambda+l})
, uniformly over
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}
and
g\in\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\lambda}
.

Proof.

This is a special case of Lemma
9
below.
∎

We assume in the above statements that the expression are all well-defined. In particular, for (i) we fix the same domain and codomain for both function spaces. For the remaining properties, we assume that the domains and codomains are fixed such that
+,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle,\otimes
and
\circ
are well-defined.

Next, we discuss continuity and polynomial growth conditions for functions which take random variables as values. Let
d\in\mathbb{N},U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a linear subspace and
X:U\to L^{\infty-}(\Omega,V)
be a function taking values in the space of
V
-valued random variables with finite moments.
Then we write
X\in\mathcal{C}(U,L^{\infty-}(\Omega,V))
if given
x_{n}\to x
in
U
we have
X(x_{n})\to X(x)
in
L^{p}
, for all
p\geq 1
.
Further, we write
X\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}(U,L^{\infty-}(\Omega,V))
if:

•

X\in\mathcal{C}(U,L^{\infty-}(\Omega,V))
,

•

\|X(x)\|_{p}\lesssim 1+|x|^{\kappa}
, uniformly over
x\in U
, for all
p\geq 1
.

Note that since the sequence
\|\cdot\|_{p}
is non-decreasing it suffices to consider the condition
\|X(x)\|_{p}\lesssim 1+|x|^{\kappa}
for
p\in\mathbb{N}
.
We equip
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}(U,L^{\infty-}(\Omega,V))
(which is indeed a vector space) with the family of norms given by

\|X\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa},p}:=\|x\mapsto\|X(x)\|_{p}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}},\quad p\geq 1.

Thus, given a family
(X^{i})_{i\in I}
, writing

X^{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}(U,L^{\infty-}(\Omega,V)),\text{ uniformly in }i\in I

means
X^{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}(U,L^{\infty-}(\Omega,V)),i\in I
and
\sup_{i\in I}\|X^{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa},p}<\infty
, for all
p\geq 1
.

In the following we call
X:\Omega\times U\to V
a
random field
if
X
is measurable; i.e. measurable with respect to the product
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{\Omega}\otimes\mathcal{B}(U)
and the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}(V)
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
X:\Omega\times U\to V
be a random field such that
X:U\to L^{\infty-}(\Omega,V)\in\mathcal{C}
, and such that there exists a random variable
Z\in L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})
with

|X(x)|\leq Z(1+|x|^{\kappa}),\text{ a.s.},\quad x\in U.

Then
X\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}(U,L^{\infty-}(\Omega,V))
and

\|X\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa},p}\leq\|Z\|_{p},\quad p\geq 1.

Proof.

From the assumption we conclude

\|X(x)\|_{p}\leq\|Z\|_{p}(1+|x|^{\kappa}),\quad x\in U,

and continuity is already satisfied.
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

Consider a function
X:U\to L^{\infty-}(\Omega,V)
. The following are equivalent:

(a)

X\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}(U,L^{\infty-}(\Omega,V))
.

(b)

g(X)\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa\lambda}(U,L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{\Pi e}))
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N},e\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
g\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}(V,\mathbb{R}^{\Pi e})
.

(c)

\mathbb{E}g(X)\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa\lambda}(U,\mathbb{R}^{\Pi e})
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N},e\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
g\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}(V,\mathbb{R}^{\Pi e})
.

In this case,

\|\mathbb{E}g(X)\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa\lambda}}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}},\text{ uniformly over }g\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}

Proof.

Assuming (a) and
g\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}
we have

|g(X(x))|\leq\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}}(1+|X(x)|^{\lambda}),\text{ uniformly over }x\in U,

and so

\|g(X(x))\|_{p}\leq\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}}(1+\|X(x)\|_{p\lambda}^{\lambda})\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}}(1+|x|^{\kappa\lambda}),\text{ uniformly over }x\in U.

(9)

Further, if
x_{n}\to x
in
U
, then
X(x_{n})\to X(x)
in
L^{p}
(and in particular in probability), and so
g(X(x_{n}))\to g(X(x))
in probability. Using Inequality (
9
) and Vitali’s convergence theorem, we conclude
g(X(x_{n}))\to g(X(x))
in
L^{p}
, for all
p\geq 1
. This proves (b).

Assuming (b) and
g\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}(V)
, we have

|\mathbb{E}g(X)|\leq\|g(X)\|_{1}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}}(1+|x|^{\kappa\lambda}),\text{ uniformly over }x\in U.

Further,
x_{n}\to x
in
U
implies
g(X(x_{n}))\to g(X(x))
in
L^{1}
, and so

\mathbb{E}g(X(x_{n}))\to\mathbb{E}g(X(x)).

Thus, (c) follows, as well as the estimate

\|\mathbb{E}g(X)\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa\lambda}}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}},\text{ uniformly over }g\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}.

Assume (c). For
p\in\mathbb{N}
, define
g:V\to\mathbb{R},x\mapsto|x|^{p}\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
. Then

\mathbb{E}[|X(x)|^{p}]=\mathbb{E}g(X)\lesssim 1+|x|^{p\kappa},\text{ uniformly over }x\in U,

Now, suppose
x_{n}\to x
in
U
. We have

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}[|X(x_{n})|^{p}]=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}g(X(x_{n}))\lesssim 1+\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}|x_{n}|^{p\kappa}<\infty,

for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular,
(\mathbb{P}_{X(x_{n})})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a tight family of measures. Since
\mathbb{E}g(X(x_{n}))\to\mathbb{E}g(X(x))
for all
g\in\mathcal{C}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
, we have
X(x_{n})\to X(x)
in distribution by the Portmanteau theorem, and, by Vitali’s convergence theorem, even in
L^{p}
. Thus, (c) implies (a).
∎

Properties similar to the ones in Lemma
2.1
also hold for functions in
U\to L^{\infty-}(\Omega,V)
. For now, we just mention the following:

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\kappa,\lambda\in\mathbb{N}_{0},X\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}(U,L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}))
and
Y\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}(U,L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{\Pi d}))
.
Then
XY\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+\lambda}(U,L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{\Pi d}))
, and

\|XY\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+\lambda},p}\lesssim\|X\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa},2p}\|Y\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\lambda},2p},

uniformly over
X\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}(U,L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}))
and
Y\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}(U,L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{\Pi d}))
, for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

This is a special case of Lemma
9
(c) below.
∎

Given
g:V\to W
we say
g
is
Lipschitz (continuous)
if

|g(x)-g(y)|\lesssim|x-y|,\text{ uniformly over }x,y\in V.

In this case we write
g\in\operatorname{Lip}
.
More generally, let
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
g\in\mathcal{C}^{l}
. We define

\|g\|_{\operatorname{Lip}^{l+1}}=\max_{|\alpha|\leq l}\sup_{x\neq y\in V}\frac{|\partial^{\alpha}g(x)-\partial^{\alpha}g(y)|}{|x-y|}

Here, the maximum is taken over all multi-indices
\alpha
with
|\alpha|\in\{0,\dots,l\}
, and
\partial^{\alpha}g=g
for
|\alpha|=0
.
Then we write
g\in\operatorname{Lip}^{l+1}
if
\partial^{\alpha}g\in\operatorname{Lip}
for all multi-indices
\alpha
with
|\alpha|\leq l
. We also write
\|g\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}:=\|g\|_{\operatorname{Lip}^{1}}
.

2.2
General assumptions on the one-step method

Consider once more a random
increment function

f:\Omega\times(0,1)\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},(\omega,h,t,x)\mapsto f_{t}^{h}(\omega)(x),

such that the finite family
(f_{nh}^{h})_{n\leq\left\lfloor T/h\right\rfloor}
is independent for all
h\in(0,1)
. Define the family of random fields
\chi
by

\chi_{n+1}^{h}(x)=\chi_{n}^{h}(x)+hf_{nh}^{h}(\chi_{n}^{h}(x)),\quad\chi_{0}^{h}(x)=\chi_{0}(x)=x,

for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},h\in(0,1)
and
n\in\{0,\dots,\left\lfloor T/h\right\rfloor\}
. We write
\Delta\chi_{n}^{h}:=\chi_{n+1}^{h}-\chi_{n}^{h}
.

Throughout this chapter we assume the following regularity conditions on
f
.

Assumption 1
.

There exists a measurable space
\mathcal{Z}
, a measurable map
F:(0,1)\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a measurable map
L:\mathcal{Z}\to[0,\infty)
, and an i.i.d. sequence
(\bm{z}(n))_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of
\mathcal{Z}
-valued random variables with
L(\bm{z}(0))\in L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathcal{Z})
such that

f_{t}^{h}(x)=F(h,t,x,\bm{z}(\left\lfloor t/h\right\rfloor)),

and

|F(h,t,x,z)|\leq L(z)(1+|x|),

for all
h\in(0,1),t\in[0,T],x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.
Further, we have
f_{t}^{h}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d},L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{d}))
uniformly in
h\in(0,1)
and
t\in[0,T]
.
Moreover, there exists a function
\bar{f}\in\operatorname{Lip}^{5+1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\bar{f}=\mathbb{E}f^{h}
for all
h\in(0,1)
,
and a function
\Sigma\in\mathcal{C}^{5}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
with
\Sigma=\mathbb{E}[(f^{h}-\bar{f})^{\otimes 2}]
for all
h\in(0,1)
.

Note that, using Assumption (A
1
), for
L_{n}:=L(\bm{z}(n))
we have

|f_{nh}^{h}(x)|\leq|F(h,t,x,\bm{z}_{n})|\leq L_{n}(1+|x|),

and
L_{n}
is independent of
\chi_{n}^{h}
for all
h\in(0,1)
.
Thus, Lemma
2.2
implies
f_{t}^{h}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, uniformly in
h\in(0,1)
and
t\in[0,T]
. Also both the expectation and covariance matrix of
f
do not depend on
h\in(0,1)
.

The following lemma and its proof are inspired by
28
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Assuming (A
1
), the following hold true:

(i)

We have

\max_{n\leq\left\lfloor T/h\right\rfloor}|\chi^{h}_{n}|\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})),\text{ uniformly in }h\in(0,1).

(ii)

We have

h^{-1}\max_{n\leq\left\lfloor T/h\right\rfloor}|\Delta\chi_{n}^{h}|\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})),\text{ uniformly in }h\in(0,1).

Proof 2.2
.

The continuity conditions are straightforward to prove. We focus on the linear growth conditions.

(i)

Define
M_{n}=\max_{m\leq n}|\chi_{m}^{h}|
. Then

|\chi_{n+1}^{h}|\leq|\chi_{n}^{h}|+h|f_{nh}^{h}(\chi_{n}^{h})|\leq M_{n}+hL_{n}(1+|\chi_{n}^{h}|)\leq M_{n}+hL_{n}(1+M_{n}),

and so

M_{n+1}=M_{n}\vee|\chi_{n+1}^{h}|\leq M_{n}\vee(M_{n}+hL_{n}(1+M_{n}))\leq M_{n}+hL_{n}(1+M_{n}),

for all
n\in\{0,\dots,T/h\}
.
Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we have

\displaystyle M_{n+1}^{p}\leq

\displaystyle(M_{n}+hL_{n}(1+M_{n}))^{p}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle M_{n}^{p}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\binom{p}{k}M_{n}^{p-k}h^{k}L_{n}^{k}(1+M_{n})^{k}.

for all
n\in\{0,\dots,\left\lfloor T/h\right\rfloor\}
.
Then, for
k\in\{1,\dots p\}
,
h\in(0,1)
and
n\in\{0,\dots,\left\lfloor T/h\right\rfloor\}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{p-k}L_{n}^{k}(1+M_{n})^{k}]=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{p-k}(1+M_{n})^{k}]\mathbb{E}[L_{0}^{k}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{k}\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{p-k}(1+M_{n}^{k})]\mathbb{E}[L_{n}^{k}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{k}\mathbb{E}[L_{0}^{k}]\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{p-k}+M_{n}^{p}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{k}(1+\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{p}]),

where
c_{k}=2^{k+1}\mathbb{E}[L_{0}^{k}]
. Here, we used that
L_{n}
and
\chi_{n}^{h}
are independent, and the inequality
y^{q}+y^{p}\leq 2(1+y^{p})
for
0<q\leq p
and
y\geq 0
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[M_{n+1}^{p}]\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{p}]+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\binom{p}{k}h^{k}\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{p-k}L_{n}^{k}(1+M_{n})^{k}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{p}]+Ch(1+\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{p}])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+Ch)\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{p}]+Ch,

where
C=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\binom{p}{k}c_{k}
.
By induction over
n
, we get

\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{p}]\leq(1+Ch)^{n}|x|^{p}+Ch\left(\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(1+Ch)^{k}\right),

for all
h\in(0,1)
and
n\in\{0,\dots,\left\lfloor T/h\right\rfloor\}
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[M_{\left\lfloor T/h\right\rfloor}^{p}]\leq

\displaystyle(1+Ch)^{\frac{T}{h}}|x|^{p}+Ch\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\left\lfloor\frac{T}{h}\right\rfloor-1}(1+Ch)^{k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1+Ch)^{\frac{T}{h}}|x|^{p}+Ch\frac{T}{h}(1+Ch)^{\frac{T}{h}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(CT+|x|^{p})(1+Ch)^{\frac{T}{h}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(CT+|x|^{p})e^{\log(1+Ch)\frac{T}{h}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(CT+|x|^{p})e^{CT}

for all
h\in(0,1)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, since
\log(1+y)\leq y
for all
y>-1
. Taking the
p
-th root, we get

\|\max_{n\leq\left\lfloor T/h\right\rfloor}|\chi^{h}_{n}|\|_{p}=\|M_{\left\lfloor T/h\right\rfloor}\|_{p}\lesssim\left(1+|x|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\lesssim 1+|x|,\text{ uniformly over }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},h\in(0,1).

For arbitrary
p\geq 1
we have
{\|Y^{*}\|_{p}}\leq{\|Y^{*}\|_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}}
and thus the result is proven.

(ii)

We have

h^{-1}|\Delta\chi_{n}^{h}|=|f_{nh}^{h}(\chi_{n}^{h})|\leq|L_{n}|(1+|\chi_{n}^{h}|),

for all
h\in(0,1)
and
n\in\{0,\dots,T/h\}
.
Thus,

\|h^{-1}\max_{n\leq T/h}|\Delta\chi_{n}^{h}|\|_{p}\leq\|L_{0}\|_{2p}\left(1+\|\max_{n\leq T/h}|\chi_{n}^{h}|\|_{2p}\right),

for all
h\in(0,1),x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
p\geq 1
.
Hence, the result follows from (i).

3
Convergence of the noisy Euler method

In this section we show that the stochastic one-step method (
6
) is a first-order weak approximation of the ODE (
7
). Further, we calculate the linear error term explicitly (see Theorem
1
below), and we show regularity of the error term in the initial condition. In some sense, this is a warm-up for the more general theory of stochastic modified equations in Section
5.1
.
Given a time horizon
T>0
write

T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1):=\{h\in(0,1):T/h\in\mathbb{N}\}

(10)

for the set of acceptable step sizes.
Let

X^{0}:[0,T]\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},(t,s,x)\mapsto X_{t}^{0,s}

be such that
X^{0,s}(x)
is the unique solution to the ODE

\partial_{t}X_{t}^{0,s}(x)=\bar{f}_{t}(X_{t}^{0,s}(x)),\quad t\in[s,T],X^{0}_{s}(x)=x,

for all
(s,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In the case
s>t
we can simply set
X^{0,s}_{t}(x)=x
.
Assumption (A
1
) implies that

X^{0,\cdot}_{t}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},(s,x)\mapsto X^{0,s}_{t}(x)\in\operatorname{Lip}^{5+1}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{5}_{1},

uniformly in
t\in[0,T]
(for example by Corollary
16
below). In detail, this means that
X^{0,s}_{t}(x)
is five-times continuously differentiable in
(s,x)
(with continuous extension to the boundary), and

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|X^{0,\cdot}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}_{1}}=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\max_{k\leq 5}\max_{|\alpha|\leq 5-k}\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\partial_{s}^{k}\partial^{\alpha}X^{0,s}_{t}(x)|}{1+|(s,x)|}<\infty.

At the expense of re-scaling the constant, we can write
|x|
instead of
|(s,x)|
, since
s\leq T
. We write
X^{0}_{t}:=X^{0,0}_{t},t\in[0,T]
in the following.
Given
g\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we define

v^{g}:[0,T]\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R},(r,t,x)\mapsto v^{g,r}_{t}(x):=g(X_{r}^{0,t}(x)).

We also write
v^{g}=v^{g,T}
and
v=v^{g}
if the choice of
g
is clear from the context.
We denote by
\nabla^{\intercal}w
and
\nabla^{2}w
the Jacobi and the Hessian matrix of a function
w
(with respect to the spatial variable), respectively.

Theorem 3.0
.

Assume (A
1
). Then for all
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
g\in\mathcal{C}^{5}_{\kappa}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists a function
\rho^{g}:T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1)\to\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+13}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),h\mapsto\rho^{g,h}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}g(\chi_{T/h}^{h})-g(X_{T}^{0})=\frac{1}{2}h\int_{0}^{T}(\langle\nabla^{2}v^{g},\Sigma\rangle-\langle\nabla v^{g},\nabla^{\intercal}\bar{f}\bar{f}+\partial_{t}\bar{f}\rangle)_{t}(X_{t}^{0})\,dt+h^{2}\rho^{g,h},

(11)

and

\|\rho^{g,h}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+13}}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}_{\kappa}},

uniformly over
g\in\mathcal{C}^{5}_{\kappa}
and
h\in T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1)
.

The degree
\kappa+13
is a bit surprising. Note that if
g\in\mathcal{C}^{5}_{\kappa}
and
X\in\mathcal{C}^{5}_{1}
, then we already have
\varphi:=g(X)\in\mathcal{C}^{5}_{\kappa+5}
(see Lemma
2.1
(v)). In the proof we iterate compositions and pair this with estimates for Taylor remainders. A careful bookkeeping of these steps yields the value
\kappa+13
. We do not claim that this number is optimal.

For deterministic
f
we have
\Sigma=0
. Thus, the second summand on the right-hand side of Equation (
11
) is the linear error term when approximating an ODE using the Euler method. The first summand accounts for the stochasticity of the noisy Euler method (
6
).

Consider the linear operator

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{C}^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{C}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})

given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}w:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla^{2}w,\bar{f}^{\otimes 2}+\Sigma\rangle+\langle\partial_{t}\nabla w,\bar{f}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}^{2}w,\quad w\in\mathcal{C}^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(12)

and we write
\mathcal{F}_{t}w(x):=(\mathcal{F}w)(t,x)
for all
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We show that the linear error term is determined by
\mathcal{F}v(X^{0})
. We can rewrite
\mathcal{F}v
using the following lemma to get the desired expression in Equation
11
.

Lemma 2
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then

\mathcal{F}v=\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla^{2}v,\Sigma\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla v,\nabla^{\intercal}\bar{f}\bar{f}+\partial_{t}\bar{f}\rangle.

Proof 3.1
.

Recall the transport equation (
13
)

\partial_{t}v+\langle\nabla v,\bar{f}\rangle=0.

Using the formula

\nabla\langle f,g\rangle=\langle\nabla^{\intercal}f,g\rangle+\langle f,\nabla^{\intercal}g\rangle,\quad f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})

we calculate

\partial_{t}\nabla v=-\nabla\langle\nabla v,\bar{f}\rangle=-\langle\nabla^{2}v,\bar{f}\rangle-\langle\nabla v,\nabla^{\intercal}\bar{f}\rangle.

Hence,

-\langle\partial_{t}\nabla v,\bar{f}\rangle=\langle\langle\nabla^{2}v,\bar{f}\rangle,\bar{f}\rangle+\langle\langle\nabla v,\nabla^{\intercal}\bar{f}\rangle,\bar{f}\rangle=\langle\nabla^{2}v,\bar{f}^{\otimes 2}\rangle+\langle\nabla v,\nabla^{\intercal}\bar{f}\bar{f}\rangle.

Therefore, again using Equation (
13
),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}v=-\partial_{t}\langle\nabla v,\bar{f}\rangle=

\displaystyle-\langle\nabla v,\partial_{t}\bar{f}\rangle-\langle\partial_{t}\nabla v,\bar{f}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\langle\nabla v,\partial_{t}\bar{f}\rangle+\langle\nabla^{2}v,\bar{f}^{\otimes 2}\rangle+\langle\nabla v,\nabla^{\intercal}\bar{f}\bar{f}\rangle,

and so

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}v-\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla^{2}v,\Sigma\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla^{2}v,\bar{f}^{\otimes 2}\rangle+\langle\partial_{t}\nabla v,\bar{f}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}^{2}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla^{2}v,\bar{f}^{\otimes 2}\rangle-(\langle\nabla^{2}v,\bar{f}^{\otimes 2}\rangle+\langle\nabla v,\nabla^{\intercal}\bar{f}\bar{f}\rangle)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}(-\langle\nabla v,\partial_{t}\bar{f}\rangle+\langle\nabla^{2}v,\bar{f}^{\otimes 2}\rangle+\langle\nabla v,\nabla^{\intercal}\bar{f}\bar{f}\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla v,\nabla^{\intercal}\bar{f}\bar{f}+\partial_{t}\bar{f}\rangle.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\kappa,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
l\leq 5
. Then

\|\mathcal{F}w\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa+2}}\lesssim\|w\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l+2}_{\kappa}},

uniformly over
w\in\mathcal{C}^{l+2}_{\kappa}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof 3.3
.

The linearity of
\mathcal{F}
is straightforward to show. By Lemma
2.1
we have
\langle\nabla^{2}w,{\bar{f}}^{\otimes 2}+\Sigma\rangle\in\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa+2}
with

\|\langle\nabla^{2}w,{\bar{f}}^{\otimes 2}+\Sigma\rangle\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa+2}}\lesssim\|\nabla^{2}w\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}}(\|\bar{f}^{\otimes 2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}_{2}}+\|\Sigma\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}_{2}})\lesssim\|w\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l+2}_{\kappa}}(\|\bar{f}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}_{1}}^{2}+\|\Sigma\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}_{2}}),

uniformly in
w\in\mathcal{C}^{l+2}_{\kappa}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
The other summands are treated similarly.

To prove Theorem
1
we use the fact that, given
g\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
, the function
v=v^{g,r}=g(X_{r}^{\cdot})
satisfies following PDE, called
transport equation
or
Kolmogorov backward equation
:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v_{t}+\langle\bar{f},\nabla v_{t}\rangle=0,&t\in[0,r],\\
v_{r}=g,\end{cases}

(13)

for all
r\in[0,T]
.

Lemma 3
.

Given
g\in\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists a function
\xi^{g}:(T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1))\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+6}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),(h,n)\mapsto\xi^{g,h}_{n}
such that

\mathbb{E}g(\chi_{n}^{h})-g(X_{nh}^{0})=h^{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{F}_{kh}[g(X^{0,\cdot}_{nh})](\chi_{k}^{h})]+h^{2}\xi^{g,h}_{n},\quad h\in T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1),

and

\|\xi^{g,h}_{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+6}}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa}},

uniformly over
g\in\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa}
,
h\in T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1)
, and
n\in\{0,\dots,T/h\}
.

Proof 3.4
.

Note that
v^{g,r}\in\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa+3}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
uniformly in
r\in[0,T]
, with

\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\|v^{g,r}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa+3}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\text{ uniformly over }g\in\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa},

by Lemma
2.1
(v) and since
X^{0}_{r}\in\mathcal{C}^{5}_{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, uniformly in
r\in[0,T]
.
Writing
v:=v^{g,r}
, Taylor’s theorem implies

\displaystyle v_{t+h}(x+\delta)-v_{t}(x)=

\displaystyle h\partial_{t}v_{t}(x)+\langle\delta,\nabla v_{t}(x)\rangle+\frac{h^{2}}{2}\partial_{t}^{2}v_{t}(x)

\displaystyle+h\langle\delta,\partial_{t}\nabla v_{t}(x)\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\delta^{\otimes 2},\nabla^{2}v_{t}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle+\zeta,

where

\zeta=\sum_{l=0}^{3}\sum_{|\beta|=3-l}\frac{1}{\beta!l!}\partial_{t}^{l}\partial_{\beta}v_{t+\theta h}(x+\theta\delta)h^{l}\delta^{\beta},

(14)

for some
\theta\in(0,1)
depending on
t\in[0,T],h\in(0,1),x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\delta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. By choosing
r=nh
,
t=kh
,
x=\chi_{k}^{h}
and
\delta=\Delta\chi_{k}^{h}=hf_{kh}^{h}(\chi_{k}^{h})
we get

\displaystyle v_{(k+1)h}(\chi_{k+1}^{h})-v_{kh}(\chi_{k}^{h})=

\displaystyle h(\partial_{t}v_{kh}+\langle f_{kh}^{h},\nabla v_{kh}\rangle)(\chi_{k}^{h})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}h^{2}(\partial_{t}^{2}v_{kh}+2\langle f_{kh}^{h},\partial_{t}\nabla v_{kh}\rangle+\langle(f^{h}_{kh})^{\otimes 2},\nabla^{2}v_{kh}\rangle)(\chi_{k}^{h})

\displaystyle+\zeta_{k,n}^{h},

with remainder term now denoted by
\zeta_{k,n}^{h}
.
Note that for a random field
Y:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and a random variable
Z:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such that
Y(x)
is independent of
Z
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\mathbb{E}[Y(Z)]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[Y(Z)|Z]]=\mathbb{E}[(\mathbb{E}Y)(Z)].

Since
f_{kh}^{h}(x)
is independent of
\chi_{k}^{h}
and
\mathbb{E}[(f^{h}_{kh})^{\otimes 2}]=\Sigma_{kh}+\bar{f}_{kh}^{\otimes 2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[v_{(k+1)h}(\chi_{k+1}^{h})-v_{kh}(\chi_{k}^{h})]=

\displaystyle h\mathbb{E}[(\partial_{t}v_{kh}+\langle\bar{f}_{kh},\nabla v_{kh}\rangle)(\chi_{k}^{h})]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}h^{2}\mathbb{E}[(\partial_{t}^{2}v_{kh}+2\langle\bar{f}_{kh},\partial_{t}\nabla v_{kh}\rangle+\langle\Sigma_{kh}+\bar{f}_{kh}^{\otimes 2},\nabla^{2}v_{kh}\rangle)(\chi_{k}^{h})]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\zeta_{k,n}^{h}.

We have
\partial_{t}v_{kh}+\langle\bar{f}_{kh},\nabla v_{kh}\rangle=0
by Equation (
13
), and so

\mathbb{E}[v_{(k+1)h}(\chi_{k+1}^{h})-v_{kh}(\chi_{k}^{h})]=h^{2}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{F}_{kh}[g(X^{0,\cdot}_{nh})](\chi_{k}^{h})]+\mathbb{E}\zeta_{k,n}^{h}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}g(\chi_{n}^{h})-g(X_{nh}^{0})=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}v_{nh}(\chi_{n}^{h})-\mathbb{E}v_{0}(\chi_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\mathbb{E}v_{(k+1)h}(\chi_{k+1}^{h})-\mathbb{E}v_{kh}(\chi_{k}^{h})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h^{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{F}_{kh}[g(X^{0,\cdot}_{nh})](\chi_{k}^{h})]+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\mathbb{E}\zeta_{k,n}^{h},

for all
h\in T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1)
.
Recall Equation (
14
). To estimate the sum of the remainder terms, note that by Lemma
2.1
(ii) and Lemma
2.3
(b),

h^{-3}|h^{l}(\Delta\chi_{k}^{h})^{\beta}|\leq|h^{-(3-l)}(\Delta\chi_{k}^{h})^{\otimes(3-l)}|\in\mathcal{C}_{3-l}(\mathbb{R}^{d},L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}))\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d},L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})),

uniformly in
h\in[0,1]
and
k\in\{0,\dots,n\}
, for
l\in\{0,1,2,3\}
and
|\beta|=3-l
. Further,

\displaystyle|\partial_{t}^{l}\partial_{\beta}v_{kh+\theta h}^{nh}(\chi_{k}^{h}+\theta\Delta\chi_{k}^{h})|\leq

\displaystyle\|v^{nh}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa+3}}(1+|(k+1)h|^{\kappa}+|\chi_{k}^{h}+\theta\Delta\chi_{k}^{h}|^{\kappa+3})

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa}}(1+T^{\kappa}+|\chi_{k}^{h}+\theta\Delta\chi_{k}^{h}|^{\kappa+3})\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+3}(\mathbb{R}^{d},L^{\infty-}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})),

uniformly in
h\in(0,1),n\in\{0,\dots,T/h\}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,n\}
.
Thus,
\mathbb{E}\zeta^{h}_{k,n}\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+6}
by Lemma
2.4
, with

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}\zeta^{h}_{k,n}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+6}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim

\displaystyle h^{3}\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\max_{l\in\{0,1,2,3\}}\|h^{-(3-l)}(\Delta\chi_{k}^{h})^{\otimes{3-l}}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{3},2}\||\Delta\chi_{k}^{h}+\theta\Delta\chi_{k}^{h}|^{\kappa+3}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+3},2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle h^{3}\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

uniformly over
g\in\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa},h\in T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1),n\in\{0,\dots,T/h\}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,n\}
. Finally, for
\xi^{g,h}_{n}:=h^{-2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\mathbb{E}\zeta^{h}_{k,n}
, we have

\|\xi^{g,h}_{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+6}}\leq h^{-2}\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{T}{h}-1}\max_{n\in\{0,\dots,T/h\}}\|\mathbb{E}\zeta^{h}_{k,n}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+6}}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa}},

uniformly over
g\in\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa},h\in T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1)
and
n\in\{0,\dots T/h\}
.

Proof 3.5

(Proof of Theorem
1
)
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{C}^{5}_{\kappa}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
h\in T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1)
. Writing
\varphi:=\mathcal{F}v^{g}
, Lemma
3
implies

\mathbb{E}g(\chi_{T/h}^{h})-g(X_{T}^{0})=h\sum_{n=0}^{\frac{T}{h}-1}h\mathbb{E}\varphi_{nh}(\chi_{k}^{h})+h^{2}\xi^{g,h}_{T/h},

with

\|\xi^{g,h}_{T/h}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+6}}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa}},\text{ uniformly over }g\in\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa},h\in T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1).

We can then express the sum as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\frac{T}{h}-1}h\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{nh}(\chi_{n}^{h})]=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\varphi_{t}(X_{t}^{0})\,dt+h\sum_{n=0}^{\frac{T}{h}-1}\mathbb{E}\varphi_{nh}(\chi_{n}^{h})-\varphi_{nh}(X_{nh}^{0})

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=0}^{\frac{T}{h}-1}h\varphi_{nh}(X_{nh}^{0})-\int_{0}^{T}\varphi_{t}(X_{t}^{0})\,dt.

Note that
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa+l+4}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with

\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa+l+4}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|g(X^{0,\cdot}_{T})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l+2}_{\kappa+l+2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l+2}_{\kappa}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

(15)

uniformly over
g\in\mathcal{C}^{5}_{\kappa}
, by Lemma
3.2
and Lemma
2.1
(v), for
l\leq 3
. Thus, we may use Lemma
3
again to estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\frac{T}{h}-1}|\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{nh}(\chi_{n}^{h})]-\varphi_{nh}(X_{nh}^{0})|\leq

\displaystyle h^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\frac{T}{h}-1}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}|\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{F}_{kh}[\varphi_{nh}(X^{0,\cdot}_{nh})](\chi_{k}^{h})]+\hat{\xi}^{g,h}_{n}|,

where by Inequality (
15
)

\|\hat{\xi}^{g,h}_{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+13}}\lesssim\|\varphi_{nh}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa+7}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa+7}}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}_{\kappa}},

uniformly over
g\in\mathcal{C}^{5}_{\kappa},h\in T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1)
and
n\in\{0,\dots,T/h\}
. Here (and implicitly also in other places) we use the fact

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|w_{t}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|w\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})},\text{ uniformly over }w\in\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),

for all
\kappa,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Using Lemma
2.3
, we also have

\|\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{F}_{kh}[\varphi_{nh}(X^{0,\cdot}_{nh})](\chi_{k}^{h})]\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+10}}\lesssim\|\mathcal{F}_{kh}[\varphi_{nh}(X^{0,\cdot}_{nh})]\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+10}}\lesssim\|\varphi_{nh}(X_{nh}^{0,\cdot})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}_{\kappa+8}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}_{\kappa+6}}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{4}_{\kappa}},

uniformly over
h\in(0,1),k\leq n\in\{0,\dots,T/h\}
, and
g\in\mathcal{C}^{4}_{\kappa}
. We conclude

h\|\sum_{n=0}^{\frac{T}{h}-1}\mathbb{E}\varphi_{nh}(\chi_{n}^{h})-\varphi_{nh}(X_{nh}^{0})\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+13}}\lesssim h\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{4}_{\kappa}},

uniformly over
g\in\mathcal{C}^{4}_{\kappa}
and
h\in(0,1)
.
Further, approximating the integral
\int\varphi(X^{0})\,dt
by a left Riemann sum yields

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{n=0}^{\frac{T}{h}-1}h\varphi_{nh}(X_{nh}^{0})-\int_{0}^{T}\varphi_{t}(X_{t}^{0})\,dt\right|\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}hT\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\partial_{t}(\varphi_{t}(X_{t}^{0}))|,

Thus, using Lemma
2.1
(v) and Inequality (
15
) once more

\displaystyle\|\sum_{n=0}^{\frac{T}{h}-1}h\varphi_{nh}(X_{nh}^{0})-\int_{0}^{T}\varphi_{t}(X_{t}^{0})\,dt\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+6}}\lesssim h\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\partial_{t}(\varphi_{t}(X^{0}_{t}))\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+6}}\lesssim h\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\varphi_{t}(X^{0}_{t})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{\kappa+6}}

\displaystyle\lesssim h\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{\kappa+5}}\lesssim h\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa}},

uniformly over
g\in\mathcal{C}^{3}_{\kappa}
and
h\in T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1)
.
Putting all estimates together yields

\mathbb{E}g(\chi_{T/h}^{h})-g(X_{T}^{0})=h\int_{0}^{T}\varphi_{t}(X_{t}^{0})\,dt+h^{2}\rho^{g,h},

with

\|\rho^{g,h}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+13}}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}_{\kappa}}

uniformly over
g\in\mathcal{C}^{5}_{\kappa}
and
h\in T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1)
.

4
Differentiation and regularity

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
F
be a Fréchet space,
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open and
f:U\to F
be a continuous function.
The
derivative
of
f
at
x\in U
in the direction
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined by

Df(x)v=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}(f(x+hv)-f(x)).

If the limit exists, then we say
f
is differentiable at
x
in the direction
v
. We say
f
is continuously differentiable on
U
if the limit exists for all
x\in U
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and if
Df:U\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to F
is continuous.

We further define higher derivatives recursively

D^{l+1}f(x)(v_{1},\dots,v_{l},v_{l+1})=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}(D^{l}f(x+hv_{l+1})(v_{1},\dots,v_{l})-D^{l}f(x)(v_{1},\dots v_{l})),\quad l\in\mathbb{N},

with
D^{0}f=f
. We say
f
is
l
-times continuously differentiable (on
U
) if
D^{1}f,\dots,D^{l}f
exist everywhere and
D^{k}f:U\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}\to F
is continuous for all
k\leq l
.
We write
\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,F)
for the set of
l
-times continuously differentiable functions
U\to F
.

If
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,F)
, then
D^{l}f(x)(v_{1},\dots,v_{l})
is symmetric in
v_{1},\dots,v_{l}
. That is, its value is unchanged when permuting
v_{1},\dots,v_{l}
.

If
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is not necessarily open with interior
\operatorname{int}U
, and
V\subseteq F
, then we write
\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,V)
for the set of continuous functions
f:U\to F
such that

•

f(U)\subseteq V
,

•

f:\operatorname{int}U\to F\in\mathcal{C}^{l}
,

•

D^{k}f:\operatorname{int}U\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}\to F
can be extended to a unique continuous function
D^{k}f:U\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}\to F
, for all
k\leq l
.

For
U=I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-m}
where
I_{1},\dots,I_{m}
are bounded intervals, one can show that
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,V)
implies that
f
has a
\mathcal{C}^{l}
extension to an open neighborhood of
U
(see Lemma
62
in the Appendix). Moreover, the derivatives of extensions of
f
are uniquely determined by
f
on the boundary of
U
.

We can express (higher) derivatives using the standard basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{d}\}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Given a multi-index
\alpha
with
|\alpha|\leq l
we define the
partial derivative
of
f
at
x
with respect to
\alpha
by

\partial^{\alpha}f(x)=D^{|\alpha|}f(x)(\overbrace{e_{1},\dots,e_{1}}^{\alpha(1)},\dots,\overbrace{e_{d},\dots,e_{d}}^{\alpha(d)}),\quad x\in U.

This defines a function
\partial^{\alpha}f:U\to F
if all the relevant limits exist. We define
\partial^{\alpha}f=f
for
|\alpha|=0
.
The symmetry of
D^{l}f(x)
implies that the order in which the vectors
e_{1},\dots,e_{d}
are listed does not matter, only how often each vector appears. It also clear that the definition of
\partial^{\alpha}f
makes sense as soon as the lower order derivatives
\partial^{\beta}f
with
\beta\leq\alpha
are defined.
For
l\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in U
we define the
l
-th order gradient
\nabla^{l}f(x)\in F^{d^{\times l}}
of
f
at
x
by

(\nabla^{l}f(x))_{i}=\partial_{i_{l}}\dots\partial_{i_{1}}f(x),\quad i\in d^{\times l},

yielding a function
\nabla^{l}f:U\to F^{d^{\times l}}
if all relevant partial derivatives exists. Also we set
\nabla^{0}f=f
.

We can extend the domain of
D^{l}f(x)
to the space of arrays
\mathbb{R}^{d^{\times l}}
as follows:

\displaystyle D^{l}f(x)A=

\displaystyle D^{l}f(x)\left(\sum_{i\in d^{\times l}}A_{i}(e_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{i_{l}})\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in d^{\times l}}A_{i}D^{l}f(x)(e_{i_{1}},\dots,e_{i_{l}}),

for
x\in U
and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\times l}}
.

For most purposes, we consider the spaces
(F^{d_{2}})^{d_{1}}
and
F^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
the same, via the canonical bijection

(F^{d_{2}})^{d_{1}}\to F^{d_{1}\times d_{2}},((x_{1,1},\dots,x_{1,d_{2}}),\dots,(x_{d_{1},1},\dots,x_{d_{1},d_{2}}))\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}x_{1,1}&\dots&x_{1,d_{2}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
x_{d_{1},1}&\dots&x_{d_{1},d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

Similarly, we identify
((F^{d_{l}})^{\dots})^{d_{1}}\cong F^{d_{1}\times\dots\times d_{l}}
.
For example, given
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
we have
\nabla f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to(\mathbb{R}^{m})^{d}
by definition. However, we prefer using the equivalent version
\nabla f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
, so that
\nabla f(x)
denotes the
transpose
of the Jacobi matrix
1
1

1

Working with the Jacobi matrix instead of its transpose is awkward in our framework. We would have to define
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
differently.
of
f
at
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We denote the Jacobi matrix of
f
by
\nabla^{\intercal}f
instead.
Moreover, given a Fréchet space
F,U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
f:U\to F
we consider
\nabla^{2}f:U\to F^{d\times d}
and
\nabla\nabla f:U\to(F^{d})^{d}
the same (and the same as
\nabla^{2}f:U\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times m}
if
F=\mathbb{R}^{m}
). Similar identifications are applied to higher order gradients.

Now with that mind, if
F
is a Fréchet algebra, then

D^{l}f(x)A=\langle A,\nabla^{l}f(x)\rangle,\quad x\in U,A\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\times l}}.

We can also characterize smoothness through partial derivatives or higher order gradients.

Lemma 4
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d},V\subseteq F
and
f:U\to V
. Then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,V)
if and only if
\partial^{\alpha}f:U\to F
exists for all
\alpha
with
|\alpha|\leq l
and
\partial^{\alpha}f\in\mathcal{C}(U,F)
.

Thus, the continuity of
D^{l}f
is also equivalent to the continuity of
\nabla^{l}f:U\to F^{d^{\times l}}
if
D^{l}f
exists. Also if
F
is a Banach space, then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,V)
is equivalent to
f
being
l
-times continuous differentiable in the sense of Fréchet derivatives.

Familiar rules for differentiating linear combinations, products and compositions apply, see Proposition
5
below. For the Faa di bruno formula we need some additional notation.
Given a multi-index
\alpha
, a
partition
of
\alpha
is a set
\mathcal{B}:=\{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k}\}
of multi-indices such that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\beta_{i}=\alpha
. We denote by
\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{k}
the set of partitions of
\alpha
with
k
elements. Further, we define
\mathcal{B}!=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\beta_{i}!
.
Denote the symmetric group on
\{1,\dots,k\}
by
\mathcal{S}_{k}
. Given arrays
(A_{1},\dots,A_{k})
we define

\bigodot_{i=1}^{k}A_{i}=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{k}}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}A_{\sigma(i)}.

Note that the order of the
A_{i}
on the left-hand side does not matter.
Given
\mathcal{B}=\{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k}\}\in\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{k}
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{|\alpha|}
we define

\partial^{\mathcal{B}}f=\bigodot_{i=1}^{k}\partial^{\beta_{i}}f.

Proposition 5
.

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

(a)

If
c\in\mathbb{R},f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{l}
, then
cf+g\in\mathcal{C}^{l}
and
\partial^{\alpha}(cf+g)=c\partial^{\alpha}f+\partial^{\alpha}g
for
|\alpha|\leq l
.

(b)

If
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{l}
, then
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{l}
and

\partial^{\alpha}\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\right)=\sum_{\beta_{1}+\dots+\beta_{n}=\alpha}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta_{1}!\cdots\beta_{n}!}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\partial^{\beta_{i}}f_{i},\quad|\alpha|\leq l\text{ (Leibniz)}.

(c)

f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{l}
, then
g\circ f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}
and

\partial^{\alpha}(g\circ f)=\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha|}\sum_{\mathcal{B}\in\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{k}}\frac{\alpha!}{\mathcal{B}!}\langle\nabla^{k}g\circ f,\partial^{\mathcal{B}}f\rangle,\quad|\alpha|\leq l\ \text{ (Faa di Bruno)}.

In Proposition
5
we implicitly assume that the sums, products and compositions are well-defined.

4.1
Weighted Hölder spaces

We introduce weighted Hölder spaces, generalizing spaces of functions of polynomial growth
\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\kappa}
and Lipschitz functions
\operatorname{Lip}^{l}
. The main goal here is just to prove various stability properties for different special cases all at once, including smooth functions with Lipschitz derivatives or derivatives of polynomial growth. We do not directly use general weighted Hölder spaces in any of our applications.

We call a function
\mathcal{V}:[0,\infty)\to[1,\infty)
a
weight function
if

•

\mathcal{V}(x+y)\lesssim\mathcal{V}(x)+\mathcal{V}(y)
,

•

\mathcal{V}(xy)\lesssim\mathcal{V}(x)\mathcal{V}(y)
,

uniformly over
x,y\geq 0
.
Given
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we write
\mathcal{V}(x)=\mathcal{V}(|x|)
and
\mathcal{V}(x,y)=\mathcal{V}(x)\vee\mathcal{V}(y)
.
The only weight functions of interest to us are polynomial weights of the form
\mathcal{V}(x)=C(1+x^{\kappa}),x\geq 0
for some
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Nevertheless, for elegance and brevity reasons we work with abstract weights
\mathcal{V}
in the following.

Note that set of weight functions is stable under products and compositions. This is proven in the polynomial case in the next lemma.
We also show that polynomial weights are indeed weight functions.

Lemma 6
.

Write
\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(x):=1+x^{\kappa},x\geq 0,\kappa\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Let
\kappa,\lambda\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then:

(a)

\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(x+y)\lesssim\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(x)+\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(y),\text{ uniformly over }x,y\geq 0
.

(b)

\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(xy)\leq\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(x)\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(y),\quad x,y\geq 0
.

(c)

If
\kappa\leq\lambda
, then
\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}\lesssim\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}
.

(d)

\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}\lesssim\mathcal{V}_{\kappa+\lambda}
.

(e)

\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}\circ\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}\lesssim\mathcal{V}_{\kappa\lambda}
.

Proof 4.1
.

Let
x,y\geq 0
. Then the estimates follow from simple calculations:

(a)

1+(x+y)^{\kappa}\leq 1+2^{\kappa-1}x^{\kappa}+2^{\kappa-1}y^{\kappa}\leq 2^{\kappa-1}(1+x^{\kappa}+1+y^{\kappa})
.

(b)

(1+x^{\kappa})(1+y^{\kappa})=(1+x^{\kappa}+y^{\kappa}+(xy)^{\kappa})\geq 1+(xy)^{\kappa}
.

(c)

1+x^{\kappa}\leq 1+(1\vee x^{\lambda})\leq 2(1+x^{\lambda})
.

(d)

(1+x^{\kappa})(1+x^{\lambda})\leq 1+x^{\kappa}+x^{\lambda}+x^{\kappa+\lambda}\leq(3+x^{\kappa+\lambda})\vee(1+3x^{\kappa+\lambda})\leq 3(1+x^{\kappa+\lambda})
.

(e)

1+(1+x^{\kappa})^{\lambda}\leq 1+\sum_{k=0}^{\lambda}\binom{\lambda}{k}x^{\kappa k}\leq 1+2^{\lambda}x^{\kappa\lambda}
.

In the following, we consider sequences
\mathbb{N}\to[0,\infty]
. Given sequences
a,b
we write
a\leq b
if
a_{p}\leq b_{p}
for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
, and similarly for
<
. Constants
c\in[0,\infty]
are identified with the constant sequence
(c,c,\dots)
. In particular,
a<\infty
means
a_{p}<\infty
for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
. We extend all functions
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
to sequences
a<\infty
in the usual way (
“component-wise”
).

Let
F
be a graded Fréchet space. We denote the grading
\mathbb{N}\to[0,\infty),p\mapsto\|\cdot\|_{p}
also simply by
\|\cdot\|
.
Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d},V\subseteq F
,
\delta\in[0,1]
,
\mathcal{V}
be a weight function and
f:U\to V\in\mathcal{C}
.
We define non-decreasing sequences
\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}}},\|f\|_{\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{V}}}:\mathbb{N}\to[0,\infty]
by

\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}},p}:=\sup_{x\in U}\frac{\|f(x)\|_{p}}{\mathcal{V}(x)},\quad\|f\|_{\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{V}},p}:=\sup_{x\neq y\in U}\frac{\|f(x)-f(y)\|_{p}}{\mathcal{V}(x,y)|x-y|^{\delta}},\quad p\in\mathbb{N}.

Recall that if
F
is a graded Fréchet algebra, then for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
we define a grading
(\|\cdot\|_{p})_{p\in\mathbb{N}}
on
F^{d^{\times k}}
so that we have

\|\nabla^{k}f\|_{p}=\max_{i\leq d^{\times k}}\|\nabla^{k}_{i}f\|_{p}=\max_{|\alpha|\leq k}\|\partial^{\alpha}f\|_{p}

for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{k}
. The latter maximum is taken over all multi-indices with sizes up
k
(including size
0
).
Given
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we further define

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\mathcal{V}}}:=

\displaystyle\max_{k\leq l}\|\nabla^{k}f\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}}}=\max_{|\alpha|\leq l}\|\partial^{\alpha}f\|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}}},

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{l,\delta}_{\mathcal{V}}}:=

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{l}f\|_{\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{V}}}=\max_{|\alpha|=l}\|\partial^{\alpha}f\|_{\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{l,\delta}_{\mathcal{V}}},

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l,\delta}_{\mathcal{V}}}=

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\mathcal{V}}}\vee\|f\|_{\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{l,\delta}_{\mathcal{V}}}.

Next, for
\delta\in(0,1]
we define

\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}_{\mathcal{V}}}:=\|f\|_{\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{1}_{\mathcal{V}}},\quad\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}^{l+\delta}_{\mathcal{V}}}:=\max_{k<l}\|\nabla^{k}f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}_{\mathcal{V}}}\vee\|f\|_{\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{l,\delta}_{\mathcal{V}}}.

We write
f\in E
if
\|f\|_{E}<\infty
for
E\in\{\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\mathcal{V}},\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{l,\delta}_{\mathcal{V}},\mathcal{C}^{l,\delta}_{\mathcal{V}},\dots\}
(also implying all derivatives necessary for this statement to be meaningful exist). We also write
\mathcal{C}^{l}_{\mathcal{V}}=\mathcal{C}^{l,0}_{\mathcal{V}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}}=\mathcal{C}^{0}_{\mathcal{V}}
, as well as
\mathcal{C}^{l,\delta}_{b}:=\mathcal{C}^{l,\delta}_{(x\mapsto 1)},\mathcal{C}^{l}_{b}:=\mathcal{C}^{l,0}_{b},\mathcal{C}_{b}:=\mathcal{C}^{0}_{b},\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{l,\delta}=\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{l,\delta}_{x\mapsto 1},\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}=\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{0,\delta}
,
\operatorname{Lip}=\operatorname{Lip}_{(x\mapsto 1)}
and
\operatorname{Lip}^{l+\delta}=\operatorname{Lip}^{l+\delta}_{(x\mapsto 1)}
. Here,
x\mapsto 1
is the constant weight, which is equal to
1
everywhere. Note that
\operatorname{Lip}^{l}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{l-1}
, but
\operatorname{Lip}^{l}\nsubseteq\mathcal{C}^{l}
.
Finally, we define
\operatorname{Lip}^{l+\delta}_{b}=\operatorname{Lip}^{l+\delta}\cap\mathcal{C}^{l}_{b}
with
