\nabla R\in\operatorname{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{d},L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z},\mathbb{R}^{d}))
we deduce the inequality for
F
.
By Lemma
20
we have
\nabla R_{\bm{z}(n)}\in\operatorname{Lip}^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d},L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
Thus, also
f_{t}^{h}\in\operatorname{Lip}^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d},L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{d}))
uniformly in
t\in[0,T],h\in(0,1)
. Further, we have
\mathbb{E}\nabla R_{\bm{z}(n)}\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
with

\mathbb{E}[\nabla R_{\bm{z}(n)}]=\nabla\mathbb{E}[R_{\bm{z}(n)}]=\nabla\mathcal{R}\in\operatorname{Lip}^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Thus,
\bar{f}\in\operatorname{Lip}^{l}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Hence, also
\nabla R_{\bm{z}(0)}-\nabla\mathcal{R}\in\operatorname{Lip}^{l}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{l-1}_{1}
and so
\Sigma\in\mathcal{C}^{l}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
by Lemma
2.1
(iv) and Lemma
11
.
Finally, we compute
\mathbb{E}f_{t}^{h}(\theta)=-u_{t}\nabla\mathcal{R}(\theta)=\bar{f}_{t}(\theta)
and

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}f_{t}^{h}(\theta)=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}\left[-\frac{u_{t}}{B}\sum_{k=0}^{B-1}\nabla R_{\bm{z}(k+B\left\lfloor t/h\right\rfloor)}(\theta)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{u_{t}^{2}}{B^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{B-1}\operatorname{Cov}[\nabla R_{\bm{z}(k+B\left\lfloor t/h\right\rfloor)}(\theta)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{u_{t}^{2}}{B}\mathbb{E}[(\nabla R_{\bm{z}(0)}(\theta)-\nabla\mathcal{R}(\theta))^{\otimes 2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t}(\theta),

for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d},h\in(0,1)
and
t\in[0,T]
.

7.2
Linear regression

In this subsection we introduce our default example: population risk minimization for linear regression.

Suppose we are given an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random variable
\bm{x}
and an
\mathbb{R}
-valued random variable
\bm{\varepsilon}
defined on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
such that
\bm{x}
and
\bm{\varepsilon}
are independent,
\mathbb{E}\bm{\varepsilon}=0,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}:=\mathbb{E}\bm{\varepsilon}^{2}<\infty
, the covariance matrix
\kappa
of
\bm{x}
is positive definite, and
\bm{x}
has finite joint fourth moments

\mathbb{E}|\bm{x}_{i}\bm{x}_{j}\bm{x}_{k}\bm{x}_{l}|<\infty,\quad i,j,k,l\in\{1,\dots,d\}.

We define
\mu_{x}^{4}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\times 4}}
by

(\mu_{x}^{4})_{i,j,k,l}=\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}_{i}\bm{x}_{j}\bm{x}_{k}\bm{x}_{l}],\quad i,j,k,l\in\{1,\dots,d\}.

For simplicity we make the stronger assumption that
|\bm{x}|+|\bm{\varepsilon}|<C
for some constant
C>0
.
Let
\theta^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We define the
\mathbb{R}
-valued random variable
\bm{y}
by

\bm{y}=\langle\theta^{*},\bm{x}\rangle+\bm{\varepsilon}.

Denote the distribution of
(\bm{x},\bm{y})
by
\nu
. By assumption
(\bm{x},\bm{y})
take values in a bounded set
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Note in the literature on linear regression the
features

\bm{x}
are often assumed to be deterministic. This is an appropriate assumption if the data is generated by a
randomized controlled trial
. In these experiments, experimental conditions are controlled by the researchers, so the features can be treated as deterministic. In contrast, in machine learning and especially anything using the label
“big data”
, the data is generated by a process outside of our control. Thus, we are working with
observational data
. In this case, it is more appropriate to assume
\bm{x}
to be random, which is why we do so here.

Let
\ell
be the
square loss
, given by
\ell(y,y^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}(y-y^{\prime})^{2}
. The goal is to fit the data drawn from
\nu
using a linear predictor
\theta\mapsto\langle\theta,x\rangle
.
Thus, for any data point
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
we consider the squared risk

R_{x,y}(\theta)=\ell(\langle\theta,x\rangle,y)=\frac{1}{2}(\langle\theta,x\rangle-y)^{2}.

We stress that the bold letters
\bm{x},\bm{y}
denote random variables, while
x,y
represent realizations.
Note that
R_{x,y}(\theta)
is smooth in
\theta
, with derivatives uniformly in
(x,y)\in\mathcal{Z}
(since
\mathcal{Z}
is bounded). That is,
R\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z},\mathbb{R}))
.
Further,

\nabla R_{x,y}(\theta)=(\langle\theta,x\rangle-y)x,\nabla^{2}R_{x,y}(\theta)=x^{\otimes 2},\quad(x,y)\in\mathcal{Z},\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Thus,
\nabla R\in\operatorname{Lip}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z},\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Hence,
R
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
21
.
The population risk is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\theta)=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[R_{\bm{x},\bm{y}}(\theta)]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[(\langle\theta-\theta^{*},\bm{x}\rangle-\bm{\varepsilon})^{2}]=\frac{1}{2}\langle\kappa,(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{2}.

The minimum of
\mathcal{R}
, that is the best possible fit for the linear model, is given by the population parameter
\theta^{*}
.

For
S(\theta):=\operatorname{Cov}[\nabla R_{\bm{x},\bm{y}}(\theta)]
we have

\displaystyle S(\theta)=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\langle\theta,\bm{x}\rangle-\bm{y})^{2}\bm{x}^{\otimes 2}]-(\kappa(\theta-\theta^{*}))^{\otimes 2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\langle\theta-\theta^{*},\bm{x}\rangle-\bm{\varepsilon})^{2}\bm{x}^{\otimes 2}]-\kappa(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\kappa

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\langle\theta-\theta^{*},\bm{x}\rangle^{2}\bm{x}^{\otimes 2}]-2\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon\langle\theta-\theta^{*},\bm{x}\rangle\bm{x}^{\otimes 2}]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}[\bm{\varepsilon}^{2}\bm{x}^{\otimes 2}]-\kappa(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\kappa

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\mu_{x}^{4},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle-\kappa(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\kappa^{\intercal}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\mu_{x}^{4}-\kappa^{\otimes 2},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa

We are mostly interested in the following two settings, where we can simplify
S
further (ignoring the boundedness of
\mathcal{Z}
for a moment).

Example 7.3
.

(a)

We assume that the features are centered Gaussian, that is
\bm{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\kappa)
.
Then we can simplify the covariance matrix of the gradient noise to

S(\theta)=2\kappa(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\kappa+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa.

To see this, let
\tau
be a permutation of the set
\{1,\dots,l\}
and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\times l}}
. Then we write
B_{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\times l}}
for

(B_{\tau})_{i_{1},\dots,i_{l}}=B_{i_{\tau(1)},\dots,i_{\tau(l)}}.

For example if
B
is matrix, then
B^{\intercal}=B_{(12)}
. Here we use the cycle notation for permutations.
By Isserli’s theorem
(
13
, see)
, the joint fourth moments of a centered Gaussian satisfy

\mu_{x}^{4}=\kappa^{\otimes 2}+\kappa^{\otimes 2}_{(23)}+\kappa^{\otimes 2}_{(13)}.

Given matrices
U,A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
we have

\displaystyle\langle U^{\otimes 2}_{(23)},A\rangle_{i,j}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}U_{i,k}U_{j,l}A_{k,l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle UAU^{\intercal},

\displaystyle\langle U^{\otimes 2}_{(13)},A\rangle_{i,j}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}U_{k,j}U_{i,l}A_{k,l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle UA^{\intercal}U.

Therefore,
S(\theta)=2\kappa(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\kappa+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa
.

(b)

We assume that
d=1
, but not that
\bm{x}
is Gaussian. Then, we can write

\displaystyle S(\theta)=\kappa^{2}(\Kurt\bm{x}-1)(\theta-\theta^{*})^{2}+\kappa\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2},

where
\Kurt\bm{x}:=\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}^{4}]/\kappa^{2}
is the
kurtosis
of
\bm{x}
(see Section
23
in the appendix for more information about kurtosis).

We can join these two settings by assuming that there exists a constant
c>0
, such that

S(\theta)=c\kappa(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\kappa+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa,\quad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(31)

In particular, in Example
7.3
(a) we have
c=2
and for (b) we have
c=\Kurt\bm{x}-1
.

Under this condition, we
\sqrt{\Sigma}=u_{t}\sqrt{S}
satisfies the assumptions of Corollary
18
as the next lemma implies.

Lemma 22
.

Let
S
be defined by (
31
). Then
\sqrt{S}\in\operatorname{Lip}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
.

Proof 7.4
.

Let
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and set
w:=\kappa^{1/2}(\theta-\theta^{*})
and assume
w\neq 0
. Then

S(\theta)=c(\kappa^{1/2}w)(\kappa^{1/2}w)^{\intercal}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa=\kappa^{1/2}(cww^{\intercal}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}1_{d\times d})\kappa^{1/2}.

Writing
M=cww^{\intercal}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}1_{d\times d}
we get

S(\theta)^{1/2}=\kappa^{1/2}M^{1/2}\kappa^{1/2}.

Note that
M
is a rank-one perturbation of
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}1_{d\times d}
. We have

ww^{\intercal}w=|w|^{2}w,\quad ww^{\intercal}u=0,\quad u\in w^{\perp},

where
w^{\perp}=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\langle u,v\rangle=0\}
.
Thus,
ww^{\intercal}
has eigenvalues
|w|^{2}
and
0
, and by the rank-nullity theorem the following orthogonal eigenspace decomposition

\mathbb{R}^{d}=\operatorname{Ker}(ww^{\intercal})\oplus\operatorname{Im}(ww^{\intercal})=w^{\perp}\oplus\operatorname{span}w.

Accordingly,

Mw=c|w|^{2}w+\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon}w=(c|w|^{2}+\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon})w,\quad Mu=\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}u,\quad u\in w^{\perp},

and
M
has the same eigenspace decomposition. Therefore,
M
has eigenvalues
c|w|^{2}+\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon}
and
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}
with (algebraic) multiplicities
1
and
d-1
respectively.
There is an orthonormal basis
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{d}\}
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
v_{1}=\frac{w}{|w|}
and by defining a matrix
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with columns
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
we can transform
M
into the diagonal matrix

Q^{\intercal}MQ=\operatorname{diag}(c|w|^{2}+\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon},\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon},\dots,\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon}).

Then,

Q^{\intercal}M^{1/2}Q=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda,\sigma_{\varepsilon},\dots,\sigma_{\varepsilon}),

where
\lambda:=\sqrt{c|w|^{2}+\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon}}
.
Note that

Q(e_{1}e_{1}^{\intercal})Q^{\intercal}=(Qe_{1})(Qe_{1})^{\intercal}=\frac{ww^{\intercal}}{|w|^{2}}=:P_{w}.

Thus,

M^{1/2}=Q(\sigma_{\varepsilon}1_{d\times d}+(\lambda-\sigma_{\varepsilon})e_{1}e_{1}^{\intercal})Q^{\intercal}=\sigma_{\varepsilon}1_{d\times d}+a(|w|^{2})P_{w},

where

a_{t}=-\sigma_{\varepsilon}+\sqrt{ct+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}=\frac{ct}{-\sigma_{\varepsilon}+\sqrt{ct+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}},\quad\geq 0.

Note that
a\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}([0,\infty))
, and

|\partial^{k}a_{t}|\asymp(ct+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}(2k-1)},\text{ uniformly over }t\geq 0,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

In particular,

|\partial^{k}a_{t}|=O(t^{1/2-k}),\quad t\to\infty.

Define
b(t)=\frac{a(t)}{t},t>0
. By Leibniz’s formula we have
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}([0,\infty))
with

|\partial^{k}b(t)|\lesssim\sum_{j=0}^{k}|\partial^{j}a(t)|t^{-(k+1-j)},\text{ uniformly over }t\geq 0,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Hence,

|\partial^{k}b(t)|=O(t^{-1/2-k}),\quad t\to\infty,\quad k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(32)

Note that

a(|w|^{2})P_{w}=b(|w|^{2})ww^{\intercal}

and by Equation (
32
)

|b(|w|^{2})ww^{\intercal}|=O(|w|),\quad|w|\to\infty.

Further, by Faa di Bruno’s formula

\displaystyle|\partial^{\beta}(b(|w|^{2}))|\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta|}\sum_{\mathcal{B}\in\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{k}}\partial_{t}^{k}b(|w|^{2})\partial^{\mathcal{B}}(|w|^{2}).

Since
|\partial^{\gamma}(|w|^{2})|=O(|w|^{2-|\gamma|})
we have for
\mathcal{B}=\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}\}\in\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{k}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i}=\beta

|\partial_{t}^{k}b(|w|^{2})\partial^{\mathcal{B}}(|w|^{2})|=O(|w|^{-1-2k}|w|^{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(2-|\gamma_{i}|)})=O(|w|^{-1-|\beta|}),\quad|w|\to\infty,

for all
k\leq|\beta|
.
Thus, using Leibniz’s formula

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}(b(|w|^{2})ww^{\intercal})|\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}|\partial^{\beta}(b(|w|^{2}))\partial^{\alpha-\beta}(ww^{\intercal})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}|w|^{-1-|\beta|}|w|^{2-|\alpha|+|\beta|}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(|w|^{1-|\alpha|}),\quad|w|\to\infty.

Thus, the derivatives of
w\mapsto b(|w|^{2})ww^{\intercal})
are bounded at
0
and
\infty
, i.e.
w\mapsto b(|w|^{2})ww^{\intercal})\in\operatorname{Lip}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
.
Since

\sqrt{S(\theta)}=\kappa^{1/2}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}1_{d\times d}+a(|w|^{2})P_{w})\kappa^{1/2}=\kappa^{1/2}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}1_{d\times d}+b(|w|^{2})ww^{\intercal})\kappa^{1/2},

even if
w=0
, we conclude
\sqrt{S}\in\operatorname{Lip}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
.

So far we described one-pass SGD for linear regression. Let us contrast this with SGD with replacement.
Consider a finite sample
(x_{n},y_{n})_{n=1}^{N}
of size
N
.

We now consider the risk minimization problem
(R,\tilde{\nu})
with

R:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{Z}\to[0,\infty),(\theta,(x,y))\mapsto R_{z}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}(\langle\theta,x\rangle-y)^{2}

and where
\tilde{\nu}
is the empirical measure

\tilde{\nu}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathds{1}_{\cdot}(x_{n},y_{n}).

We can set
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
here since
\tilde{\nu}
automatically has bounded support.
Let
\bm{z}
be a random variable
\bm{z}\sim\tilde{\nu}
.
The expected risk for
(R,\tilde{\nu})
is the empirical risk for our finite sample:

\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}[R_{\bm{z}}(\theta)]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}R_{(x_{n},y_{n})}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\langle\theta,x_{n}\rangle-y_{n})^{2},\quad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Its minimum given by the OLS estimator:

\hat{\theta}:=\left(\sum_{n=1}^{N}x_{n}x_{n}^{\intercal}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{n=1}^{N}x_{n}y_{n}\right).

If we want to compare one-pass SGD and SGD with replacement, then the right way to proceed is to replace
(x_{n},y_{n})_{n=1}^{N}
with an i.i.d. sequence
(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n})_{n=1}^{N}
drawn from the population
\nu
. Thus, in this situation
\tilde{\nu},\hat{\mathcal{R}}
and the corresponding gradient covariance matrix
\operatorname{Cov}_{z\sim\tilde{\nu}}[\nabla R_{z}]
are random quantities.

Chapter 5
A comparison of first-order SMEs

In this chapter we investigate and compare first-order continuous-time approximations to stochastic gradient descent.
We focus on the case of population risk minimization and one-pass SGD for linear regression.
This chapter is adapted from
6
.

8
Introduction and problem statement

Consider a risk minimization problem
(R:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{Z}\to[0,\infty),\nu)
on a measurable space
\mathcal{Z}
. Fix a batch size
B\in\mathbb{N}
, an i.i.d. sequence
(\bm{z}(n))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathcal{Z}
with
\bm{z}_{n}\sim\nu
and consider the mini-batch SGD method

\chi_{n+1}^{h}=\chi_{n}^{h}-\frac{h}{B}\sum_{k=0}^{B-1}\nabla R_{\bm{z}{(k+Bn)}}(\chi_{n}^{h}).

(33)

Note that for
S(\theta):=\operatorname{Cov}[\nabla\mathcal{R}_{\bm{z}(0)}(\theta)]
, we have

\Sigma(\theta)=\operatorname{Cov}\left(\frac{1}{B}\sum_{k=0}^{B-1}\nabla R_{\bm{z}(k+Bn)}(\theta)\right)=\frac{1}{B}\operatorname{Cov}[\nabla\mathcal{R}_{\bm{z}(0)}(\theta)]=\frac{1}{B}S(\theta),\quad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We can approximate (
33
) using gradient flow given by

\dot{X}_{t}^{0}=-\nabla\mathcal{R}(X_{t}^{0}),

where
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{E}[R_{\bm{z}_{1}}]
. More generally, to model the noise inherent to SGD, we can approximate (
33
) using a first-order stochastic modified equation

dX_{t}^{h}=-\nabla\mathcal{R}(X_{t}^{h})\,dt+\sqrt{hD(X_{t}^{h})}\,dW_{t}.

(34)

There are arguably two main choices considered (more or less explicitly) in the literature. Set
\Sigma(\theta)=\operatorname{Cov}[R_{\bm{z}_{1}}]
.
Then, for
D=\Sigma
, Equation (
34
) becomes a family of stochastic differential equations first introduced by
27
to approximate SGD. We refer to a family of processes solving (
34
) with
D=\Sigma
as (first-order)
stochastic gradient flow with non-constant covariance
or NCC-SGF for short
(
3
, in accordance with the terminology used by)
, and we denote it as
X^{\operatorname{NCC}}
.

In order to simplify the analysis of Equation
34
, in many cases the covariance matrix
\Sigma
is assumed to be well approximated by a constant. For example, frequently one is interested in the behavior of SGD around a stationary point. In fact, suppose gradient flow
X^{0}
converges to a, necessarily stationary, point
X^{0}_{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and set
D=\Sigma(X^{0}_{\infty})
.
Then we refer to a solution of Equation
34
as (first-order)
stochastic gradient flow with constant covariance
or CC-SGF for short
(
3
, again, in accordance with the terminology used by)
, and we denote it as
X^{\operatorname{CC}}
. This is essentially the continuous-time approximation introduced by
32
. Note that the diffusion coefficient may depend on the initial condition, since
X_{\infty}^{0}
may already depend on it.

The introduction of a non-zero diffusion coefficient comes with the implicit promise of having a better approximation to SGD.
However, without an additional modification of the
drift coefficient

-\nabla\mathcal{R}
in Equation (
34
) the SGF dynamics are still merely a first-order approximation. Given that the order of approximation is not improved, does it make sense at all to add a diffusion term to the gradient flow dynamics? And if it does, how can one quantify the benefit?

Theorem
17
indicates that the linear error term when approximating (
33
) by (
34
) depends on
D
.
We find that the linear error terms for GF, CC-SGF and NCC-SGF are generally all different.

Further, we show that for linear regression models, the linear error terms for the objective function can be calculated in closed form. A comparison then reveals that any of three continuous-time approximations can be the best, depending on the batch size (see Theorem
22
below).
However, there is a notable caveat for the case of gradient flow being the best approximation:
Note that the dynamics of learning a linear model using SGD with constant learning rate can be roughly separated into the initial
descent phase
and the final
fluctuation phase
, where SGD, due to the variance of the stochastic gradients, is mostly fluctuating around the global minimum. The batch size at which gradient flow becomes the best approximation increases as the duration of the fluctuation phase increases, relative to the time horizon. On the other hand, the approximation quality of the stochastic approximations is unaffected by the relative duration of the fluctuation phase.
In fact, we show that there are two special batch sizes
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}
and
B^{\operatorname{GF}}
, such that for batch sizes
B<B^{\operatorname{Eq}}
the NCC approximation is the best, followed by CC-SGF for
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}<B<B^{\operatorname{GF}}
and GF for
B>B^{\operatorname{GF}}
. However, we also observe that
B^{\operatorname{GF}}
increases with the duration of the fluctuation phase of SGD. On the other hand,
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}
only depends on the kurtosis of the features.

Fix
T>0
.
Given a continuous-time (stochastic) approximation
Y=(Y^{h}_{t})_{t\in[0,T],h\in T/\mathbb{N}}
of SGD (
33
) we define the linear error term (with respect to
\mathcal{R}
) by

\operatorname{LE}(Y):=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}(\chi_{T/h}^{h})-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}(Y_{T}^{h})}{h},

where the limit is taken in
T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1)
.

9
Comparison of the Linear Error Terms

In this section we compare gradient flow and the two stochastic gradient flow approximations (NCC and CC) in the setting of linear regression using mini-batch SGD.

Firstly, we provide a theoretical comparison using Theorem
17
(see Theorem
22
). We will see that the comparison highly depends on the batch size and on the kurtosis of the features (also called independent variables).
Secondly, we substantiate the theoretical findings using a numerical example.
Proofs are postponed until Section
10
.

We work in the setting of linear regression, as in Subsection
7.2
, and Example
7.3
.
That is, there exist
\theta^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\sigma_{\varepsilon}>0
and a symmetric and positive definite matrix
\kappa
such that

\mathcal{R}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\langle\kappa,(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{2},\quad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

Further, we assume the existence of a constant
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}>0
, such that

S(\theta)=2B^{\operatorname{Eq}}\kappa(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\kappa+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa,\quad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

In particular, in Example
7.3
(a) we have
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}=1
and for (b) we have
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}=\frac{1}{2}(\Kurt\bm{x}-1)
.
Proposition
9.1
below implies that if
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}\in\mathbb{N}
, then it is the batch size
B
where the NCC and CC approximation have the same error, up to flipping the sign.

For technical reasons we need to assume that the population is bounded. However, in our applications we sometimes ignore this extra assumption and also allow, say, for Gaussian features.

Now, the three continuous-time approximations (
34
) with
D=\{0,\Sigma,\Sigma(X_{\infty}^{0})\}
take the form

\displaystyle dX_{t}^{0}=

\displaystyle-\kappa(X_{t}^{0}-\theta^{*})\,dt

\displaystyle dX^{\operatorname{NCC},h}_{t}=

\displaystyle-\kappa(X^{\operatorname{NCC},h}_{t}-\theta^{*})\,dt+\sqrt{\frac{h}{B}}\sqrt{2B^{\operatorname{Eq}}\kappa(X^{\operatorname{NCC},h}_{t}-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\kappa+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa}\,dW_{t}

\displaystyle dX_{t}^{\operatorname{CC},h}=

\displaystyle-\kappa(X_{t}^{\operatorname{CC},h}-\theta^{*})\,dt+\sqrt{\frac{h}{B}\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa}\,dW_{t}.

(35)

Note that the process with constant covariance dynamics (
9
) is an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process. Using (
10.1
) we can derive the following expressions for the linear error terms of the three continuous-time approximations of SGD.

Proposition 9.1
.

Suppose
\chi_{0}^{h}=X_{0}^{0}=X^{\operatorname{NCC},h}_{0}=X^{\operatorname{CC},h}_{0}=\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}(\chi_{T/h}^{h})-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}(X_{T}^{h})=

\displaystyle-\frac{h}{2}T\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2T\kappa},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}(\chi_{T/h}^{h})-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}(X_{T}^{\operatorname{CC},h})=

\displaystyle h\left(\frac{B^{\operatorname{Eq}}}{B}-\frac{1}{2}\right)T\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2T\kappa},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}(\chi_{T/h}^{h})-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}(X_{T}^{0})=

\displaystyle h\left(\frac{B^{\operatorname{Eq}}}{B}-\frac{1}{2}\right)T\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2T\kappa},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{h}{4B}\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\langle\kappa,1_{d\times d}-e^{-2\kappa T}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}).

(36)

as
h\downarrow 0
, with
h\in T/\mathbb{N}\cap(0,1)
.

We introduce some additional notation to succinctly state the following theorem. Given two continuous-time approximations
Y,Z
we write
Y\preceq Z
if
|\operatorname{LE}(Y)|\geq|\operatorname{LE}(Z)|
,
that is if the approximation of SGD with
Y
has (in absolute terms) a greater linear error term than the one using
Z
. More briefly it means that
Z
is not worse than
Y
.
Evidently
\preceq
is a reflexive and transitive relation. We write
Y\asymp Z
if
Y\preceq Z
and
Z\preceq Y
, that is if
Y
and
Z
are equally good approximations. Further, we write
Y\prec Z
if
Y\preceq Z
and
Z\npreceq Y
, that is if
Z
is strictly a better approximation than
Y
.

Theorem 9.1
.

Suppose
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}>0
and we are given an initial value
\theta\neq\theta^{*}
.
Define

B^{\operatorname{GF}}=2B^{\operatorname{Eq}}+\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\langle\kappa,1-e^{-2T\kappa}\rangle}{4T\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2T\kappa},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle}.

Then, we have the following

(i)

X^{0}\prec X^{\operatorname{CC}}\prec X^{\operatorname{NCC}}
, if
B<B^{\operatorname{Eq}}
,

(ii)

X^{0}\prec X^{\operatorname{CC}}\asymp X^{\operatorname{NCC}}
, if
B=B^{\operatorname{Eq}}
,

(iii)

X^{0}\prec X^{\operatorname{NCC}}\prec X^{\operatorname{CC}}
, if
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}<B<B^{\operatorname{GF}}-B^{\operatorname{Eq}}
,

(iv)

X^{\operatorname{NCC}}\prec X^{0}\prec X^{\operatorname{CC}}
, if
B^{\operatorname{GF}}-B^{\operatorname{Eq}}<B<B^{\operatorname{GF}}
,

(v)

X^{\operatorname{NCC}}\prec X^{\operatorname{CC}}\prec X^{0}
, if
B>B^{\operatorname{GF}}
,

(vi)

\operatorname{LE}(X^{\operatorname{CC}})=0
, if
B=2B^{\operatorname{Eq}}
.

In other words, for small batch sizes the best approximation is NCC-SGF, followed by CC-SGF and then gradient flow. If we increase the batch size, then NCC and CC switch places. After that NCC and GF switch places. Finally, for large batch sizes GF becomes the best approximation. Somewhere in between CC is not only the best approximation among the three, but also has a linear error of
0
.

Even though the gradient flow approximation can be the best approximation for large batch sizes, the lower bound
B^{\operatorname{GF}}
for this to occur diverges to
\infty
as

T\to\infty\text{, or }\sigma_{\varepsilon}\to\infty\text{, or }\kappa\to\infty\text{ (for }d=1\text{)}\text{, or }\theta-\theta^{*}\to 0\text{ (for }d=1\text{)}.

(37)

In fact, one can summarize (
37
) by saying
\tau\to\infty
, where
\tau
is the time that SGD spends fluctuating around the global minimum
\theta^{*}
. Therefore, for large
\tau
the SGF approximations are preferable to gradient flow, for all reasonably large batch sizes.

When it comes to deciding between NCC and CC-SGF, the important quantity is
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}
. This quantity only depends on the distribution of
\bm{x}
and
not
on
T,\kappa,\sigma_{\varepsilon}
or
\theta-\theta^{*}
.
For
\bm{x}
Gaussian we have
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}=1
, so the CC-SGF approximation is, perhaps surprisingly, almost always preferred over the NCC approximation. We also consider the case where
d=1
and
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}=\frac{1}{2}(\Kurt x-1)
. In this case we observe for batch sizes that are small, relative to the kurtosis of the features
\bm{x}
, the NCC approximation can still be the best one (see also Section
23
in the appendix for more information on kurtosis).

Overall, one can also say that for highly leptokurtic features, the NCC approximation is the best across a large range of batch sizes. On the other hand, for lower kurtosis the CC approximation is best.

Figure
1
below provides a visual comparison of the three approximations in terms of kurtosis and batch size in two specific examples.

Figure 1:
The best continuous-time approximation of SGD for linear regression in dimension
1
in terms of the kurtosis of the features and the batch size. Here
\kappa=1
,
(\theta-\theta^{*})^{2}=1
and
T=0.5
(
T=2.0
) in the left (right) plot. In the lower part of the middle region, where CC-SGF is the best approximation, gradient flow is worse than NCC-SGF. In the upper part of the middle region, gradient flow is better than NCC-SGF.

9.0.1
The Case of Batch Size 1

Here, we specifically study the case
B=1
. Firstly, we have

X^{0}\prec X^{\operatorname{CC}}\asymp X^{\operatorname{NCC}},\text{ if }\bm{x}\text{ is Gaussian}.

Secondly, if
d=1
and so
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}=\frac{1}{2}(\Kurt\bm{x}-1)
, then

(i)

X^{0}\prec X^{\operatorname{CC}}\prec X^{\operatorname{NCC}}
, if
\Kurt\bm{x}>3
,

(ii)

X^{0}\prec X^{\operatorname{CC}}\asymp X^{\operatorname{NCC}}
, if
\Kurt\bm{x}=3
,

(iii)

X^{\operatorname{NCC}}\prec X^{\operatorname{CC}}
, if
\Kurt\bm{x}\in(1,3)
,

(iv)

X^{\operatorname{NCC}}=X^{\operatorname{CC}}
, if
\Kurt\bm{x}=1
,

(v)

\operatorname{LE}(X^{\operatorname{CC}})=0
, if
\Kurt\bm{x}=2
.

Note that distributions with kurtosis
<3
/
=3
/
>3
are also called platykurtic / mesokurtic / leptokurtic (see also Section
23
)

Gradient flow is always the worst approximation for
\Kurt x\geq 3
.
Assume we are in the platykurtic setting
\Kurt\bm{x}\in(1,3)
. Then gradient flow is the worst / second-best / best approximation if

1<B^{\operatorname{GF}}-B^{\operatorname{Eq}}\quad/\quad B^{\operatorname{GF}}-B^{\operatorname{Eq}}<1<B^{\operatorname{GF}}\quad/\quad B^{\operatorname{GF}}<1.

9.1
A Numerical Example

In this subsection we present results from a numerical experiment confirming the theoretical results presented in Theorem
22
. We also compare the three approximations to the second-order SME (see Corollary
18
), which we here call
second-order stochastic gradient flow
, or SGF2 for short. The corresponding family of stochastic differential equations is given by

\displaystyle dX_{t}^{2,h}=

\displaystyle-\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(X_{t}^{2,h})-\frac{h}{2}\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}(X_{t}^{2,h})\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(X_{t}^{2,h})\,dt+\sqrt{h\Sigma(X_{t}^{2,h})}\,dW_{t}

(38)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\kappa\left(1_{d\times d}+\frac{h}{2}\kappa\right)(X_{t}^{h}-\theta^{*})\,dt+\sqrt{\frac{h}{B}}\sqrt{2B^{\operatorname{Eq}}\kappa(X_{t}^{2,h}-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\kappa+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa}\,dW_{t}

with
X_{0}^{2,h}=\chi_{0}
.

For the remainder of this section we exclusively work in setting (b) from Example
7.3
.

9.1.1
Experimental Setup

We consider using SGD for fitting the particular one-dimensional linear model

\bm{y}=-\bm{x}+\bm{\varepsilon}

(39)

with
\bm{x},\bm{\varepsilon}
independent, centered and of variance
1
, where
\bm{\varepsilon}
is Gaussian. Note that in this case we have
\theta^{*}=-1
.
We compare the weak errors of the population risk
\mathcal{R}
for different continuous-time approximations of SGD. Here we use time horizons
T=0.5
and
T=2.0
, varying distributions of
\bm{x}
and initial values
\theta
. We use a Monte Carlo approximation to estimate
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}(\chi_{T/h}^{h})
, that is

\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}(\chi_{T/h}^{h})\approx\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}(\hat{\chi}_{T/h}^{i,h})

where
\hat{\chi}^{1},\dots,\hat{\chi}^{M}
are independent copies of
\chi
. More precisely, to compute one copy
\hat{\chi}^{i}
we draw
BT/h
i.i.d. samples from the data-generating model (
39
) and then perform SGD for
T/h
steps using a batch of
B
samples in each step, never using any sample twice. Thus, every copy of
\hat{\chi}
uses a different (pseudo-) data set.
For the experiments we have chosen
M
large enough (between
10^{8}
and
2\cdot 10^{9}
) so that the variance of the Monte Carlo estimator is negligible compared to the weak error.
Moreover, to reduce the computational burden significantly, we determine
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}^{e}(Y_{T}^{h})
for
Y=X^{0},X^{\operatorname{NCC}}{h},X^{\operatorname{CC},h},X^{2,h}
using explicit formulas, which can be derived in this example (see Proposition
10.6
in Section
10.2
).
We consider the learning rates
h=0.5,0.1,0.05,0.01,0.005,0.001
. Notice that
T/h
is an integer in each case, where
T\in\{0.5,2.0\}
. Plotted is the dependence of the weak error

\frac{1}{\kappa}|\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}(\chi_{T/h}^{h})-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{R}(Y_{T}^{h})|,

divided by

\kappa
(!), on the learning rate
h
.

9.1.2
Results

In the following
\nu_{x}
denotes any distribution with expectation
m
, such that
\bm{x}+m\sim\nu_{x}
. That is
\bm{x}
has distribution
\nu_{x}
, but shifted to have expectation zero.

2
depicts the weak error’s dependence on the learning rate in the following settings:

Figure 2:
The weak error’s dependence on the learning rate for several continuous-time approximations to SGD, in various settings. The plots (1)-(5) correspond to the settings (i)-(v) in Theorem
22
. Further, (4) and (6) also correspond to (vi). Finally, (6) depicts a situation where
X^{\operatorname{CC}}\not\asymp X^{\operatorname{NCC}}
, but the weak errors are close to each other since the common initial value is close to the minimum.

Aside from minor deviations stemming from the Monte Carlo estimation, the empirical results in Figure
2
confirm the theoretical results in the last subsection. In particular, we observe:

(i)

The experimental settings (1)—(5) correspond exactly to the settings (i)—(v) in Theorem
22
. Note that instead of merely varying the batch size
B
we also varied
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}
and
B^{\operatorname{GF}}
by choosing different
T
and distributions of
\bm{x}
.

(ii)

As indicated by Proposition
9.1
, the experimental setting (6) shows that for
\theta\approx\theta^{*}
and only moderately small learning rates there is little difference between the NCC- and the CC-SGF approximations, while gradient flow is lagging behind by neglecting to model the variance of the residuals
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}
.

(iii)

For
B=B^{\operatorname{Eq}}
, NCC- and CC-SGF are equally good (setting (2)).

(iv)

For
B=2B^{\operatorname{Eq}}
the CC-SGF approximation is of second order
2
2

2

More precisely, the approximation is of order
2
for the chosen test function
\mathcal{R}
. This is a weaker property than being a second-order SME.
(settings (4) and (6)).

(v)

The SGF2 approximation is always best, irrespective of batch size.

We remark that the theoretical rates of convergence are difficult to observe without using a high number of Monte Carlo samples.
Moreover, note that in the experiments we always plotted the weak error while Theorem
22
only applies to the linear error term. The results indicate that the higher order error terms have negligible impact on the total error.

10
Derivations and Proofs for Section
9

In this section we give proper justifications for the results of Section
9
.

10.1
Quadratic Objectives

Here, we derive the linear error terms for the three continuous-time approximations when the objective function is quadratic. This includes ordinary linear regression with SGD using the population risk, but the derivation applies more generally.

Suppose we are given a symmetric and positive definite matrix
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and a quadratic form

\mathcal{R}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\intercal}\kappa\theta+\theta^{\intercal}c^{\prime}+d^{\prime},\quad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

where
c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then
\mathcal{R}
has a global minimum
\theta^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and so we may rewrite it as

\mathcal{R}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\langle\kappa,(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle+d,\quad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

for some
d\in\mathbb{R}
.
Now, consider SGD with
\bar{H}(\theta)=-\nabla\mathcal{R}(\theta)
. The gradient flow equation

dX_{t}^{0}=-\nabla\mathcal{R}(X_{t}^{0})=-\kappa(X_{t}^{0}-\theta^{*})\,dt,

has the unique solution

X_{t}^{0}(\theta)=e^{-t\kappa}(\theta-\theta^{*})+\theta^{*},\quad t\in[0,T],

for every initial condition
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Note that
X_{t}^{0}(\theta)\to X_{\infty}^{0}(\theta)=\theta^{*}
, as
t\to\infty
, for every
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Set
v_{t}(\theta)=\mathcal{R}(X_{T}^{0,t}(\theta)),t\in[0,T],\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, given
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle v_{t}(\theta)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(e^{-(T-t)\kappa}(\theta-\theta^{*})+\theta^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle\kappa,(e^{-(T-t)\kappa}(\theta-\theta^{*}))^{\otimes 2}\rangle+d

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle\kappa,(e^{-(T-t)\kappa}(\theta-\theta^{*}))(e^{-(T-t)\kappa}(\theta-\theta^{*}))^{\intercal}\rangle+d

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle\kappa,e^{-(T-t)\kappa}(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}e^{-(T-t)\kappa}\rangle+d

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle\kappa e^{-2(T-t)\kappa},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle,

Here, we used the property

\langle A,BCD\rangle=\langle B^{\intercal}AD^{\intercal},C\rangle,\quad A,B,C,D\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},

and the fact that
\kappa
and
e^{-2(T-t)\kappa}
are symmetric and commute with each other.
Further,

\nabla\mathcal{R}(\theta)=\kappa(\theta-\theta^{*}),\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}(\theta)=\kappa.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\langle\nabla v_{t}(\theta),\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}\nabla\mathcal{R}\rangle=

\displaystyle\langle\kappa e^{-2(T-t)\kappa}(\theta-\theta^{*}),\kappa^{2}(\theta-\theta^{*})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2(T-t)\kappa},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle\nabla v_{t},\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}\nabla\mathcal{R}\rangle(X_{t}^{0}(\theta))=

\displaystyle\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2(T-t)\kappa},(X_{t}^{0}-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2(T-t)\kappa},(e^{-t\kappa}(\theta-\theta^{*}))^{\otimes 2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2T\kappa},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle,

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}v_{t}(\theta)=

\displaystyle\kappa e^{-2(T-t)\kappa}.

Now, consider
D\in\{0,\Sigma(\theta^{*}),\Sigma\}
and

dX_{t}^{h}=-\nabla\mathcal{R}(X_{t}^{h})+\sqrt{hD(X_{t}^{h})}\,dW_{t},\quad t\in[0,T],h\in\mathcal{H}.

By Theorem
17
we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{LE}(X)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\langle\nabla^{2}v_{t},(\Sigma-D)\rangle(X_{t}^{0})-\langle\nabla v_{t},\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}\nabla\mathcal{R}\rangle(X_{t}^{0})\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\langle\kappa e^{-2(T-t)\kappa},(\Sigma-D)(X_{t}^{0})\rangle\,dt-\frac{1}{2}T\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2T\kappa},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle.

(40)

Proof 10.1

(Proof of Proposition
9.1
)
.

Recall Equation (
10.1
). The first equation in Proposition
9.1
follows by setting
\Sigma=D
. Moreover,

\Sigma(X_{t}^{0})-\Sigma(\theta^{*})=2\frac{B^{\operatorname{Eq}}}{B}\kappa e^{-t\kappa}(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}e^{-t\kappa}\kappa,

and so

\displaystyle\langle\kappa e^{-2(T-t)\kappa},\Sigma(X_{t}^{0})-\Sigma(\theta^{*})\rangle\,dt=

\displaystyle 2\frac{B^{\operatorname{Eq}}}{B}\langle\kappa e^{-2(T-t)\kappa},\kappa e^{-t\kappa}(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}e^{-t\kappa}\kappa\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{B^{\operatorname{Eq}}}{B}\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2T\kappa},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle.

Therefore, by Equation (
10.1
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{LE}(X^{\text{CC}})=

\displaystyle T\left(\frac{B^{\operatorname{Eq}}}{B}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2T\kappa},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\operatorname{LE}(X^{0})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\langle\kappa e^{-2(T-t)\kappa},\Sigma(X_{t}^{0})\rangle\,dt-\frac{1}{2}T\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2T\kappa},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{LE}(X^{\text{CC}})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\langle\kappa e^{-2(T-t)\kappa},\Sigma(\theta^{*})\rangle\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{LE}(X^{\text{CC}})+\frac{1}{2B}\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\langle\kappa^{2},\int_{0}^{T}e^{-2(T-t)\kappa}\,dt\rangle.

Finally, since
\kappa
is positive definite, we may simplify

\frac{1}{2B}\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\langle\kappa^{2},\int_{0}^{T}e^{-2(T-t)\kappa}\,dt\rangle=\frac{1}{4B}\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\langle\kappa^{2},(1_{d\times d}-e^{-2\kappa T})\kappa^{-1}\rangle=\frac{1}{4B}\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\langle\kappa,1_{d\times d}-e^{-2\kappa T}\rangle.

The following lemma is used in the proof of Theorem
22
.

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
a,b_{1},b_{2},B>0
with
b_{1}<b_{2}
and set
e_{i}=-a+\frac{b_{i}}{B}

Then,

\operatorname{sgn}(|e_{1}|-|e_{2}|)=\operatorname{sgn}\left(B-\frac{b_{1}+b_{2}}{2a}\right).

Proof 10.3
.

Note that
B\leq\frac{b_{i}}{a}
if and only if
e_{i}\geq 0
, and
B\geq\frac{b_{i}}{a}
if and only if
e_{i}\leq 0
. Moreover,

\frac{b_{1}}{a}<\frac{b_{1}+b_{2}}{2a}<\frac{b_{2}}{a}.

Thus, we have
|e_{1}|<|e_{2}|
if and only if

(a)

B\leq\frac{b_{1}}{a}
and
e_{1}<e_{2}
, or

(b)

\frac{b_{2}}{a}<B\leq\frac{b_{1}}{a}
and
e_{1}<-e_{2}
, or

(c)

\frac{b_{1}}{a}<B\leq\frac{b_{2}}{a}
and
-e_{1}<e_{2}
, or

(d)

B>\frac{b_{2}}{a}
and
-e_{1}<-e_{2}
.

Since
e_{1}<e_{2}
, case (d) can never occur and (a) is equivalent to
B\leq\frac{b_{1}}{a}
. Further, since
b_{1}<b_{2}
, (b) is also impossible.
Moreover, (c) is equivalent to

\frac{b_{1}}{a}\leq B<\frac{b_{1}+b_{2}}{2a}.

Putting (a) and (c) together yields

|e_{1}|<|e_{2}|\Leftrightarrow B<\frac{b_{1}+b_{2}}{2a}.

Finally, since

|e_{1}|=|e_{2}|\Leftrightarrow e_{1}=-e_{2}\Leftrightarrow B=\frac{b_{1}+b_{2}}{2a},

the result follows.

Proof 10.4

(Proof of Theorem
22
)
.

Set

a:=\frac{1}{2}T\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2T\kappa},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle,b:=B^{\operatorname{Eq}}T\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2T\kappa},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle,c:=\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\langle\kappa,1_{d\times d}-e^{-2\kappa T}\rangle>0.

By definition and Proposition
9.1

\operatorname{LE}(X^{\operatorname{NCC}})=-a,\quad\operatorname{LE}(X^{\operatorname{CC}})=-a+\frac{b}{B},\quad\operatorname{LE}(X^{0})=-a+\frac{b}{B}+\frac{c}{B}.

Lemma
10.2
implies

\displaystyle B<\frac{b}{2a}\Leftrightarrow|\operatorname{LE}(X^{\operatorname{NCC}})|<|\operatorname{LE}(X^{\operatorname{CC}})|,

\displaystyle\quad B>\frac{b}{2a}\Leftrightarrow|\operatorname{LE}(X^{\operatorname{NCC}})|>|\operatorname{LE}(X^{\operatorname{CC}})|,

\displaystyle B<\frac{b+c}{2a}\Leftrightarrow|\operatorname{LE}(X^{\operatorname{NCC}})|<|\operatorname{LE}(X^{0})|,

\displaystyle\quad B>\frac{b+c}{2a}\Leftrightarrow|\operatorname{LE}(X^{\operatorname{NCC}})|>|\operatorname{LE}(X^{0})|,

\displaystyle B<\frac{2b+c}{2a}\Leftrightarrow|\operatorname{LE}(X^{\operatorname{CC}})|<|\operatorname{LE}(X^{0})|,

\displaystyle\quad B>\frac{2b+c}{2a}\Leftrightarrow|\operatorname{LE}(X^{0})|>|\operatorname{LE}(X^{\operatorname{CC}})|.

Further,

B^{\operatorname{Eq}}=\frac{b}{2a},\quad\frac{c}{2a}=\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\langle\kappa,1-e^{-2T\kappa}\rangle}{4T\langle\kappa^{3}e^{-2T\kappa},(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle},\quad B^{\operatorname{GF}}=\frac{2b+c}{2a},

and so the cases (i) - (iv) are proven. Finally,

\displaystyle\operatorname{LE}(X^{\operatorname{CC}})=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow B=\frac{b}{a}=2B^{\operatorname{Eq}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{LE}(X^{\operatorname{CC}})=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow B=\frac{b+c}{a}=\frac{2b+c}{2a}+\frac{c}{2a}=B^{\operatorname{GF}}+B^{\operatorname{GF}}-2B^{\operatorname{Eq}},

proving (v) and (vi).

Remark 10.5
.

There are few additional statements one can make, adding to the list in Theorem
22
. Firstly,

(i)

X^{0}\asymp X^{\operatorname{NCC}}
, if
B=B^{\operatorname{GF}}-B^{\operatorname{Eq}}
,

(ii)

X^{0}\asymp X^{\operatorname{CC}}
, if
B=B^{\operatorname{GF}}
,

(iii)

\operatorname{LE}(X^{0})=0
, if
B=2(B^{\operatorname{GF}}-B^{\operatorname{Eq}})
.

Note however that these will almost never occur in practice because it is unlikely that
B^{\operatorname{GF}}
is an integer. That is, unless one specifically designs the problem in such a way. On the other hand, notice that
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}=1
if
\bm{x}
is Gaussian and
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}=4
if
d=1
and
\bm{x}
is exponentially distributed and so the case (ii) in Theorem
22
can realistically occur in applications.

Moreover, note that for
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}=0
we have
\Sigma(\theta)=\Sigma(\theta^{*})
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and so
X^{\operatorname{CC}}=X^{\operatorname{NCC}}
. In particular, this happens for
d=1
and if
\bm{x}
has a symmetric Rademacher distribution, since then
\Kurt x=1
(recall example
7.3
).
Thus, we are left with the cases

(i)

X^{0}\prec X^{\operatorname{NCC}}
, if
B<B^{\operatorname{GF}}
,

(ii)

X^{\operatorname{NCC}}\prec X^{0}
, if
B>B^{\operatorname{GF}}
.

10.2
Explicit Formulas for the Expected Risk of the Continuous-Time Approximations of SGD for Linear Regression

Here, we derive explicit formulas for the expected (excess) population risk for four

continuous-time approximation of SGD for linear regression. These are used in the numerical experiments to compute the continuous-time half of the weak error.
Firstly, we consider the following families of modified equations

\displaystyle dX_{t}^{0}=

\displaystyle-\kappa(X_{t}^{0}-\theta^{*})\,dt,

\displaystyle dX^{\operatorname{NCC},h}_{t}=

\displaystyle-\kappa(X_{t}^{h}-\theta^{*})\,dt+\sqrt{\frac{h}{B}}\sqrt{2B^{\operatorname{Eq}}\kappa(X^{\operatorname{NCC},h}_{t}-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\kappa+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa}\,dW_{t},

\displaystyle dX_{t}^{\operatorname{CC},h}=

\displaystyle-\kappa(X_{t}^{\operatorname{CC},h}-\theta^{*})\,dt+\sqrt{\frac{h}{B}\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa}\,dW_{t}.

\displaystyle dX_{t}^{2,h}=

\displaystyle-\kappa\left(1_{d\times d}+\kappa\frac{h}{2}\right)(X_{t}^{h}-\theta^{*})\,dt+\sqrt{\frac{h}{B}}\sqrt{2B^{\operatorname{Eq}}\kappa(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\otimes 2}\kappa+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa}\,dW_{t}.

For simplicity we set
d=1
and
B^{\operatorname{Eq}}=\frac{1}{2}(\Kurt\bm{x}-1)
.
The next Proposition gives explicit formulas for the expected
excess population risk

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}^{e}(Y_{t})]
for
Y\in\{X^{0},X^{\operatorname{NCC},h},X^{\operatorname{CC},h},X^{2,h}\}
, where
\mathcal{R}^{e}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\theta^{*})^{2}
. The actual population risk is also given by
\mathcal{R}=\kappa\mathcal{R}^{e}+\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{2}
. Note that

\mathcal{R}^{e}(\theta)-\mathcal{R}^{e}(\tilde{\theta})=\frac{1}{\kappa}(\mathcal{R}(\theta)-\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\theta})),\quad\theta,\tilde{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}.

Proposition 10.6
.

Define

\zeta^{h}=1-\frac{h}{2B}\kappa(\Kurt\bm{x}-1),\quad\xi^{h}:=\zeta^{h}+\frac{h}{2}\kappa=1+\frac{h}{2B}\kappa(B+1-\Kurt\bm{x}),\quad h\in[0,1).

Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{e}(X_{t}^{0})=

\displaystyle e^{-2\kappa t}\mathcal{R}^{e}(\theta),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}^{e}(X_{t}^{\operatorname{CC},h})]=

\displaystyle e^{-2\kappa t}\mathcal{R}^{e}(\theta)+\frac{h\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4B}(1-e^{-2\kappa t}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}^{e}(X^{\operatorname{NCC},h}_{t})]=

\displaystyle e^{-2\kappa\zeta_{h}t}\mathcal{R}^{e}(\theta)+\frac{h\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4B\zeta_{h}}(1-e^{-2\kappa\zeta_{h}t}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}^{e}(X_{t}^{2,h})]=

\displaystyle e^{-2\kappa\xi_{h}t}\mathcal{R}^{e}(\theta)+\frac{h\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4B\xi_{h}}(1-e^{-2\kappa\xi_{h}t}),

for all
h\in(0,1)
and
t\geq 0
.

Proof 10.7
.

Recall that

X_{t}^{0}=e^{-\kappa t}(\theta-\theta^{*})+\theta^{*},

and so

\mathcal{R}^{e}(X_{t}^{0})=e^{-2\kappa t}\mathcal{R}^{e}(\theta).

Further,
X^{\text{CC},h}
is an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process and so

X_{t}^{\operatorname{CC},h}=X_{t}^{0}+\sqrt{\frac{h\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{2B}}W_{1-e^{-2\kappa t}}.

Hence,

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}^{e}(X_{t}^{\operatorname{CC},h})]=e^{-2\kappa t}\mathcal{R}^{e}(\theta)+\frac{h\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4B}(1-e^{-2\kappa t}).

Now, by Itô’s formula

\displaystyle d\mathcal{R}^{e}(X^{\operatorname{NCC},h}_{t})=

\displaystyle-\kappa(X^{\operatorname{NCC},h}_{t}-\theta^{*})^{2}+\frac{h}{2B}\kappa^{2}(\Kurt\bm{x}-1)(X^{\operatorname{NCC},h}_{t}-\theta^{*})^{2}+\frac{h}{2B}\kappa\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\,dt+M_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{h}{B}\kappa^{2}(\Kurt\bm{x}-1)-2\kappa\right)\mathcal{R}^{e}(X^{\operatorname{NCC},h}_{t})+\frac{h}{2B}\kappa\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\,dt+M_{t}

where
M
is a martingale starting in
0
, a.s. Hence, by optional stopping

d\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}^{e}(X^{\operatorname{NCC},h}_{t})]=-2\kappa\zeta_{h}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}^{e}(X^{\operatorname{NCC},h}_{t})]+\frac{h}{2B}\kappa\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\,dt,

and so

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}^{e}(X^{\operatorname{NCC},h}_{t})]=e^{-2\kappa\zeta_{h}t}\mathcal{R}^{e}(\theta)+\frac{h\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4B\zeta_{h}}(1-e^{-2\kappa\zeta_{h}t}).

Similarly,

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}^{e}(X_{t}^{2,h})]=e^{-2\kappa\xi_{h}t}\mathcal{R}^{e}(\theta)+\frac{h\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4B\xi_{h}}(1-e^{-2\kappa\xi_{h}t}).

Chapter 6
SMEs for SGD without replacement

Training algorithms using epochs, that is those based on stochastic gradient descent without replacement (SGDo) are predominantly used to train machine learning models in practice. However, the mathematical theory of SGDo and related algorithms remain underexplored compared to their
“with replacement”
and
“one-pass”
counterparts. Further, there is no existing theory of modified equations for SGDo.
In this chapter, we propose a stochastic, continuous-time approximation to SGDo with additive noise based on a
Young differential equation
driven by a stochastic process we call
epoched Brownian motion
. We show its usefulness by proving the convergence of the solution of this continuous-time approximation for strongly convex objectives and the learning rate schedule
u_{t}=\frac{1}{(1+t)^{\beta}},\beta\in(0,1)
, and computing an upper bound on the asymptotic rate of convergence.

This chapter is inspired by and significantly expands on
5
.

11
Introduction

Consider a risk minimization problem
(R:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{Z}\to[0,\infty),\nu)
on a measurable space
\mathcal{Z}
. Fix an i.i.d. sequence
(\bm{z}(n))_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
in
\mathcal{Z}
with
\bm{z}(0)\sim\nu
. For now, consider one-pass SGD with a sequence of learning rates
(\eta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, given by

\chi_{n+1}=\chi_{n}-\eta_{n}\nabla R_{\bm{z}({n})}(\chi_{n}),\quad h\in(0,1),n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(41)

In order to better understand SGD several authors have proposed approximating their dynamics by the solution of an SDE. In particular, in the case of a constant learning rate (
\eta_{n}=h
),
32
propose the following family of stochastic differential equations as an approximation of (
41
)

dY_{t}^{h}=-\nabla\mathcal{R}(Y_{t}^{h})\,dt+\sqrt{h}\sigma\,dW_{t}.

Here,
\sigma
is a symmetric and positive semi-definite matrix approximating the gradient covariance in a
“region of interest”
,
W
is a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion, and
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{E}R_{z(0)}
. Time is scaled in such a way that heuristically we have
Y_{nh}^{h}\approx\chi_{n}
.
Consider now a learning rate schedule
u:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
such that
\eta_{n}=hu_{nh}
.

27
further investigated this case of a non-constant learning rate schedules, and they heuristically used the following non-homogeneous dynamics

dY_{t}^{h}=-u_{t}\nabla\mathcal{R}(Y_{t}^{h})\,dt+u_{t}\sqrt{h\Sigma(Y_{t}^{h})}\,dW_{t}.

(42)

The presence of
u
in both coefficients can be motivated as follows. By multiplying the stochastic gradients with
u
, the expected gradients are multiplied by
u
and their covariance by
u^{2}
. Thus, the diffusion coefficient - being the square root of the covariance is multiplied by
u
as well.
While high learning rates seem to promise fast convergence via the drift, they also increase the variance of the gradients. A well-chosen learning rate schedule should thus balance both effects to ensure convergence.

Theorem
17
implies that under certain regularity conditions (
42
) is a first-order SME of SGD. However, we know from Chapter
5
that, among first-order SMEs, choosing a state-dependent diffusion coefficient is not always better than a state-independent one (see in particular Theorem
22
).
Therefore, in the following we elect to work with the simpler additive noise approximation of the form

dY_{t}^{h}=-u_{t}\nabla\mathcal{R}(Y_{t}^{h})\,dt+\sqrt{h}u_{t}\sigma\,dW_{t},

(43)

in the spirit of
32
.

The Markov property of Brownian motion says that the future is independent of the past given the current state. In the approximation (
42
) this reflects the idea that all future data points of SGD are new data points, independent of those we have seen so far.

Consider now a finite i.i.d. sequence
(\bm{z}(n))_{n=0}^{N-1}
with
\bm{z}(0)\sim\nu
, and the following variant of SGD, called SGD
without replacement (with finite data)
(SGDo)

\chi_{n+1}=\chi_{n}-hu_{nh}\nabla R_{\bm{z}({\pi^{\left\lfloor n/N\right\rfloor}(n\operatorname{mod}N)})}(\chi_{n}),\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(44)

Here,
(\pi^{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is a sequence of permutations of the set
\{0,\dots,N-1\}
. Wlog we set
\pi^{0}=\operatorname{id}
.
Then the dynamics (
44
) and (
41
) coincide for
n\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
. In the following
epoch
, i.e. for
n\in\{N,\dots,2N-1\}
, we reuse the same finite sample
(\bm{z}(k))_{k=0}^{N-1}
in perhaps a different order
(\bm{z}(\pi^{1}(k)))_{k=0}^{N-1}
. We continue on like this in subsequent epochs using the sequence of permutations
(\pi^{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. In general, we allow
(\pi^{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
to be random, but independent of
(\bm{z}(n))_{n=0}^{N-1}
.

For
t\in[0,T]
with
T=Nh
, Equation (
43
) is a reasonable approximation of (
44
). However, Equation (
44
) no longer defines a Markov process for
n\geq N
on the state space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, because it cannot be written in the form
\chi_{n+1}=g(\chi_{n},Z_{n})
for some i.i.d. sequence
(Z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.
Thus, the Markov property for the driver
W
in Equation (
43
) is no longer appropriate if we try to find a continuous-time model for SGDo (for finite data).

For now, let us consider
single-shuffle
SGDo, that is we choose
3
3

3

Technically, in the literature on SGDo
“single shuffle”
means
“shuffle once”
. We assume no shuffling here because it makes no difference: the distribution of the sample is unaffected.

\pi^{j}=\operatorname{id},j\geq 1
.
Given
T>0
and a Brownian motion
W:\Omega\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, define

\hat{W}_{t}:=W_{\{t/T\}T}+\left\lfloor t/T\right\rfloor W_{T},\quad t\geq 0.

Here,
\{r\}=r-\left\lfloor r\right\rfloor
is the fractional part of
r\in\mathbb{R}
.
Note that
\hat{W}
is a Brownian motion when restricted to the interval
[0,T)
, and
\hat{W}
satisfies

\hat{W}_{t+jT}=\hat{W}_{t}+jW_{T},\qquad t\geq 0,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Note that
\hat{W}
is almost surely continuous and even locally Hölder continuous.
The increments of
\hat{W}
on
[jT,(j+1)T]
coincide with the increments of
W
on
[0,T]
(up to translating time).
We call
\hat{W}
a
single shuffle Brownian motion
with period
T
. The fact that we reuse the same Brownian path
(W_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
corresponds to using the same data points in the same order in later epochs (single-shuffle).

By replacing the driving path of the diffusion in (
43
) by single shuffle Brownian motion, we arrive at the following differential equation with additive noise

dY_{t}=-u_{t}\nabla\mathcal{R}(Y_{t})\,dt+u_{t}\sqrt{h}\sigma\,d\hat{W}_{t}.

(45)

Since
\hat{W}
is not a semimartingale we cannot interpret the term
u_{t}\,d\hat{W}_{t}
using Itô integration. Instead, we interpret it pathwise as the Young integral

\int_{0}^{t}u_{s}\,d\hat{W}_{s}=\lim_{|\mathcal{P}|\to 0}\sum_{[r,s]\in\mathcal{P}}u_{r}(\hat{W}_{s}-\hat{W}_{r}),

where the limit is taken with respect to all partitions of
[0,t]
with mesh size
|\mathcal{P}|
. The integral exists for example if
u
is Lipschitz. Thus, we understand (
45
) as Young differential equation.

More generally, we allow the driver
\hat{W}
in Equation (
45
) to be an
epoched Brownian motion
(EBM). An EBM
\hat{W}
is roughly speaking a single shuffle Brownian motion, except on
[jT,(j+1)T]
the increments of
\hat{W}
may be
“infinitesimally shuffled”
according to
\pi^{j}
(see Section
12
for a proper explanation). We can thereby encode different shuffling schemes for SGDo in the approximating equation (
45
).

Previous works on SGDo have mainly focused on comparing the convergence rates of SGD with replacement and SGDo, where empirically the latter is known to converge faster.
39
establishes lower bounds on
in expectation
convergence rates for SGDo with constant learning rates.

35
use the method of exchangeable pairs to derive non-asymptotic in expectation convergence results for general smooth, strongly convex functions.

19
focuses on the speed of in expectation and almost sure convergence for single-shuffle and random reshuffling SGDo. The later algorithm uses an i.i.d. sequence
(\pi^{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of permutations where
\pi^{0}
uniformly distributed. Using martingale techniques, they prove convergence results for learning rates decaying like the schedule
u_{t}=\frac{1}{(1+t)^{\beta}},t\geq 0
with
\beta\in(1/2,1]
, and strongly convex
\mathcal{R}
.

To demonstrate the usefulness of our heuristic SME approximation (
45
), we study the almost sure convergence of the solution of (
45
) for Lipschitz and strongly convex
\mathcal{R}
with Hölder continuous Hessian matrix, and with
u_{t}=\frac{1}{(1+ct)^{\beta}},t\geq 0
with
\beta\in(0,1)
and
c>0
. Here, we leave out the case
\beta=1
for brevity reasons. On the other hand, we cover the case
\beta\in(0,1/2]
as well since our main strategy uses the Young-Lóeve inequality instead of martingale techniques.
We show convergence to a random point depending on
\hat{W}_{T}
and compute an asymptotic upper bound on the convergence speed. Our result for the single shuffle cases matches previous results by
19
. In the case of general random permutations, our results suggest markedly better upper bounds than the best results known for random reshuffling. Note that, heuristically speaking,
\hat{W}_{T}
encodes information about the random sample
(\bm{z}(n))_{n=1}^{N}
including the sample size
N
, which is why the limit depends on it. In the setting of linear regression, we identify the random limit with the (random) OLS estimator which further substantiates the legitimacy of our approximation.

12
SMEs driven by epoched Brownian motions

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{\Omega},\mathbb{P})
be a complete probability space,
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
T>0
.
Recall that
\hat{W}
is a single shuffle Brownian motion (of period
T
) if there exists a Brownian motion
W:\Omega\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
with

\hat{W}_{t}:=W_{\{t/T\}T}+\left\lfloor t/T\right\rfloor W_{T},\quad t\geq 0.

Note that given a single shuffle Brownian motion
\hat{W}
we can define a
Brownian bridge

B:\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
from
0
to
0
by setting

B_{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}(\hat{W}_{tT}-t\hat{W}_{T}),\quad t\in[0,1].

Then,

\hat{W}_{t}=\sqrt{T}B_{\{t/T\}}+\frac{t}{\sqrt{T}}V,\quad t\geq 0.

with
V:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\hat{W}_{T}
a standard Gaussian.

More generally, we may replace the single Brownian bridge
B
with a sequence of bridges
(B^{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
, one for each epoch. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 22
.

A stochastic process
X:\Omega\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called an
epoched Brownian bridge
if there exists a jointly Gaussian
4
4

4

Jointly Gaussian family means
(B^{j_{1}}_{t_{1}},\dots,B^{j_{m}}_{t_{m}})
is Gaussian for all
j_{1},\dots,j_{m}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\in[0,1]
.
family
(B^{j}:\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of Brownian bridges from
0
to
0
, such that

X_{t}=B^{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor}_{\{t\}},\quad t\geq 0.

A stochastic process
\hat{W}:\Omega\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called an
epoched Brownian motion
of period
T>0
if there exists an epoched Brownian bridge
X
and a random variable
V\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1_{d\times d})
independent of
X
, such that

\hat{W}_{t}=\sqrt{T}X_{t/T}+\frac{t}{\sqrt{T}}V,\quad t\geq 0.

We highlight the following examples:

(a)

Single shuffle (SS):
B^{0}=B^{1}=\dots
,

(b)

Random reshuffling (RR):
(B^{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
are independent,

(c)

Flip-flop single shuffle:
B^{0}=B^{2}=\dots
, and
B^{j+1}_{t}=-B^{j}_{1-t},t\in[0,1]
,

(d)

Flip-flop random reshuffling:
(B^{2j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
are independent,
B^{j+1}_{t}=-B^{j}_{1-t},t\in[0,1]
.

In our framework, the epoched Brownian motion
\hat{W}
corresponds to the versions of SGDo with the same name. That is, they correspond to the following shuffling schemes for SGDo for large samples sizes
N
:

(a)

Single shuffle (SS):
\pi^{j}=\operatorname{id}_{N},j\in\mathbb{N}
,

(b)

Random reshuffling (RR):
(\pi^{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
are independent with
\pi^{j}
uniformly distributed on the symmetric group of order
N
,

(c)

Flip-flop single shuffle:
\pi^{2j}=\operatorname{id}_{N},\pi^{2j+1}=\tau,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, where
\tau(n)=N-n+1
is the
reversal
permutation
5
5

5

Not to be confused with the inverse of a permutation.
,

(d)

Flip-flop random reshuffling:
(\pi^{2j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
are independent with
\pi^{j}
uniformly distributed on the symmetric group of order
N
, and
\pi^{2j+1}=\tau\circ\pi^{2j},j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

We do not claim that every epoched Brownian motion or bridge correspond to a shuffling scheme for SGDo. Instead, a
one-dimensional
epoched Brownian motion (or bridge) given by a family of Brownian bridges
(B^{n}:\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
corresponds to a shuffling scheme for SGDo for large sample sizes
N
if there exists a measure
\mu
on
[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
with uniform marginals, such that

\mathbb{E}[B_{s}^{i}B_{t}^{j}]=C^{ij}(s,t)-st,\quad i\neq j\in\mathbb{N},s,t\in[0,1],

where

C^{ij}(s,t)=\mu([0,1]\times\dots\times[0,1]\times\overbrace{[0,s]}^{i}\times[0,1]\times\dots\times[0,1]\times\overbrace{[0,t]}^{j}\times[0,1]\times\dots),\quad i\neq j

and
C^{ii}(s,t)=s\wedge t
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Note that the functions
C^{ij}
are
2
-copulas. A
d
-dimensional epoched Brownian bridge corresponding to a shuffling scheme consists of
d
independent copies of such a one-dimensional process (the same measure is used for all dimensions).

The reason we claim correspondence to shuffling schemes, provided such a measure
\mu
exists, is that these processes arise as scaling limits of the joint distributions of random walks that have the same increments, up to a (random) permutation, see Chapter
7
.

All our previous examples satisfy this condition, with

(a)

Single Shuffle (SS):
C^{ij}(s,t)=s\wedge t
,

(b)

Random reshuffling (RR):
C^{ij}(s,t)=st
,

(c)

Flip-flop single shuffle:

C^{ij}(s,t)=\begin{cases}s\wedge t,&i,j\text{ are both odd or even},\\
(s+t-1)\vee 0,&\text{else},\end{cases}

(d)

Flip-flop random reshuffling:

C^{ij}(s,t)=\begin{cases}(s+t-1)\vee 0,&i\text{ is even and }i+1=j,\\
st,&\text{else},\end{cases}

for
i\neq j
.

The first formula is simply stating that the covariance of a single Brownian bridge is given by

\operatorname{Cov}(B_{s},B_{t})=s\wedge t-st=s(1-t)\wedge t(1-s),\quad s,t\in[0,1].

The second formula just says that independent Brownian bridges have covariance
0
. To show (c) and (d) it remains the consider a Brownian bridge
B
and calculate

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}(B_{s},-B_{1-t})=

\displaystyle-(s\wedge(1-t))+s(1-t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-s)\vee(t-1)+s-st

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(s+t-1)\vee 0-st,\quad s,t\in[0,1].

Since most of our results do not depend on the existence of such a measure
\mu
we will not assume such a covariance structure in general.

13
Main result

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\lambda>0
. We say a function
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
is
\lambda
-strongly convex
if it satisfies any of the following equivalent properties:

•

\langle\nabla\mathcal{R}(x)-\nabla\mathcal{R}(y),x-y\rangle\geq\lambda|x-y|^{2},\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

•

\mathcal{R}(y)\geq\mathcal{R}(x)+\langle\nabla\mathcal{R}(x),y-x\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\lambda|x-y|^{2},\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

•

\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}(x)-\lambda 1_{d\times d}
is a positive semi-definite matrix, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Let
L>0
. We say
\mathcal{R}
is
L
-smooth
if
\nabla\mathcal{R}
is Lipschitz, with
\|\nabla\mathcal{R}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\leq L
.
Our main (mathematical) result is the following.

Theorem 22
.

Let
\beta\in(0,1)
,
c>0
,
L,\lambda>0
and
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
be
\lambda
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth such that
\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}
is Hölder continuous.
Let
Y
be the solution to the Young differential equation

dY_{t}=-\frac{1}{(1+ct)^{\beta}}\nabla\mathcal{R}(Y_{t})\,dt+\frac{1}{(1+ct)^{\beta}}\sigma\,d\hat{W}_{t},

(46)

driven by an epoched Brownian motion
\hat{W}
with period
T
. Then

\left|Y_{t}-(\nabla\mathcal{R})^{-1}(T^{-1}\sigma\hat{W}_{T})\right|\leq T^{1/2-\beta}|\sigma|\left(4.7\frac{L}{\lambda}+1.2\right)c^{-\beta}\frac{\sqrt{\log t}}{t^{\beta}}+o\left(\sqrt{\log t}\cdot t^{-\beta}\right),t\to\infty,\quad a.s.

Theorem
22
may give the impression that its optimal to let
\beta\to 1
-. After all, that choice gives us the fastest asymptotic rate of convergence. However, in actuality the constant hidden in
o(\sqrt{\log t}\cdot t^{-\beta})
diverges to
\infty
, as
\beta\to 1
. Therefore, we cannot conclude that
\beta\to 1
is optimal. In fact, in practice setting
\beta=1
makes the learning rates decay much too fast.

In certain situations we can get a better decay rate compared to Theorem
22
.
The following theorem applies to all epoched Brownian motions which have only finitely many different epochs over their entire time horizon. For example, this is the case for single shuffle Brownian motion, which only has a single repeated epoch.

Theorem 22
.

Let
\beta\in(0,1)
,
c>0
,
L,\lambda>0
and
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
be
\lambda
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth, such that
\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}
is Hölder continuous.
Let
Y
be the solution to the Young differential equation

dY_{t}=-\frac{1}{(1+ct)^{\beta}}\nabla\mathcal{R}(Y_{t})\,dt+\frac{1}{(1+ct)^{\beta}}\sigma\,d\hat{W}_{t},

(47)

driven by an epoched Brownian motion
\hat{W}
with period
T
. Suppose further there exists a number
J\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
\mathcal{I}:=\{(\hat{W}_{(j+t)T}-\hat{W}_{jT})_{t\in[0,1]}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}|
satisfies
|\mathcal{I}|=J
, almost surely. Then, for all
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
,

\left|Y_{t}-(\nabla\mathcal{R})^{-1}(T^{-1}\sigma\hat{W}_{T})\right|\leq C_{\alpha}T^{1/2-\beta}|\sigma|\left(\frac{1}{1-2^{-\alpha}}\frac{L}{\lambda}+1\right)\frac{1}{t^{\beta}}+o\left(C_{\alpha}t^{-\beta}\right),t\to\infty,\quad a.s.

where
C_{\alpha}=\max_{w\in\mathcal{I}}\|w\|_{\alpha}
.

Note that the only random factor in
o(C_{\alpha}t^{-\beta})
is
C_{\alpha}
.

As an example, consider SGDo applied to linear regression, which corresponds to the Young differential equation

dY_{t}=-\frac{1}{(1+t)^{\beta}}\kappa(Y_{t}-\theta^{*})\,dt+\frac{1}{(1+t)^{\beta}}\sqrt{h\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa}\,d\hat{W}_{t}.

Here,
\hat{W}
has period
T=Nh
where
N
is the sample size and
h
the maximal learning rate. We implicitly assume we are in the underparameterized regime
N\gg d
.

Then

\displaystyle(\nabla\mathcal{R})^{-1}(T^{-1}\sigma\hat{W}_{T})=

\displaystyle\theta^{*}+\kappa^{-1}((Nh)^{-1/2}\sqrt{h\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\kappa}T^{-1/2}\hat{W}_{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\theta^{*}+\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{N}}\kappa^{-1/2}(T^{-1/2}\hat{W}_{T})

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\left(\theta^{*},\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{N}\kappa^{-1}\right),

and Theorem
22
implies

\displaystyle\left|Y_{t}-\left(\theta^{*}+\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{N}}\kappa^{-1/2}(T^{-1/2}\hat{W}_{T})\right)\right|\leq

\displaystyle(Nh)^{1/2-\beta}\sqrt{h}\sigma_{\varepsilon}|\sqrt{\kappa}|\left(4.7\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\kappa)}{\lambda_{\min}(\kappa)}+1.2\right)c^{-\beta}\frac{\sqrt{\log t}}{t^{\beta}}

\displaystyle+o\left(\sqrt{\log t}\cdot t^{-\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle N^{1/2-\beta}dh^{1-\beta}\sigma_{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(\kappa)}\left(4.7\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\kappa)}{\lambda_{\min}(\kappa)}+1.2\right)\frac{\sqrt{\log t}}{t^{\beta}}

\displaystyle+o\left(\sqrt{\log t}\cdot t^{-\beta}\right),

as
t\to\infty
, almost surely. The limit
Y_{\infty}:=\theta^{*}+\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{N}}\kappa^{-1/2}T^{-1/2}\hat{W}_{T}
of
Y
has the same mean and covariance matrix as the OLS estimator

\hat{\theta}=\left(\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bm{x}_{n}\bm{x}_{n}^{\intercal}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bm{x}_{n}\bm{y}_{n}\right),

if
(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n})_{n=1}^{N}
is a finite i.i.d. sample with
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{y}_{0})\sim\nu
, and
\nu
is the corresponding population.
Since
\hat{W}
is independent of
(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
we
do not
have
\hat{\theta}=Y_{\infty}
, even if
\hat{\theta}
was Gaussian. Nevertheless, this result suggests that spiritually
Y_{\infty}
represents the OLS estimator in our model in the case of linear regression.

The factor
T^{1/2-\beta}
(or
N^{1/2-\beta}
after setting
T=Nh
) in the convergence speed may be surprising. It can be heuristically explained as follows:
Set
u_{t}=\frac{1}{(1+ct)^{\beta}},t\geq 0
. The noise accumulated in epoch
j
is given by

\int_{jT}^{(j+1)T}u_{t}\sigma\,d\hat{W}_{t}\approx(cjT)^{-\beta}\sigma(\hat{W}_{(j+1)T}-\hat{W}_{jT})=T^{1/2-\beta}(jc)^{-\beta}\sigma Z,

where

Z=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}(\hat{W}_{(j+1)T}-\hat{W}_{jT})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1_{d\times d}).

If
\beta>1/2
, then
u
decays faster than the noise accumulates. In this case the accumulated noise vanishes, as
T\to\infty
, since increasing
T
means we are effectively averaging over more i.i.d. random variables per epoch. On the other hand, if
\beta<1/2
, then
u
decays too slowly to overcome the noise accumulation. More steps per epoch means more accumulation, so the accumulated noise diverges to infinity, as
T\to\infty
. Finally, at
\beta=1/2
both effects (decay and noise accumulation) are balanced.

These different regimes implicitly also exist in other works on stochastic gradient descent (with or without replacement). In particular, usually only the case
\beta>1/2
is covered (see the end of the following paragraph).

Comparison with existing results

Our main theorem complements findings by
19
.
They proved that single shuffle SGDo satisfies

|\chi_{k}-\hat{\theta}|\leq\frac{h|\mu(\pi^{1})|}{\lambda}\frac{1}{k^{\beta}}+o(k^{-\beta}),a.s.\quad k\to\infty,

for
\beta\in(1/2,1)
.
Here,
\chi
is given by Equation
44
with
\eta_{k}=hk^{-\beta}
and
\pi^{1}=\pi^{j},j\in\mathbb{N}
. Further,
\mathcal{R}
is given as a sum of
N
quadratic forms, is
\lambda
-strongly convex and has its minimum at
\hat{\theta}
. Moreover,
\mu(\pi)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a sum of
\frac{1}{2}N(N-1)
terms depending on
\mathcal{R}
and the permutation
\pi
. In general,
|\mu(\pi)|
can grow with rate
O(N^{2})
, as
N\to\infty
.
In contrast, Theorem
22
suggests a rate of

\tilde{C}N^{1/2-\beta}k^{-\beta}+o(k^{-\beta}),a.s.\quad k\to\infty.

where
\tilde{C}
is independent of
N
.
They also provide a crude bound for the random reshuffling case:

|\chi_{k}-\hat{\theta}|\leq\frac{h\sup_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{N}}|\mu(\pi)|}{\lambda}\frac{1}{k^{\beta}}+o(k^{-\beta}),a.s.\quad k\to\infty,

where
\mathcal{S}_{N}
is the symmetric group of degree
N
.
However, in the worst case
\sup_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{N}}|\mu(\pi)|=O(N^{2}N!)
, as
N\to\infty
, making this result not very useful for moderately large
N
, say
6
6

6

The observable universe is estimated to have less than
60!
particles.

N>100
. Naturally, they mention that the constant
\sup_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{N}}|\mu(\pi)|
is pessimistic.
Our Theorem
22
suggests a rate of

\tilde{C}N^{1/2-\beta}\frac{\sqrt{\log k}}{k^{\beta}}+o(\sqrt{\log k}\cdot k^{-\beta}),a.s.\quad k\to\infty,

for the convergence of SGDo on strongly convex objectives using any shuffling scheme.
Thus, Theorem
22
suggests good almost sure convergence rates for SGDo even for large sample sizes
N
.

Finally, note the restriction
\beta>1/2
imposed by
19
. It stems from the application of martingale techniques which require learning rates to be square summable. Indeed,

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{n^{\beta}}\right)^{2}<\infty\text{ if and only if }\beta>1/2.

Since we do not use any martingale techniques, this barrier only appears implicitly in our main results as the convergence rate factor
T^{1/2-\beta}
.

14
Properties of (epoched) Brownian bridges

In the following we will mostly work with epoched Brownian
bridges
. By the definition they concatenations of Brownian bridges. Recall, that a Brownian bridge is
(1/2-)
-Hölder continuous, that is
(1/2-\varepsilon)
-Hölder continuous for every
\varepsilon>0
. Together with the following lemma, this implies that epoched Brownian bridges are locally
(1/2-)
-Hölder continuous.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
. In this chapter we denote by
\|\cdot\|_{\alpha}
the
\alpha
-Hölder seminorm given by

\|f\|_{\alpha}=\sup_{s,t\in I}\frac{\|f(t)-f(s)\|_{E}}{|t-s|^{\alpha}},

where
f:I\to E
for
E=(\mathbb{R}^{d},|\cdot|)
or
7
7

7

In contrast to Chapter
3
, here we define Hölder norms with respect to the spectral norm for matrices, not the Frobenius norm.

E=(\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},\|\cdot\|_{\operatorname{op}})
and some interval
I
. Here,

\|A\|_{\operatorname{op}}:=\sup_{|x|=1}|Ax|=\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(A^{\intercal}A)}.

denotes the
spectral norm
of a square matrix
A
. We also write
\|f\|_{\alpha;I}=\|f|_{I}\|_{\alpha}
when
f
is defined on a set containing
I
. In the case
\alpha=1
we prefer writing
\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}
and
\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip};I}
.

Lemma 23
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
f,g:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\in\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}
be functions with
f(1)=g(0)
. Then the concatenation

f\ast g:[0,2]\to\mathbb{R}^{d},t\mapsto f(t)1_{[0,1]}(t)+g(t-1)1_{(1,2]}(t)

satisfies
f\ast g\in\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}
with
\|f\ast g\|_{\alpha}\leq 2^{1-\alpha}(\|f\|_{\alpha}\vee\|g\|_{\alpha})
.

Proof 14.1
.

It suffices to check the Hölder condition for
s<1<t
. In this case

\displaystyle|f\ast g(t)-f\ast g(s)|\leq

\displaystyle|f\ast g(t)-f\ast g(1)|+|f\ast g(1)-f\ast g(s)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|g(t-1)-g(0)|+|f(1)-f(s)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\|f\|_{\alpha}\vee\|g\|_{\alpha})(|t-1|^{\alpha}+|1-s|^{\alpha})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{1-\alpha}(\|f\|_{\alpha}\vee\|g\|_{\alpha})(|t-1|+|1-s|)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{1-\alpha}(\|f\|_{\alpha}\vee\|g\|_{\alpha})|t-s|^{\alpha},

since
|t-1|+|1-s|=t-1+1-s
.

Lemma 24

(Borell-TIS)
.

Let
D
be a topological space and
Q:\Omega\times D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be Gaussian random field, which is almost surely bounded on
D
.
Define
m=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in D}|Q_{t}|\right]
and
\sigma^{2}=\sup_{t\in D}\lambda_{\max}(\operatorname{Cov}(Q_{t}))
.
Then

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in D}|Q_{t}|>x\right)\leq e^{-\frac{(x-m)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}},\quad x>m.

Proof 14.2
.

We write
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|v|=1\}
. Note that

|Q_{t}|=\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\langle Q_{t},v\rangle,

since
|\langle Q_{t},v\rangle|\leq|Q_{t}||v|=|Q_{t}|
for
v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and because we can pick
v=Q_{t}/|Q_{t}|
.
Define

\tilde{Q}:\Omega\times D\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R},(\omega,t,v)\mapsto\langle Q_{t}(\omega),v\rangle.

Then
\tilde{Q}
is again a Gaussian random field and almost surely bounded. We have

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(t,v)\in D\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\tilde{Q}_{t,v}\right]=m.

Moreover, we have
\operatorname{Var}(\langle Q_{t},v\rangle)=v^{\intercal}\operatorname{Cov}(Q_{t})v
, and so

\sup_{(t,v)\in D\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\operatorname{Var}(\langle Q_{t},v\rangle)=\sup_{t\in D}\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}v^{\intercal}\operatorname{Cov}(Q_{t})v=\sup_{t\in D}\lambda_{\max}(\operatorname{Cov}(Q_{t}))=\sigma^{2}.

The penultimate equality follows because we are maximizing the Rayleigh quotient of
\operatorname{Cov}(Q_{t})
.
Now, using the standard Borell-TIS inequality
(see
2
, Theorem 2.1.1)
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{(t,v)\in D\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\tilde{Q}_{t,v}-m>x\right)\leq e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}},\quad x>0,

or equivalently

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in D}|Q_{t}|>x\right)\leq e^{-\frac{(x-m)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}},\quad x>m.

Lemma 25
.

Let
g:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
and
Z
be a non-negative random variable. Then

\mathbb{E}g(Z)=g(0)+\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{\prime}(x)\mathbb{P}(Z>x)\,dx.

Proof 14.3
.

We have

g(z)=g(0)+\int_{0}^{z}g^{\prime}(x)\,dx,

and so

\mathbb{E}g(Z)=g(0)+\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{Z}g^{\prime}(x)dx\right]=g(0)+\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{\prime}(x)\mathbb{P}(Z>x)\,dx.

Lemma 26
.

Let
B:\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a Brownian Bridge. Then

\mathbb{E}[e^{a\|B\|_{\alpha}^{2}}]<\infty

for all
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
and
a\in(0,\frac{1}{2(1-b)b^{1-2\alpha}})
, where
b=\frac{1-2\alpha}{2-2\alpha}
.

Proof 14.4
.

Define

Q_{s,t}=\begin{cases}\frac{B_{t}-B_{s}}{|t-s|^{\alpha}},&s\neq t,\\
0,&s=t,\end{cases}

for all
s,t\in[0,1]
, and write
\hat{Q}:=\sup_{s,t\in[0,1]}Q_{s,t}
.
Then
Q
is a Gaussian random field
\Omega\times[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\sup_{s,t\in[0,1]}|Q_{s,t}|=\|B\|_{\alpha}
. Thus, by Lemma
24

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|B\|_{\alpha}>x)\leq e^{-\frac{(x-m)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}},\quad x>m:=\mathbb{E}\|B\|_{\alpha},

where
\sigma^{2}:=\sup_{s,t\in[0,1]}\lambda_{\max}(\operatorname{Cov}Q_{s,t})
. Because the components of
B
are independent, Brownian bridges have stationary increments and using the covariance formula for a one-dimensional Brownian bridge we have

\lambda_{\max}(\operatorname{Cov}(B_{t}-B_{s}))=\operatorname{Var}(B_{t}^{1}-B_{s}^{1})=\operatorname{Var}(B_{t-s}^{1})=|t-s|(1-|t-s|),\quad s,t\in[0,1].

Thus,

\lambda_{\max}(\operatorname{Cov}Q_{s,t})=\begin{cases}\frac{|t-s|(1-|t-s|)}{|t-s|^{2\alpha}},&s\neq t,\\
0,&s=t\end{cases}=f(|t-s|),\quad s,t\in[0,1],

where
f(b)=(1-b)b^{1-2\alpha}
. The function
f
attains its maximum at
b^{*}:=\frac{1-2\alpha}{2-2\alpha}
. Hence
\sigma^{2}=f(b^{*})
.
Let
a>0
. Then Lemma
25
implies

\mathbb{E}[e^{a\|B\|_{\alpha}^{2}}]=1+\int_{0}^{\infty}2axe^{ax^{2}}\mathbb{P}(\|B\|_{\alpha}>x)\,dx.

Estimating the tail of the integral, we have

\int_{m}^{\infty}2axe^{ax^{2}}\mathbb{P}(\|B\|_{\alpha}>x)\,dx\leq\int_{m}^{\infty}2axe^{ax^{2}}e^{-\frac{(x-m)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}\,dx.

Since

ax^{2}-\frac{(x-m)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}=\left(a-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)x^{2}+\frac{m}{\sigma^{2}}x-\frac{m^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}

the integral converges if
a<\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}=\frac{1}{2f(b^{*})}
.

The following lemma gives us one factor in the decay rate of Theorem
22
.

Lemma 27
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
,
a\in(0,\frac{1}{2(1-b)b^{1-2\alpha}})
, where
b=\frac{1-2\alpha}{2-2\alpha}
, and
(B^{j})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a family of Brownian bridges.
Then

\max_{j\leq n}\|B^{j}\|_{\alpha}\leq a^{-1/2}\sqrt{\log n},

for large
n\in\mathbb{N}
, almost surely.

Proof 14.5
.

We use Lemma
26
.
By Markov’s inequality

\mathbb{P}(\|B\|_{\alpha}\geq x)=\mathbb{P}(e^{a\|B\|_{\alpha}^{2}}\geq e^{ax^{2}})\leq\mathbb{E}[e^{a\|B\|_{\alpha}^{2}}]e^{-ax^{2}},

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
Define
Z_{j}=\|B^{j}\|_{\alpha},j\in\mathbb{N}
, and
Z_{n}^{*}=\max(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n})
.
Then

\mathbb{P}(Z_{n}^{*}>x)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}(Z_{j}>x)\lesssim ne^{-ax^{2}},

uniformly over
x
and
n
. For any
\varepsilon>0
we thus have

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(Z_{2^{j}}^{*}>\sqrt{\frac{1+\varepsilon}{c}\log 2^{j}})\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j\varepsilon}<\infty.

By Borel-Cantelli

\mathbb{P}\left(\limsup_{n\to\infty}\{Z_{n}^{*}>\sqrt{\frac{1+\varepsilon}{a}\log n}\}\right)=0,

that is

\max_{j\leq n}\|B^{j}\|_{\alpha}=Z_{n}^{*}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1+\varepsilon}{a}\log n},

for large
n\in\mathbb{N}
, almost surely. Finally, by picking a slightly smaller
a
we can leave out the
+\varepsilon
. However, since we started with an arbitrary
a<\frac{1}{2(1-b)b^{1-2\alpha}}
we have

\max_{j\leq n}\|B^{j}\|_{\alpha}\leq a^{-1/2}\sqrt{\log n},

for large
n\in\mathbb{N}
, almost surely, for all
a\in(0,\frac{1}{2(1-b)b^{1-2\alpha}})
.

15
Young differential equations driven by epoched noise

In this section we study the properties of Young differential equations with state-independent noise term, specifically driven by an epoched bridge
X
. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. We call
X:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
an
epoched bridge
if
X
is locally Hölder continuous and
X_{n}=0,n\in\mathbb{N}
. None of the arguments in this section directly depend on
X
being an epoched
Brownian
bridge
8
8

8

For example, all arguments here apply to
X_{t}=\sin(\pi t)
.
. Hence, we work without this specific assumption.

We consider Young differential equations of the form

dY_{t}=f_{t}(Y_{t})\,dt+\sigma_{t}\,dX_{t},\quad t\geq 0,Y_{0}\in\mathbb{R},

with
f_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\sigma_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
,
which is strictly speaking a different way of writing the integral equation

Y_{t}=Y_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}f_{s}(Y_{s})\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{s}\,dX_{s},\quad t\geq 0.

(48)

Here,

\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{s}\,dX_{s}=\lim_{|\mathcal{P}|\to 0}\sum_{[r,s]\in\mathcal{P}}\sigma_{r}X_{r,s},

where the limit is taken with respect to all partitions of
[0,t]
with mesh size
|\mathcal{P}|
, and
X_{r,s}=X_{s}-X_{r}
