. This is the
Young integral
. If
X\in\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}([0,T])
and
\sigma\in\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta}([0,T])
with
\alpha+\beta>1
, then the Young integral is guaranteed to exist (see Proposition
28
).

To give an idea what is so special about (epoched) bridges consider the Young-Lóeve inequality.

Proposition 28

(Young-Lóeve)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1]
with
\alpha+\beta>1
. Given
X\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\alpha}_{\operatorname{loc}}
and
\sigma\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\beta}_{\operatorname{loc}}
, the Young integral
\int_{s}^{t}\sigma_{u}\,dX_{u}
exists, and we have

\left|\int_{s}^{t}\sigma_{u}\,dX_{u}-\sigma_{s}X_{s,t}\right|\leq\frac{(t-s)^{\alpha+\beta}}{2^{1-(\alpha+\beta)}}\|X\|_{\alpha;[s,t]}\|\sigma\|_{\beta;[s,t]},\quad 0\leq s\leq t.

Further,
\int_{s}^{\cdot}\sigma_{u}\,dX_{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\alpha}_{\operatorname{loc}}
.

Proof 15.1
.

See
18
and note that any
\alpha
-Hölder continuous function
X
on
[s,t]
(even if matrix-valued) has finite
1/\alpha
-variation
\|X\|_{1/\alpha\operatorname{-var}}
, with

\|X\|_{1/\alpha\operatorname{-var}}\leq(t-s)^{\alpha}\|X\|_{\alpha}.

Note that for any epoched bridge
X
we have
X_{n,n+1}=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, so in this case Proposition
28
implies

\left|\int_{n}^{n+1}\sigma_{s}\,dX_{s}\right|\leq\frac{1}{2^{1-(\alpha+\beta)}}\|X\|_{\alpha;[n,n+1]}\|\sigma\|_{\beta;[n,n+1]},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

(49)

This is a crucial estimate in our convergence arguments (see the proof of Proposition
35
).

15.1
Existence and Uniqueness

Our first aim is to show existence and uniqueness of a global solution
Y
to (
48
).

Proposition 29
.

Suppose we are given the following.

•

\alpha,\beta\in(0,1]
with
\alpha+\beta>1
,

•

X:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\alpha}_{\operatorname{loc}}
,

•

\sigma:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\beta}_{\operatorname{loc}}
,

•

f:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is (jointly) measurable, such that

(a)

f_{t}(\cdot)\in\operatorname{Lip}
, uniformly in
t\geq 0
,

(b)

f_{\cdot}(0)\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
.

Then there exists a unique solution
Y:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
to the Young differential equation

dY_{t}=f_{t}(Y_{t})\,dt+\sigma_{t}\,dX_{t},\quad t\geq 0,Y_{0}=y,

(50)

and it satisfies
Y\in\mathcal{C}^{0,(\alpha\wedge\beta)^{-}}_{\operatorname{loc}}([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof 15.2
.

Let
T>0,\gamma\in(0,\alpha\wedge\beta)
and define

E=\{Y\in\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{\gamma}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d}):Y_{0}=y\}.

This is a complete metric space when equipped with
d(Y,\tilde{Y})=\|Y-\tilde{Y}\|_{\gamma}
.
Define the map
\Phi:E\to E
by

(\Phi Y)_{t}=y_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}f_{s}(Y_{s})\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{s}\,dX_{s}.

Note that the latter summand is a proper Young integral, since
\alpha+\beta>1
.
We have

|f_{s}(Y_{s})|\leq|f_{s}(0)|+|f_{s}(Y_{s})-f_{s}(0)|\leq|f_{s}(0)|+\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}|Y_{s}|,

which is locally integrable in
s
. Thus,
\int_{0}^{\cdot}f_{s}(Y_{s})\,ds\in\operatorname{Lip}([0,T])
.
Further,
(\Phi Y)_{0}=y_{0}
and
\int_{0}^{\cdot}\sigma_{s}\,dX_{s}\in\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}([0,T])\subseteq\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{\gamma}([0,T])
by Proposition
28
. Hence,
\Phi
is well-defined.
For
s,t\in[0,T]
we estimate

\displaystyle|\Phi Y_{s,t}-\Phi\tilde{Y}_{s,t}|\leq

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}|f_{r}(Y_{r})-f_{r}(\tilde{Y}_{r})|\,dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\int_{s}^{t}|Y_{r}-\tilde{Y}_{r}|\,dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\|Y-\tilde{Y}\|_{\gamma}\int_{s}^{t}(r-s)^{\gamma}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\gamma}\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\|Y-\tilde{Y}\|_{\gamma}(t-s)^{1+\gamma}.

Thus,

|\Phi Y_{s,t}-\Phi\tilde{Y}_{s,t}|(t-s)^{-\gamma}\leq\frac{T}{1+\gamma}\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\|Y-\tilde{Y}\|_{\gamma},\quad s,t\in[0,T],

i.e.

\|\Phi Y-\Phi\tilde{Y}\|_{\gamma}\leq\frac{T}{1+\gamma}\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\|Y-\tilde{Y}\|_{\gamma},

or, in other words,
\Phi
is Lipschitz with constant bounded by
\frac{T}{1+\gamma}\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}
.
By picking
T=\frac{1+\gamma}{2\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}}
we get
\|\Phi\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
. In particular,
\Phi
is a contraction and has a fixed point
Y\in E
, using the Banach fixed-point theorem. Being a fixed point means it is a solution of (
50
) on
[0,T]
.
If a solution
Y
of (
50
) exists on
[0,nT]
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then by applying the same argument with

E=\{\tilde{Y}\in\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{\gamma}([nT,(n+1)T],\mathbb{R}^{d}):\tilde{Y}_{nT}=Y_{nT}\}

extends the solution
Y
to
[0,(n+1)T]
. Thus, a solution
Y
exists on
[0,\infty)
.

If there are two solutions
Y,\tilde{Y}
on some interval
[0,T]
, then

|Y_{t}-\tilde{Y}_{t}|\leq\int_{0}^{t}|f_{s}(Y_{s})-f_{s}(\tilde{Y}_{s})|\leq\|f\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}\int_{0}^{t}|Y_{s}-\tilde{Y}_{s}|\,ds,

and then Grönwalls inequality implies
Y_{t}=\tilde{Y}_{t}
, for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Proposition 30
.

Suppose we are given the following.

•

\alpha,\beta\in(0,1]
with
\alpha+\beta>1
,

•

X:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\alpha}_{\operatorname{loc}}
,

•

\sigma:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\beta}_{\operatorname{loc}}
,

•

A:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}\cap L^{\infty}
,

•

b:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
.

Let
\varphi
be the unique solution to the linear matrix integral equation

\varphi_{t}=1_{d\times d}+\int_{0}^{t}A_{s}\varphi_{s}\,ds.

(51)

Then the unique solution
Y:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
to the Young differential equation

dY_{t}=A_{t}Y_{t}+b_{t}\,dt+\sigma_{t}\,dX_{t},Y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(52)

is given by

Y_{t}=\varphi_{t}\left(Y_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\varphi_{s}^{-1}b_{s}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\varphi_{s}^{-1}\sigma_{s}\,dX_{s}\right),\quad t\geq 0.

Proof 15.3
.

Define

Z_{t}=Y_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\varphi_{s}^{-1}b_{s}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\varphi_{s}^{-1}\sigma_{s}\,dX_{s},\quad t\geq 0.

Note that
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{0,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
. Thus, the product formula (see
17
Exercise 7.4) implies

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}Z_{t}=

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}Z_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}(d\varphi_{s})Z_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\varphi_{s}\,dZ_{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}Z_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}A_{s}\varphi_{s}Z_{s}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}b_{s}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{s}dX_{s}.

Hence,
Y=\varphi Z
is a solution to (
48
). Uniqueness follows from Proposition
29
.

We can transform our main equation (
46
) into the simpler form (see Lemma
39
for details)

dY_{t}=-\tilde{u}_{t}\nabla\tilde{\mathcal{R}}(Y_{t})\,dt+\tilde{u}_{t}dX_{t},

Here,
X
is an epoched Brownian bridge,
\tilde{u}_{t}=(1+tT)^{-\beta}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
is a random function satisfying the same conditions as
\mathcal{R}
in Theorem
22
, almost surely, except its global minimum is at
0
.
Thus, we will work mainly with equations of this form from now on.

15.2
Cooling down under epoched bridge noise

15.2.1
Preliminaries

For some asymptotic integral estimates we use the theory of regular variation
(see
9
, for more information)
.
A function
f:[0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
is called
regularly varying of index

\rho
if
f
is measurable and

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{f(ct)}{f(t)}\to c^{\rho},\quad c>0.

Further, we call
f

slowly varying
if it is regularly varying of index
\rho=0
.
If
f
is regularly varying, then
f
and
1/f
are locally bounded and locally integrable on
[t_{0},\infty)
for some
t_{0}\geq 0
. Moreover,
we can write

f(t)=t^{\rho}\ell(t),\quad t>0

where
\ell
is slowly varying.

If
f
is regularly varying and
f\sim g
, then
g
is also regularly varying with the same index. In particular, if
g=o(f)
and
f
is regularly varying of index
\rho
, then so is
f+g
(provided
f+g>0
everywhere).

If
f
is regularly varying with negative index, then
f(t)\to 0
, as
t\to\infty
.

If
\ell
is slowly varying, then
\ell(t)=o(t^{\alpha}),t\to\infty
for any
\alpha>0
.
Examples of slowly varying functions include
\log(t)^{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 31
.

Let
\beta\in(0,1)
and
u
be regularly varying with index
-\beta
and define
U_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}u_{s}\,ds
. Then

e^{-U_{t}}=o(f(t)),\quad t\to\infty,

for any regularly varying function
f
.

Proof 15.4
.

Writing
u_{t}=t^{-\beta}\ell(t)
for large
t
, we have by L’Hôpital’s rule

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{U_{t}}{\log t}=\lim_{t\to\infty}tu_{t}=\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{1-\beta}\ell(t)=\infty.

Now, let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
-U_{t}+\alpha\log t\to-\infty
, and so
e^{-U_{t}}t^{\alpha}\to 0
, as
t\to\infty
.
If
f
is regularly varying of index
\alpha
, then
e^{-U_{t}}=o(t^{-|\alpha|-1})=o(f(t))
as
t\to\infty
.

Proposition 32
.

Let
f
and
u
be regularly varying functions with indices
-\rho,-\beta<0
and
\beta<1
. Suppose further that
f
is locally bounded and
u\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
is non-increasing.
Then we have

\int_{0}^{t}f(s)e^{-U_{t}^{s}}\,ds\leq\frac{f(t)}{u(t)}+o\left(\frac{f(t)}{u(t)}\right),\quad t\to\infty,

where
U_{t}^{s}=\int_{s}^{t}u(s)\,ds
.

Proof 15.5
.

Since
u
is non-increasing,
U
is concave and we have

U(s)\leq U(t)+u(t)(s-t),\quad s,t\geq 0,

where
U(t)=U_{t}^{0}
.
Therefore,

\int_{0}^{t}f(s)e^{-U_{t}^{s}}\,ds\leq\int_{0}^{t}f(s)e^{-(t-s)u(t)}\,ds=f(t)\int_{0}^{t}\frac{f(t-s)}{f(t)}e^{-su(t)}\,ds.

(53)

Let
\tau:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be non-increasing, such that

\frac{\tau_{t}}{t}\to 0,\,\tau_{t}u(t)\to\infty,\quad t\to\infty.

(54)

In particular,
\tau_{t}\to\infty
since
u(t)\leq u(0),t\geq 0
. We make a particular choice of
\tau
towards the end.
We split the integral on the RHS of Inequality (
53
) into a main part
\int_{0}^{\tau_{t}}\dots\,ds
and a tail part
\int_{\tau_{t}}^{t}\dots\,ds
.

Let us first estimate the main part. Because
f
is regularly varying with index
-\rho
, we have

\lim_{t\to\infty}\sup_{c\in[a,\infty)}\left|\frac{f(ct)}{f(t)}-c^{-\rho}\right|=0,

for all
a>0

(
9
, Theorem 1.5.2)
.
Since
t-s=t(1-s/t)
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in(0,\tau_{t}]}\left|\frac{f(t-s)}{f(t)}-1\right|=

\displaystyle\sup_{c\in[1-\frac{\tau_{t}}{t},1)}\left|\frac{f(ct)}{f(t)}-1\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{c\in[1-\frac{\tau_{t}}{t},1)}\left|\frac{f(ct)}{f(t)}-c^{-\rho}\right|+\sup_{c\in[1-\frac{\tau_{t}}{t},1)}|c^{-\rho}-1|

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle 0,

because
\frac{\tau_{t}}{t}\to 0
, as
t\to\infty
.
Hence,

\int_{0}^{\tau_{t}}\frac{f(t-s)}{f(t)}e^{-su(t)}\,ds\sim\int_{0}^{\tau_{t}}e^{-su(t)}\,ds=\frac{1}{u(t)}(1-e^{-\tau_{t}u(t)})\sim\frac{1}{u(t)}

as
t\to\infty
.

To estimate the tail integral let
\varepsilon>0
.
By Potter’s theorem
(
9
, Theorem 1.5.6 (iii))
, there exists a
t_{0}\geq 0
with

\frac{f(r)}{f(t)}\lesssim\left(\left(\frac{r}{t}\right)^{-\rho+\varepsilon}\vee\left(\frac{r}{t}\right)^{-\rho-\varepsilon}\right)=\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{\rho+\varepsilon}\leq t_{0}^{-(\rho+\varepsilon)}t^{\rho+\varepsilon},

uniformly over
t\geq r\geq t_{0}
. In particular, by writing
r=t-s
we have

\sup_{s\in[0,t-t_{0}]}\frac{f(t-s)}{f(t)}\lesssim t^{\rho+\varepsilon},

uniformly over large
t
.
Since
f
is locally bounded, we have

\sup_{s\in[t-t_{0},t]}\frac{f(t-s)}{f(t)}\lesssim\frac{1}{f(t)}\sim\ell(t)t^{\rho},\quad t\to\infty,

for some slowly varying function
\ell
. Hence,

\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\frac{f(t-s)}{f(t)}\lesssim t^{\rho+\varepsilon}\ell(t),

uniformly over large
t
, for slowly varying
\ell
.
Thus,

\int_{\tau_{t}}^{t}\frac{f(t-s)}{f(t)}e^{-su(t)}\,ds\lesssim\ell(t)t^{\rho+\varepsilon}\int_{\tau_{t}}^{\infty}e^{-su_{t}}\,ds=\frac{1}{u(t)}\ell(t)t^{\rho+\varepsilon}e^{-\tau_{t}u(t)},

uniformly over large
t
.
Finally, define
\tau_{t}=\frac{(\rho+2\varepsilon)\log t}{u(t)}
. Then the first convergence in (
54
) is satisfied because
u
is regularly varying with index
-\beta\in(-1,0)
. The second follows from
\log t\to\infty
, as
t\to\infty
.
Moreover,
t^{\rho+\varepsilon}e^{-\tau_{t}u(t)}=t^{-\varepsilon}
and so

\int_{\tau_{t}}^{t}\frac{f(t-s)}{f(t)}e^{-su(t)}\,ds=o\left(\frac{1}{u(t)}\right),\quad t\to\infty.

Using Inequality (
53
) we conclude

\int_{0}^{t}f(s)e^{-U_{t}^{s}}\,ds\leq\frac{f(t)}{u(t)}+o\left(\frac{f(t)}{u(t)}\right),\quad t\to\infty.

Lemma 33
.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
a<b
and
f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
be integrable with finite
1
-variation
\|f\|_{1\operatorname{-var}}
. Then

\left|\sum_{n=a+1}^{b}f(n)-\int_{a}^{b}f(t)\,dt\right|\leq\|f\|_{1\operatorname{-var}}.

Proof 15.6
.

We calculate

\displaystyle\sum_{n=a+1}^{b}f(n)=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=a}^{b-1}f(n+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=a}^{b-1}\int_{n}^{n+1}f(t)\,dt+\sum_{n=a}^{b-1}\left(f(n+1)-\int_{n}^{n+1}f(t)\,dt\right)

Note that

\left|f(n+1)-\int_{n}^{n+1}f(t)\,dt\right|\leq\sup_{t\in[n,n+1)}|f(t)-f(n+1)|.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. There exist
t_{a},\dots,t_{b-1}
with
t_{n}\in[n,n+1)
, such that

\sup_{t\in[n,n+1)}|f(t)-f(n+1)|\leq|f(t_{n})-f(n+1)|+\varepsilon.

Then

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{n=a}^{b-1}\left(f(n+1)-\int_{n}^{n+1}f(t)\,dt\right)\right|\leq\|f\|_{1\operatorname{-var}}+(b-a)\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, the desired conclusion follows.

Now, let
\beta\in(0,1),c>0
and consider
u:[0,\infty)\to[0,1],t\mapsto\frac{1}{(1+ct)^{\beta}}
.
Given a positive definite and symmetric matrix
\kappa
, the unique solution to the ODE

\dot{\varphi}_{t}^{s}=-u_{t}\kappa\varphi_{t}^{s}\,\quad t\geq s,y_{s}=1_{d\times d}

is given by
\varphi_{t}^{s}=e^{-\kappa U_{t}^{s}}
, where
U_{t}^{s}=\int_{s}^{t}u_{r}\,dr
, and we have

\|\varphi_{t}^{s}\|_{\operatorname{op}}=\lambda_{\max}(\varphi_{t}^{s})\leq e^{-\lambda U_{t}^{s}},

(55)

where
\lambda:=\lambda_{\min}(\kappa)
. In particular,
\varphi_{t}^{s}
converges to
0
, as
t\to\infty
.

Lemma 34
.

We have

(a)

u\in\operatorname{Lip}^{1}([0,\infty))
,

(b)

u
is strictly decreasing, convex and
\lim_{t\to\infty}u_{t}=0
,

(c)

U
is concave and
\lim_{t\to\infty}U_{t}=\infty
,

(d)

|\dot{u}_{t}|=c\beta u_{t}^{2+\gamma}
for all
t\geq 0
, where
\gamma=\frac{1-\beta}{\beta}>0
,

(e)

\|u_{\cdot}\varphi^{\cdot}_{t}\|_{\operatorname{Lip};[k,(k+1)\wedge t]}\leq(\lambda_{\max}(\kappa)+c\beta u_{k}^{\gamma})u_{k}^{2}e^{-\lambda U_{t}^{(k+1)\wedge t}},

for all
t\geq 1
and
k\leq t
,
In particular,
\|u_{\cdot}\varphi^{\cdot}_{t}\|_{\operatorname{Lip};[k,(k+1)\wedge t]}=o(u_{t}),t\to\infty
.

(f)

For all
\rho>1
and
t\geq 1
we have

\sum_{k=0}^{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor-1}u_{k}^{\rho}e^{-\lambda U^{k+1}_{t}}\leq I_{t}(\rho)+I_{t}(\rho+1)+\rho c\beta I_{t}(\rho+\gamma+1)+e^{-\lambda U_{t}},

where
I_{t}(\alpha)=\int_{0}^{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor-1}u_{s}^{\alpha}e^{-\lambda U^{s+1}_{t}}\,ds
.

(g)

I_{t}(\rho)\leq\lambda^{-1}(ct)^{-(\beta(\rho-1))}+o(t^{-(\beta(\rho-1))}),t\to\infty
, for all
\rho>1
.

(h)

e^{-\lambda U_{t}}=o(t^{-\alpha}),t\to\infty
, for all
\alpha>0
.

(i)

\sum_{k=0}^{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor-1}\|u_{\cdot}\varphi^{\cdot}_{t}\|_{\operatorname{Lip};[k,k+1]}\leq\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\kappa)}{\lambda_{\min}(\kappa)}(ct)^{-\beta}+o(t^{-\beta}),

as
t\to\infty
.

Proof 15.7
.

(a)

u
is differentiable with
\dot{u}_{t}=-c\beta(1+t)^{-(1+\beta)}
and
|\dot{u}_{t}|\leq\beta
,

(b)

Straightforward.

(c)

We have

U_{t}=\frac{1}{1-\beta}\left((1+t)^{1-\beta}-1\right),

so
\lim_{t\to\infty}U_{t}=\infty
. Concavity follows from
u
being strictly decreasing.

(d)

|\dot{u}_{t}|=c\beta(1+t)^{-(1+\beta)}=c\beta(1+t)^{-(1-\beta)}(1+t)^{-2\beta}=c\beta u_{t}^{2+\gamma}
for all
t\geq 0
,

(e)

Let
f_{s}=u_{s}\varphi_{t}^{s}
. Then

\dot{f}_{s}=(\dot{u}_{s}1_{d\times d}+u_{s}^{2}\kappa)\varphi_{t}^{s},

and so

\|\dot{f}_{s}\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq\|\dot{u}_{s}1_{d\times d}+u_{s}^{2}\kappa\|_{\operatorname{op}}\|\varphi_{t}^{s}\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq(|\dot{u}_{s}|+u_{s}^{2}\|\kappa\|_{\operatorname{op}})e^{-\lambda U_{t}^{s}}=(\|\kappa\|_{\operatorname{op}}+c\beta u_{s}^{\gamma})u_{s}^{2}e^{-\lambda U_{t}^{s}},

for all
0\leq s\leq t
. Taking the supremum over
[k,k+1]
for each factor individually yields the estimate.

(f)

Set
n=\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor
. By applying Lemma
33
we have

e^{-\lambda U_{t}}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}u_{k}^{\rho}e^{\lambda U_{k+1}}\leq e^{-\lambda U_{t}}\|(u^{\rho}e^{\lambda U_{\cdot+1}})|_{[0,n-1]}\|_{1\operatorname{-var}}+e^{-\lambda U_{t}}+I_{t}(\rho).

Since

|\partial_{s}(u^{\rho}_{s}e^{\lambda U_{s+1}})|=(\rho u_{s}^{\rho-1}|\dot{u}_{s}|+u_{s}^{\rho+1})e^{\lambda U_{s+1}}\leq u_{s}^{\rho+1}(1+\rho c\beta u_{s}^{\gamma})e^{\lambda U_{s+1}},

we conclude

e^{-\lambda U_{t}}\|(u^{\rho}e^{\lambda U_{\cdot+1}})|_{[0,n-1]}\|_{1\operatorname{-var}}\leq I_{t}(\rho+1)+\rho c\beta I_{t}(\rho+\gamma+1).

(g)

Proposition
32
implies

I_{t}(\rho)\leq\int_{1}^{t}u_{s-1}^{\rho}e^{-\lambda U_{t}^{s}}\leq\frac{u_{t-1}^{\rho}}{\lambda u_{t}}+o\left(\frac{u_{t-1}^{\rho}}{u_{t}}\right),\quad t\to\infty.

Now observe that for
c=1

\frac{u_{t-1}^{\rho}}{u_{t}}=u_{t-1}^{\rho-1}\left(1+\frac{1}{t}\right)^{\beta}=t^{-(\beta(\rho-1))}+o(t^{-(\beta(\rho-1))}),t\to\infty,

so for general
c>0

\frac{u_{t-1}^{\rho}}{u_{t}}=(ct)^{-(\beta(\rho-1))}+o(t^{-(\beta(\rho-1))}),t\to\infty.

(h)

Follows from Lemma
31
.

(i)

By applying (e) and (f) we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\|u_{\cdot}\varphi^{\cdot}_{t}\|_{\operatorname{Lip};[k,k+1]}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}u_{k}^{2}(\lambda_{\max}(\kappa)+\beta u_{k}^{\gamma})e^{-\lambda U_{t}^{(k+1)}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\kappa)(I_{t}(2)+I_{t}(3)+2c\beta I_{t}(3+\gamma)+e^{-\lambda U_{t}})

\displaystyle+\beta(I_{t}(2+\gamma)+I_{t}(3+\gamma)+(2+\gamma)c\beta I_{t}(3+2\gamma)+e^{-\lambda U_{t}}).

We conclude the desired result using (g) and (h).

15.2.2
Convergence results

Proposition 35
.

Let
X
be a locally
\alpha
-Hölder epoched bridge and
Y
be the solution to the linear Young differential equation

dY_{t}=-u_{t}\kappa Y_{t}\,dt+u_{t}\,dX_{t},\quad Y_{0}\in\mathbb{R},t\geq 0.

Then

|Y_{t}|\leq\left(\frac{1}{1-2^{-\alpha}}\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\kappa)}{\lambda_{\min}(\kappa)}+1\right)c^{-\beta}\frac{x^{*}_{t}}{t^{\beta}}+o\left(x^{*}_{t}t^{-\beta}\right),\quad t\to\infty,

where
x^{*}_{t}:=\max_{k\leq t}\|X\|_{\alpha;[k,(k+1)\wedge t]}
.

Proof 15.8
.

Let
t\geq 0
and
n=\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor
. By Proposition
30
we have

Y_{t}=\varphi_{t}Y_{0}+\int_{n}^{t}u_{s}\varphi^{s}_{t}\,dX_{s}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\int_{0}^{1}u_{s+k}\varphi^{s+k}_{t}\,dX_{s+k},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}.

We estimate using the Young-Lóeve inequality in its original form (Proposition
28
) and in the form (
49
) (with
\beta=1
), as well as Inequality (
55
)

\displaystyle|Y_{t}|\leq

\displaystyle|Y_{0}|e^{-\lambda U_{t}}+(|u_{n}\varphi_{t}^{n}X_{n,t}|+C\|u_{\cdot}\varphi^{\cdot}_{t}\|_{\operatorname{Lip};[n,t]}\|X\|_{\alpha;[n,t]})+C\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\|u_{\cdot}\varphi^{\cdot}_{t}\|_{\operatorname{Lip};[k,k+1]}\|X\|_{\alpha;[k,k+1]},

where
C=\frac{1}{1-2^{-\alpha}}
.
We have
e^{-\lambda U_{t}}=o(t^{-\beta})
by Lemma
34
(h).
Further,

|u_{n}\varphi_{t}^{n}X_{n,t}|\leq u_{n}\|\varphi_{t}^{n}\|_{\operatorname{op}}|X_{n,t}|\leq u_{n}\cdot 1\cdot(t-n)^{\alpha}\|X\|_{\alpha;[n,t]}=(x^{*}_{t}t^{-\beta}+o(x^{*}_{t}t^{-\beta})),

t\to\infty
, and

\|u_{\cdot}\varphi^{\cdot}_{t}\|_{\operatorname{Lip};[n,t]}\|X\|_{\alpha;[n,t]}=o(x_{t}^{*}t^{-\beta}),\quad t\to\infty,

by Lemma
34
(e).
Finally,

\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\|u_{\cdot}\varphi^{\cdot}_{t}\|_{\operatorname{Lip};[k,k+1]}\|X\|_{\alpha;[k,k+1]}\leq\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\kappa)}{\lambda_{\min}(\kappa)}\frac{x_{t}^{*}}{t^{\beta}}+o(x_{t}^{*}t^{-\beta}),\quad t\to\infty,

by Lemma
34
(i).

Proposition 36
.

Let
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
be
\lambda
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth with
\nabla\mathcal{R}(0)=0
and
\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}
Hölder continuous.
Let
X
be locally Hölder continuous and assume that
X
does not vanish on any closed interval of positive measure.
Let
Y_{0}=Z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
Y,Z
be the solutions to the Young differential equations

\displaystyle dY_{t}=

\displaystyle-u_{t}\nabla\mathcal{R}(Y_{t})\,dt+u_{t}\,dX_{t},

\displaystyle dZ_{t}=

\displaystyle-u_{t}\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}(0)Z_{t}\,dt+u_{t}\,dX_{t},\quad t\geq 0.

Let
f
be regularly varying with negative index and assume
|Z_{t}|\leq f(t),t\to\infty
.
Then also

|Y_{t}|\leq f(t)+o(f(t)),\quad t\to\infty.

Proof 15.9
.

Firstly, assume
\mathcal{R}
is not quadratic. Otherwise,
Y=Z
and we are done.
Now, using Hadarmard’s lemma we have

r(y):=\nabla\mathcal{R}(y)-\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}(0)y=\int_{0}^{1}(\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}(ty)-\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}(0))y\,dt.

Thus, the Hölder continuity of
\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}
implies

|\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}(ty)-\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}(0)|\lesssim|ty|^{\gamma}\leq|y|^{\gamma},\quad t\in[0,1],y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

for some
\gamma\in(0,1]
. Thus,

|r(y)|\lesssim|y|^{1+\gamma}

(56)

uniformly over
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and we can write

dY_{t}=-u_{t}(\kappa Y_{t}+r(Y_{t}))\,dt+u_{t}\,dX_{t},\quad t\geq 0,

where
\kappa:=\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}(0)
.
Let
\delta=Y-Z
. Then

\dot{\delta}_{t}=-u_{t}\kappa\delta_{t}-u_{t}r(Y_{t}).

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}(|\delta_{t}|^{2})=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\langle\delta_{t},\delta_{t}\rangle=\langle\dot{\delta}_{t},\delta_{t}\rangle=

\displaystyle-u_{t}\langle\kappa\delta_{t}+r(Y_{t}),\delta_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-u_{t}\langle\kappa\delta_{t}+r(Y_{t})-r(Z_{t}),\delta_{t}\rangle+u_{t}\langle r(Z_{t}),\delta_{t}\rangle,\quad t\geq 0.

Since
\mathcal{R}
is
\lambda
-strongly convex we have

\langle\kappa y+r(y)-(\kappa z+r(z)),y-z\rangle=\langle\nabla\mathcal{R}(y)-\nabla\mathcal{R}(z),y-z\rangle\geq\lambda|y-z|^{2},\quad y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Hence, writing
v=|\delta|
,

\dot{v}_{t}v_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}(v_{t}^{2})\leq-u_{t}\lambda v_{t}^{2}+u_{t}|r(Z_{t})|v_{t},

and so

\dot{v}_{t}\leq-u_{t}\lambda v_{t}+u_{t}|r(Z_{t})|,

(57)

for all
t\geq 0
, such that
\delta_{t}\neq 0
.
The set

\{t\geq 0:\delta_{t}=0\}

has Lebesgue measure zero. To show this note that if
\delta_{t}=0
, then

\dot{\delta}_{t}=-u_{t}r(Y_{t}).

Assume
\delta=0
on an interval
[t,w]
. Then

\dot{\delta}_{s}=-u_{s}r(Y_{s})=0,\quad s\in[t,w].

Since
\mathcal{R}
is not quadratic we have
r(y)=0
if and only if
y=0
.
Together with
u>0
everywhere this implies
Y=0
on
[t,w]
.
Thus,

Y_{s}=Y_{t}+\int_{t}^{s}u_{v}\,dX_{v}=\int_{t}^{s}u_{v}\,dX_{v}

implying
X=0
on
[t,w]
, which we assumed to be impossible.
Thus,
\delta_{t}=0
only at isolated points
t\geq 0
.
Hence, the set of
\delta
s zeros has measure
0
.

Moving on, define the integrating factor
I_{t}=e^{\lambda U_{t}}
. Then using Inequality (
57
)

\partial_{t}(I_{t}v_{t})=I_{t}\dot{v}_{t}+\lambda u_{t}v_{t}I_{t}\leq u_{t}|r(Z_{t})|I_{t},

for almost all
t\geq 0
.
Hence,

|\delta_{t}|e^{\lambda U_{t}}=I_{t}v_{t}\leq\int_{0}^{t}u_{s}|r(Z_{s})|e^{\lambda U_{s}}\,ds.

Note that the function
\tilde{f}=uf^{1+\gamma}
is again regularly varying with negative index.
Thus, using Inequality (
56
) and Proposition
32
for the function
\tilde{f}
,

|\delta_{t}|\leq\int_{0}^{t}u_{s}e^{-\lambda U_{t}^{s}}|Z_{s}|^{1+\gamma}\,ds\leq\int_{0}^{t}u_{s}e^{-\lambda U_{t}^{s}}f(s)^{1+\gamma}\,ds=O\left(\frac{\tilde{f}(t)}{u(t)}\right)=o(f(t)),\quad t\to\infty.

We conclude

|Y_{t}|\leq|\delta_{t}|+|Z_{t}|\leq f(t)+o(f(t)),\quad t\to\infty.

Corollary 37
.

Let
X
be a locally
\alpha
-Hölder epoched bridge that does not vanish on any closed interval of positive measure, and such that

\max_{k\leq t}\|X\|_{\alpha;[k,(k+1)\wedge t]}\leq\ell(t),\quad t\to\infty,

for some slowly varying function
\ell
.
Further, let
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
be
\lambda
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth with
\nabla\mathcal{R}(0)=0
and
\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}
Hölder continuous.
If
Y
is the solution to the Young differential equation

dY_{t}=-u_{t}\nabla\mathcal{R}(Y_{t})\,dt+u_{t}\,dX_{t},\quad Y_{0}\in\mathbb{R},t\geq 0,

then

|Y_{t}|\leq\left(\frac{1}{1-2^{-\alpha}}\frac{L}{\lambda}+1\right)c^{-\beta}\frac{\ell(t)}{t^{\beta}}+o\left(\ell(t)t^{-\beta}\right),\quad t\to\infty.

Proof 15.10
.

We apply Proposition
35
to the linear ODE

dZ_{t}=-u_{t}\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}(0)Z_{t}\,dt+u_{t}\,dX_{t}.

Then, Proposition
36
implies the desired conclusion.

16
Proof of the main theorem

Firstly, let us prove that
(\nabla\mathcal{R})^{-1}
is actually well-defined.

Lemma 38
.

Let
\lambda>0
. Suppose
\mathcal{R}
is
\lambda
-strongly convex with Lipschitz gradient. Then
\nabla\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is bijective.

Proof 16.1
.

Strong convexity implies strong monotonicity, that is

\langle\nabla\mathcal{R}(x)-\nabla\mathcal{R}(y),x-y\rangle\geq\lambda|x-y|^{2},\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

In particular,
\nabla\mathcal{R}
is injective.
To show surjectivity we use the Browder-Minty theorem
(see
38
, Theorem 10.49)
, identifying
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with its dual space. Indeed,
\nabla\mathcal{R}
is monotone, as shown before. Also since
\nabla\mathcal{R}
is Lipschitz, it is in particular continuous and preserves bounded sets. To show coercivity, note that strong convexity of
\mathcal{R}
implies

\mathcal{R}(0)\geq\mathcal{R}(x)+\langle\nabla\mathcal{R}(x),0-x\rangle+\frac{\lambda}{2}|x|^{2},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

That is,

\langle\nabla\mathcal{R}(x),x\rangle\geq\mathcal{R}(x)-\mathcal{R}(0)+\frac{\lambda}{2}|x|^{2}.

In particular,

\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{\langle\nabla\mathcal{R}(x),x\rangle}{|x|}=\infty.

Hence,
\nabla\mathcal{R}
is coercive, and thus also surjective.

Now, let us transform equation (
46
) into a simpler form.
We can rewrite

dY_{t}=-u_{t}(\nabla\mathcal{R}(Y_{t})-T^{-1/2}\sigma Z)\,dt+u_{t}\sqrt{T}\sigma dX_{t/T},

or equivalently

dY_{tT}=-u_{tT}\nabla\hat{\mathcal{R}}(Y_{tT})\,dt+u_{tT}\sqrt{T}\sigma dX_{t},

where
Z=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\hat{W}_{T}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1_{d\times d}),\hat{W}_{t}=\sqrt{T}X_{t/T}+\frac{t}{\sqrt{T}}Z
and
X
is an epoched Brownian bridge independent of
Z
,
and
\hat{\mathcal{R}}(y)=\mathcal{R}(y)-T^{-1/2}\sigma Zy
. Note that

(\nabla\hat{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}(0)=(\nabla\mathcal{R}-T^{-1/2}\sigma Z)^{-1}(0)=(\nabla\mathcal{R})^{-1}(T^{-1/2}\sigma Z).

Define

\tilde{Y}_{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sigma^{-1}(Y_{tT}-(\nabla\hat{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}(0)),\quad t\geq 0.

Then

d\tilde{Y}_{t}=-u_{tT}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sigma^{-1}\nabla\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\sqrt{T}\sigma\tilde{Y}_{t}+(\nabla\hat{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}(0))\,dt+u_{tT}dX_{t},\quad t\geq 0.

Equivalently, we can write

d\tilde{Y}_{t}=-u_{tT}\nabla\tilde{\mathcal{R}}(Y_{t})\,dt+u_{tT}\,dX_{t},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{R}}(y):=

\displaystyle T^{-1}\sigma^{-2}\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\sqrt{T}\sigma y+(\nabla\hat{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}(0))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T^{-1}\sigma^{-2}\mathcal{R}(\sqrt{T}\sigma y+T^{-1}\sigma\hat{W}_{T})-T^{-1}\sigma\hat{W}_{T}y,\quad y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Let us summarize this procedure in a proposition.

Lemma 39
.

Let
Y
be the solution to (
46
). Then

\tilde{Y}_{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sigma^{-1}(Y_{tT}-(\nabla\mathcal{R})^{-1}(T^{-1}\sigma\hat{W}_{T}))

is the unique solution to the Young differential equation

d\tilde{Y}_{t}=-\tilde{u}_{t}\nabla\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\tilde{Y}_{t})+\tilde{u}_{t}\,dX_{t},\quad t\geq 0,

where
\tilde{u}_{t}=u_{tT}
and

\tilde{\mathcal{R}}(y)=T^{-1}\sigma^{-2}\mathcal{R}(\sqrt{T}\sigma y+T^{-1}\sigma\hat{W}_{T})-T^{-1}\sigma\hat{W}_{T}y,\quad y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Proof 16.2

(Proof of Theorems
22
and
22
)
.

Recall the definition of
Y
in (
46
). Apply Lemma
39
, then

Y_{t}=\sqrt{T}\sigma\tilde{Y}_{t/T}+(\nabla\mathcal{R})^{-1}(T^{-1}\sigma\hat{W}_{T}).

Note that
X
does not vanish on any closed interval of positive measure, almost surely.
Suppose for now we are given slowly varying function
\ell
with

\max_{k\leq t}\|X\|_{\alpha;[k,(k+1)\wedge t]}\leq\ell(t),\quad a.s.,t\to\infty.

(58)

By Corollary
37

\left|Y_{t}-(\nabla\mathcal{R})^{-1}(T^{-1}\sigma\hat{W}_{T})\right|\leq\sqrt{T}|\sigma|\left(\frac{1}{1-2^{-\alpha}}\frac{L}{\lambda}+1\right)(cT)^{-\beta}\frac{\ell(t)}{t^{\beta}}+o\left(\ell(t)t^{-\beta}\right),\quad t\to\infty.

Here, we used that
\nabla^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}(0)=\nabla^{2}\mathcal{R}((\nabla\mathcal{R})^{-1}(T^{-1}\sigma\hat{W}_{T}))
.

We can find a slowly varying function
\ell
such that Inequality (
58
) holds true. Indeed, by Lemma
27
we can set

\ell(t):=a^{-1/2}\sqrt{\log t}+g(t)\geq a^{-1/2}\sqrt{\log{(\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor+1)}},

for
a\in(0,\frac{1}{2(1-b)b^{1-2\alpha}})
, where
b=\frac{1-2\alpha}{2-2\alpha}
, and

g(t)=a^{-1/2}(\sqrt{\log{(\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor+1)}}-\sqrt{\log t})=o(\sqrt{\log t}),\quad t\to\infty.

If we pick
\alpha=0.42
,
a=0.8\in(0,0.858581)=(0,\frac{1}{2(1-b)b^{1-2\alpha}})
, then

a^{-1/2}=1.11803<1.2,\quad a^{-1/2}\frac{1}{1-2^{-\alpha}}=4.61727<4.7,

proving Theorem
22
(the second constant cannot be lowered much further).
Assume now there exists a number
J\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
\mathcal{I}:=\{(W_{(j+t)T}-W_{jT})_{t\in[0,1]}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}|
satisfies
|\mathcal{I}|=J
, almost surely. Then we can instead set
\ell(t)=\max_{w\in\mathcal{I}}\|w\|_{\alpha},t\geq 0
in Inequality (
58
), proving Theorem
22
.

Chapter 7
On the weak convergence of shuffled random walks

In this chapter, we study scaling limits of random walks that share the same increments up to a (random) permutation.
Assuming convergence of the sequence of random permutations to a deterministic
J
-dimensional permuton
\mu
(i.e. a probability measure on
[0,1]^{J}
with uniform marginals), we show weak convergence of these random walks to a
J
-dimensional Gaussian process
W
. The covariance of two different components of
W
is then given by the bivariate marginals of the joint distribution function of
\mu
. Conversely, given a
J
-dimensional permuton
\mu
we can find a sequence of (higher dimensional) random permutations converging to
\mu
. Thus, we further prove that every Gaussian process
W
, whose covariance function is determined by
\mu
, is the scaling of some family of random walks sharing the same increments up to a random permutation.
Finally, we show that epoched Brownian processes determined by a permuton
\mu
arise as scaling limits of random walks that use only finitely many distinct increments.

17
Introduction

Consider a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
and a sequence of i.i.d. random variables
(Z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\mathbb{E}Z_{1}=0,\operatorname{Var}Z_{1}=1
and
\mathbb{E}[Z_{1}^{4}]<\infty
. Denote the group of permutations of size
N
by
\mathcal{S}_{N}
. Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
and a family of permutations of increasing size, that is
(\tau_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\tau_{N}\in\mathcal{S}_{N}
, define

S_{n}^{N,\tau}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}Z_{\tau_{N}(k)},\quad n\in\{0,\dots,N\},N\in\mathbb{N}.

Then
S^{N,\tau}
is a simple random walk using the shuffled increments
(Z_{\tau_{N}(i)})_{i=1}^{N}
. Using appropriate scaling and interpolation we expect this random walk to converge weakly to a Brownian motion, as
N\to\infty
. We are interested in the limiting behavior of the joint distribution of the random vector
(S^{N,\operatorname{id}_{N}},S^{N,\tau})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
consisting of the random walk with unshuffled increments and the one with increments shuffled by
(\tau_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, appropriately scaled and interpolated.

More generally, let
J\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}:=\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. We call a sequence of
N
-permutations
(\sigma^{j})_{j<J}=(\sigma^{j})_{j\in[0,J)\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}}
a
J
-dimensional permutation
9
9

9

There are several possible interpretations for the term
“
J
-dimensional permutation”
. We follow the convention by
11
,
except
what we call
J
-dimensional they call
J-1
dimensional.
of size
N
. We let
\mathcal{S}^{J}_{N}
denote the set of
J
-dimensional permutation of size
N
. Now, consider a sequence of
random

J
-dimensional permutations
10
10

10

Without loss of generality we may set
\sigma_{N}^{0}=\operatorname{id}_{N},N\in\mathbb{N}
, but since it does not simplify any of the upcoming arguments we will not. However, it can be useful in examples to make this choice, especially in the case
J=2
.
of increasing size
\sigma=(\sigma_{N}:\Omega\to\mathcal{S}^{J}_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
. Equivalently,
\sigma
is a random element of
\prod_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{S}^{J}_{N}
. Define the family of
\mathbb{R}^{J}
-valued stochastic processes
(X^{N}:\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{J})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
by

X^{N}_{t}=(X^{N,0}_{t},X^{N,1}_{t},\dots):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}(S^{N,\sigma^{j}}_{\left\lfloor Nt\right\rfloor})_{j<J},\quad t\in[0,1],N\in\mathbb{N}.

Here, we equip
\mathbb{R}^{J}=\prod_{j<J}\mathbb{R}
with the product topology. Note that
\mathbb{R}^{J}
is a Polish space. Our goal is to find sufficient conditions, such that
(X^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in distribution in the Skorokhod space
\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{J})
, as
N\to\infty
, to a Gaussian process
W
.

To our knowledge, scaling limits of random walks with shared increments up to a permutation have not been studied in this form before. We note however that Gaussian limits with the same law as
W
(see our main theorem
40
) have been studied in the theory of empirical processes, albeit with no connection to permutations or random walks, and only for finite
J
. Consider for simplicity
J=2
. Given a
2
-copula
C
(i.e. the distribution function of a
2
-dimensional permuton) we call a random field
B:\Omega\times[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
a
C
-Brownian bridge
if

\mathbb{E}[B_{u,v}B_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}]=C(u\wedge u^{\prime},v\wedge v^{\prime})-C(u,v)C(u^{\prime},v^{\prime}),\quad u,u^{\prime},v,v^{\prime}\in[0,1].

Then the
2
-dimensional Gaussian process
W
in Theorem
40
is given by

W_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}W^{0}_{t}\\
W^{1}_{t}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}B_{t,1}\\
B_{1,t}\end{pmatrix}+t\begin{pmatrix}V\\
V\end{pmatrix},\quad t\in[0,1],

for some
C
-Brownian bridge
B
and a standard Gaussian random variable
V
independent of
B
. Here,
C=F^{01}_{\mu}
is the distribution function of the limiting permuton
\mu
determining the law of
W
.
Indeed, in this case the components of
W
are Brownian motions with

\mathbb{E}[W_{s}^{0}W_{t}^{1}]=\mathbb{E}[(B_{s,1}+sV)(B_{1,t}+tV)]=\mathbb{E}[B_{s,1}B_{1,t}]+st=C(s,t)-st+st=C(s,t),

for
s,t\in[0,1]
. This coincides with the covariance formula in Theorem
40
.

18
The story in two dimensions

Before we start with the general theory, let us heuristically explore the case
J=2
first. Let
(Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be an i.i.d. sequence with
\mathbb{E}Z_{0}=0,\operatorname{Var}Z_{0}=1
and finite fourth moment, and let
(\pi_{N}:\Omega\to\mathcal{S}_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of random permutations increasing in size, independent of
(Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.
We consider the following two random sums with shared increments

S_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}Z_{k},\quad S_{n}^{N,\pi}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}Z_{\pi_{N}(k)},\quad n\in\{0,\dots,N\},N\in\mathbb{N}.

Let

X_{t}^{N}=(X_{t}^{N,0},X_{t}^{N,1})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}(S_{\left\lfloor Nt\right\rfloor},S^{N,\pi}_{\left\lfloor Nt\right\rfloor}),\quad t\geq 0,N\in\mathbb{N}.

Then, it is well-known that
X^{N,0}
and
X^{N,1}
converge to Brownian motions
W^{0}
and
W^{1}
in law. We are interested in the convergence of the joint distribution
X^{N}
. A straightforward computation shows

\operatorname{Cov}(X_{s}^{N,0},X_{t}^{N,1})=\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}|[Ns]\cap\pi_{N}[Nt]|,\quad s,t\in[0,1],

where
\tau[x]=\{\tau(1),\dots,\tau(\left\lfloor x\right\rfloor)\}
for
x\geq 0
.
Looking closely at the quantity
\frac{1}{N}|[Ns]\cap\pi_{N}[Nt]|
we notice that it is the joint distribution function of the empirical measure

\hat{\mu}_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\pi_{N}^{-1})}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathds{1}_{\cdot}(k/N,\pi_{N}^{-1}(k)/N)

on the unit square
[0,1]^{2}
(notice the inverse
\pi^{-1}_{N}
). Here, the joint distribution function of a probability measure
\nu
on
[0,1]^{2}
is given by

F_{\nu}(s,t)=\nu([0,s]\times[0,t]),\quad s,t\in[0,1].

Suppose
\hat{\mu}_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\pi_{N}^{-1})}
converges weakly in the space of probability measures on
[0,1]^{2}
to a deterministic probability measure
\mu
.
Then we can actually already conclude that
X^{N}
converges weakly to a Gaussian process
W=(W^{0},W^{1})
.
The limiting measure
\mu
necessarily has uniform marginals on
[0,1]
. A probability measure on
[0,1]^{2}
with uniform marginals is called a (two-dimensional)
permuton
.
In the proof for convergence we use the fact that the distribution function of
\hat{\mu}_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\pi_{N}^{-1})}
, i.e.
(s,t)\mapsto\frac{1}{N}|[Ns]\cap\pi_{N}[Nt]|
, must also converge, in fact uniformly, to the distribution function
F_{\mu}
of
\mu
. The distribution function of a permuton is called a (
2-
)
copula
.
The copula
F_{\mu}
determines the covariance function of
W
, via

\operatorname{Cov}(W_{s}^{0},W_{t}^{1})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}|[Ns]\cap\pi_{N}[Nt]|=F_{\mu}(s,t),\quad s,t\in[0,1].

Further, we find it more convenient to work with permutons throughout. For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the permuton

\mu_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\pi_{N}^{-1})}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathcal{U}\left[\frac{k-1}{N},\frac{k}{N}\right]\otimes\mathcal{U}\left[\frac{\pi_{N}^{-1}(k)-1}{N},\frac{\pi_{N}^{-1}(k)}{N}\right].

Here,
\mathcal{U}[a,b]
is the uniform distribution on
[a,b]
.
Compared to the empirical measure
\hat{\mu}_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\pi_{N}^{-1})}
, we essentially replaced all mass points with squares of side length
1/N
. Since

\|F_{\mu_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\pi_{N}^{-1})}}-F_{\hat{\mu}_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\pi_{N}^{-1})}}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{2}{N},\quad N\in\mathbb{N},

we can work with the copulas
F_{\mu_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\pi_{N}^{-1})}}
instead of
F_{\hat{\mu}_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\pi_{N}^{-1})}}
throughout the proof.

Now, let us invert the situation. Suppose we are given a permuton
\mu
. Then it well-known from the theory of permutons and large permutations that we can construct a sequence of random permutations
(\pi_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\mu_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\pi_{N}^{-1})}
converges weakly to
\mu
.

In fact, consider an i.i.d. sequence
(U_{N}:\Omega\to[0,1]^{2})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
with
U_{N}\sim\mu
. Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
define a permutation
\operatorname{Perm}(v)
of size
N
such that it orders the elements of
v
from least to greatest. Ties are ranked according to their original position in
v
, ensuring
\operatorname{Perm}(v)
is an actual permutation.
Set
\sigma^{j}_{N}=\operatorname{Perm}(U^{j}_{1},\dots,U^{j}_{N}),j=1,2
, i.e. define permutations
\sigma^{1}_{N},\sigma^{2}_{N}
by sorting the random vectors
(U_{1}^{1},\dots,U_{N}^{1}),(U_{1}^{2},\dots,U_{N}^{2})
. By defining
\pi_{N}^{-1}=\sigma^{2}_{N}\circ(\sigma^{1}_{N})^{-1}
we can indeed show that
\mu_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\pi_{N}^{-1})}
converges weakly to
\mu
.
Thus, given a two-dimensional Gaussian process
W
with
\operatorname{Cov}(W_{s}^{1},W_{t}^{2})=F_{\mu}(s,t)
and Brownian marginals, we can construct
S
and
X
in such a way that
X^{N}\to W
in law.

19
Main theorem

Let us rewrite

S_{n}^{N,\sigma^{j}}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{j}_{N}[n]}(k)Z_{k},\quad j<J,0\leq n\leq N.

Here and in the following we write
[x]:=\mathbb{N}\cap[0,x]
, for all
x\geq 0
. So given
\tau\in\mathcal{S}_{N}
, where
N\geq\left\lfloor x\right\rfloor
, we have
\tau[x]=\{\tau(1),\dots,\tau(\left\lfloor x\right\rfloor)\}
.
The covariance between the components of
X
satisfies

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}(X^{N,i}_{s},X^{N,j}_{t})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\operatorname{Cov}(\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{i}_{N}[Ns]}(k)Z_{k},\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt]}(l)Z_{l})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{i}_{N}[Ns]}(k)\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt]}(k)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}|\sigma^{i}_{N}[Ns]\cap\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt]|,\quad i,j<J,

since by the law of total covariance

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}(\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{i}_{N}[Ns]}(k)Z_{k},\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt]}(l)Z_{l})=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Cov}(\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{i}_{N}[Ns]}(k)Z_{k},\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt]}(l)Z_{l}|\sigma)]

\displaystyle+\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{i}_{N}[Ns]}(k)Z_{k}|\sigma],\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt]}(l)Z_{l}|\sigma])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{i}_{N}[Ns]}(k)\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt]}(k)]\delta_{k,l},\quad i,j<J,k,l\in[N].

Thus, if
X^{N}
converges in distribution, then
\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}|\sigma^{i}_{N}[Ns]\cap\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt]|
also has to converge for all
i,j,s,t
, under uniform integrability assumptions. Conversely, this suggests that the sequence of
J
-dimensional permutations
(\sigma_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
needs to converge in a certain sense.

Given a Polish space
S
, we consider the space of probability measures
\mathcal{P}(S)
equipped with the topology of weak convergence, i.e.
\nu_{n}\to\nu
if

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{S}f\,d\mu_{n}=\int_{S}f\,d\mu,

for all continuous and bounded
f:S\to\mathbb{R}
. Equivalently,
\nu_{n}\to\nu
if
\nu_{n}(A)\to\nu(A)
for all
A\in\mathcal{F}_{S}
with
\nu(\partial A)=0
. The space
\mathcal{P}(S)
is again Polish and thus we may consider weak convergence in
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}(S)
.
Given random measures
\nu_{n},\nu:\Omega\to\mathcal{P}(S),n\in\mathbb{N}
, we say
\nu_{n}
converges to
\nu
in distribution if
\nu_{n}\mathbb{P}\to\nu\mathbb{P}
in
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}(S)
. Here,
\nu\mathbb{P}(A)=\mathbb{P}(\nu^{-1}(A)),A\in\mathcal{F}
.

Given
J\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
and
\tau\in\mathcal{S}^{J}_{N}
we define the empirical measure
\hat{\mu}_{\tau}\in\mathcal{P}([0,1]^{J})
by

\hat{\mu}_{\tau}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathds{1}_{\cdot}((\tau^{j}(k)/N)_{j<J}).

This measure has discrete uniform marginals on the set
\{\frac{1}{N},\dots,\frac{N}{N}\}
. As
N\to\infty
the marginals converge to the uniform distribution on
[0,1]
.
Alternatively, it can be more convenient to have continuous uniform marginals even before taking the limit. We call a probability measure on
[0,1]^{J}
with
\mathcal{U}{[0,1]}
-marginals a
J
-dimensional permuton
or
J
-permuton
for short. With
\tau\in\mathcal{S}^{J}_{N}
we associate the following
J
-permuton

\mu_{\tau}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\bigotimes_{j<J}\mathcal{U}\left(\frac{1}{N}[\tau^{j}(k)-1,\tau^{j}(k)]\right),

where
\mathcal{U}{[a,b]}
denotes the uniform distribution on
[a,b]
. Here,
\bigotimes
denotes the product measure operation. Effectively, this replaces the mass points from the empirical measure of
\tau
with hypercubes of side length
1/N
.

For
J<\infty
and any finite-dimensional measure
\nu\in\mathcal{P}([0,1]^{J})
we consider the joint distribution function

F_{\nu}(t)=\nu([0,t_{0}]\times\dots\times[0,t_{J-1}]),\quad t=(t_{0},\dots,t_{J-1})\in[0,1]^{J}.

Given
\nu\in\mathcal{P}([0,1]^{J})
and a tuple
a\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
with length
n=|a|<J
we consider the projection
\nu^{a}\in\mathcal{P}([0,1]^{n})
given by

\nu^{a}(A):=\operatorname{pr}^{a}\nu(A)=\nu((\operatorname{pr}^{a})^{-1}(A)),\quad A\in\mathcal{F}_{[0,1]^{n}},

where

\operatorname{pr}^{a}:\mathbb{R}^{J}\to\mathbb{R}^{n},(x_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\mapsto(x_{a_{1}},\dots,x_{a_{n}}).

We also write
F^{a}_{\nu}:=F_{\nu^{a}}
and define the (joint) distribution function of an infinite-dimensional measure
\nu\in\mathcal{P}([0,1]^{\mathbb{N}})
by

F_{\nu}:[0,1]^{*}\to[0,1],t\mapsto F_{\nu}^{1\dots|t|}(t).

Any
\nu\in\mathcal{P}([0,1]^{J})
is uniquely determined by its distribution function (even for
J=\infty
).
The distribution function of
J
-permutons are known as
J
-
copulas
for finite
J
. Accordingly, we also call the distribution function of an
\infty
-permuton an
\infty
-
copula
.

Write
\tau^{-1}:=((\tau^{j})^{-1})_{j<J}
for any
J
-dimensional permutation
\tau
.
We can relate the bivariate marginals of the empirical measure
\hat{\mu}_{\sigma_{N}^{-1}}
to the covariance of the process
X^{N}
.
Specifically, we will show that for
i,j<J
(see Lemma
42
(a) below)

\hat{F}_{N}^{ij}(s,t):=F_{\hat{\mu}_{\sigma^{-1}_{N}}}^{ij}(s,t)=\frac{1}{N}|\sigma^{i}_{N}[Ns]\cap\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt]|,\quad s,t\in[0,1].

The case
i=j
is included and we have

\hat{F}^{ii}_{N}(s,t)=\frac{1}{N}|[Ns]\cap[Nt]|\to s\wedge t,\text{ as }N\to\infty.

By Lemma
42
(b) below, the convergence of the bivariate distribution functions
\hat{F}_{N}^{ij}
is equivalent to the convergence of the bivariate marginals of the
\infty
-permutons
\mu_{\sigma^{-1}_{N}}
. Nevertheless, we require convergence of not just the bivariate marginals in the following.

Assumption 40
.

The sequence of random
J
-permutons
(\mu_{\sigma_{N}^{-1}})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to a deterministic measure in distribution, i.e. there exists a
\mu\in\mathcal{P}([0,1]^{\mathbb{N}})
, such that

\mu_{N}:=\mu_{\sigma_{N}^{-1}}\to\mu,

in distribution, as
N\to\infty
.

In this case
\mu
is also a
J
-permuton and, since the limit is deterministic, the convergence is also in probability, with respect to the weak convergence topology.

Recall that for any set
T
a random field
Y:\Omega\times T\to\mathbb{R}
is called Gaussian if
(Y_{t_{1}},\dots,Y_{t_{m}})
is Gaussian, for all
t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\in T
. We say
Y
is centered if
\mathbb{E}Y_{t}=0
for all
t\in T
.

Let
(U^{j})_{j<J}
be a family of
\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
-random variables and define

A_{(t,j)}=\{U^{j}\leq t\},\quad j<J,t\in[0,1].

Then the kernel on the set
[0,1]\times([0,J)\cap\mathbb{N}_{0})
given by

K((s,i),(t,j))=\mathbb{P}(A_{(s,i)}\cap A_{(t,j)})=\mathbb{P}(U^{i}\leq s,U^{j}\leq t)=\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{A(s,i)}\mathds{1}_{A(t,j)}],\quad i,j<J,s,t\in[0,1]

is positive semi-definite. This is because for every finite family
(t_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(t_{m},j_{m})
, the matrix

(K((t_{k},j_{k}),(t_{l},j_{l})))_{k,l\in\{1,\dots,m\}}

is the Gram matrix of the indicator functions
\mathds{1}_{A(t_{1},j_{1})},\dots,\mathds{1}_{A(t_{m},j_{m})}
in the Hilbert space
11
11

11

The inner product is
(X,Y)\mapsto\mathbb{E}[XY]
.

\mathcal{L}^{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{P})
and thus positive semi-definite.
By choosing
U^{j}\sim\mu,j<J
, we conclude that there exists a stochastic process
W:\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{J}
, such that

\Omega\times[0,1]\times([0,J)\cap\mathbb{N}_{0})\to\mathbb{R},(\omega,t,j)\mapsto W^{j}_{t}(\omega)

is centered Gaussian, and

\operatorname{Cov}(W^{i}_{s},W^{j}_{t})=\mathbb{P}(U^{i}\leq s,U^{j}\leq t)=F^{ij}_{\mu}(s,t),\quad s,t\in[0,1],i,j<J.

Our goal is first to show that
W
is indeed a limit of the sequence of processes
(X^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
in distribution.
We want also show that conversely, if we are given a
J
-permuton
\mu
, then we can find a sequence of random
J
-dimensional permutations
(\sigma_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, such that Assumption (A
40
) is satisfied.

Theorem 40
.

Suppose we are given a sequence
(\sigma_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
of random
J
-dimensional permutations, such that (A
40
) holds true.
Then
(X^{N}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
converges in distribution to a centered Gaussian process
W
, as
N\to\infty
, with

\operatorname{Cov}(W^{i}_{s},W^{j}_{t})=F^{ij}_{\mu}(s,t),\quad s,t\in[0,1],i,j<J.

(59)

Conversely, given a centered Gaussian process
W
and a
J
-permuton
\mu
, such that (
59
) holds true, there exists a sequence
(\sigma_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
of random
J
-dimensional permutations, such that (A
40
) holds true and so
(X^{N})_{t\in[0,1]}
converges in distribution to
W
.

Example 19.1
.

We give several examples for
(\sigma_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
such that (A
40
) is fulfilled and thus the convergence in Theorem
40
holds true.

(a)

Let
J=\infty
and
\sigma_{N}=(\operatorname{id}_{N})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
, so that no shuffling occurs at all. Then

\mu_{N}
converges to the (deterministic) singular measure
\mu
given by the
comonotonicity copula

F_{\mu}(t)=t_{0}\wedge\dots\wedge t_{m-1},\quad t\in[0,1]^{m},m<J.

The process
W
is equal in distribution to
(\tilde{W})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
where
\tilde{W}
is a one-dimensional Brownian motion on
[0,1]
.

(b)

Let
J=\infty
. Suppose
(\sigma^{j}_{N})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is i.i.d. with
\sigma^{1}_{N}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathcal{S}_{N})
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then one can show that
\mu_{N}
converges to the infinite product of the Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]
with itself, i.e.

\mu=\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])\otimes\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])\otimes\dots

That is, its joint distribution function is the
independence copula

F_{\mu}(t)=\prod_{k=0}^{m-1}t_{k},\quad t\in[0,1]^{m},m<J.

(c)

Set
J=2
.
Define
\tau\in\mathcal{S}_{N}
by
\tau_{N}(n)=N-n+1,n\in\mathbb{N}
, that is
\tau
puts the elements of
\{1,\dots,N\}
into reverse order. Note that
\tau_{N}^{-1}=\tau_{N}
. The distribution function of the sequence of
2
-permutons
\mu_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\tau_{N})}
converges to the
countermonotonicity copula

\mathcal{W}(s,t)=\max(s+t-1,0)

There is no direct higher-dimensional analogue to this because reversing the order twice leaves us with the original order.

We justify these claims (see in particular Example
20.10
) and give further examples in Subsection
20.2
.

20
Permutons and copulas

In this section we give additional background on (higher-dimensional) permutons and copulas. None of this material is really new, except for the simple extension to
J=\infty
.
For permutons we follow
11
and for copulas
16
.

20.1
Approximation by random permutations

The following lemma explains that while our definition of
J
-dimensional permutations and their associated permutons differs slightly from the typical definitions, in particular the ones by
11
, the difference is not crucial. This is also the reason why we said we
can
set
\sigma^{N}_{0}=\operatorname{id}_{N}
, but do not
need
to do that.

Lemma 41
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N},J\in\bar{\mathbb{N}},\tau\in\mathcal{S}_{N}^{J}
and
\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{N}
. Then
\mu_{\tau\circ\pi}=\mu_{\tau}
, where
\tau\circ\pi=(\tau^{j}\circ\pi)_{j<J}
. In particular, the
J
-permuton
\mu_{\tau}
is completely determined by a
J-1
-dimensional permutation via

\mu_{\tau}=\mu_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\tau^{1}\circ(\tau^{0})^{-1},\tau^{2}\circ(\tau^{0})^{-1},\dots)}.

Proof 20.1
.

We have

\displaystyle\mu_{\tau\circ\pi}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\bigotimes_{j<J}\mathcal{U}\left(\frac{1}{N}[(\tau^{j}(\pi(k)))-1,\tau^{j}(\pi(k))]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\bigotimes_{j<J}\mathcal{U}\left(\frac{1}{N}[(\tau^{j}(k))-1,\tau^{j}(k)]\right),

where in the last step we permuted the summands by
\pi
(which leaves the sum unchanged).

Lemma 42
.

Let
N,J\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{J}_{N}
.
Then the following hold true.

(a)

F_{\hat{\mu}(\sigma^{-1})}(t)=\frac{1}{N}|\bigcap_{j<J}\sigma^{j}[Nt_{j}]|,\quad t\in[0,1]^{J}
,

(b)

\|F_{\mu_{\sigma}}-F_{\hat{\mu}_{\sigma}}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{J}{N}
.

Proof 20.2
.

(a)

Let
t=(t_{0},\dots,t_{J-1})\in[0,1]^{J}
. Then

\displaystyle NF_{\hat{\mu}_{\sigma^{-1}}}(t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\prod_{j=1}^{J}\mathds{1}_{[0,t_{j}]}\left(\frac{(\sigma^{j})^{-1}(k)}{N}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\{k\leq N:(\sigma^{j})^{-1}(k)\leq Nt_{j},j<J\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\{k\leq N:k=\sigma^{j}(l_{j}),l_{j}\leq Nt_{j},j<J\text{ for some }l_{1},\dots,l_{J}\leq N\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\bigcap_{j\leq J}\sigma^{j}[Nt_{j}]|.

(b)

Let
t\in[0,1]^{J}
. Then
12
12

12

In the second to last equality, the first colon is for the set builder notation
\{\nu:\phi(\nu)\}
and the other one is part of the
\forall
-quantifier.

\displaystyle F_{\mu_{\sigma}}(t)=

\displaystyle\mu_{\sigma}([0,t_{0}]\times\dots\times[0,t_{J-1}])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}\mathcal{U}\left(\frac{1}{N}[\sigma^{j}(k)-1,\sigma^{j}(k)]\right)(0,t_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}(\sigma^{j}(k)\wedge Nt_{j}-(\sigma^{j}(k)-1)\wedge Nt_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}|\{k\leq N:\forall j<J:\sigma^{j}(k)\leq\left\lfloor Nt_{j}\right\rfloor\}|+R_{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F_{\hat{\mu}_{\sigma}}(t)+R_{N},

where the first summand accounts for all the
1/N\times\dots\times 1/N
hypercubes fully contained in
[0,t_{1}]\times\dots\times[0,t_{J}]
and
R_{N}
accounts for the smaller rectangular hypercuboids still left over in
[0,t_{1}]\times\dots\times[0,t_{J}]
. More precisely,
consider
K=\{k\leq N:\exists j\leq J:\sigma^{j}(k)=\left\lfloor Nt_{j}\right\rfloor+1\}
. This is the image of the map
[J]\to K,j\mapsto(\sigma^{j})^{-1}(\left\lfloor Nt_{j}\right\rfloor+1)
and so
|K|\leq J
. Thus,

R_{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\in K}\prod_{j=1}^{J}(\sigma^{j}(k)\wedge Nt_{j}-(\sigma^{j}(k)-1)\wedge Nt_{j})\leq\frac{|K|}{N}\leq\frac{J}{N}.

Lemma
42
(b) says that the Kolmogorov-Smirnov distance of
\mu_{\sigma}
and
\hat{\mu}_{\sigma}
is bounded by
J/N
. This cannot be improved to a bound on, say, the total variation distance, since
\mu_{\sigma}(\operatorname{supp}\hat{\mu}_{\sigma})=0
.

Lemma 43
.

Let
J\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
. Suppose we are given a sequence of
J
-permutons
(\nu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and a
J
-permuton
\nu
. Then
\nu_{n}\to\nu
weakly if and only if

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{J}2^{-j}\|F^{1\dots j}_{\nu_{n}}-F^{1\dots j}_{\nu}\|_{\infty}=0.

(60)

Proof 20.3
.

For
J<\infty
, we have
\nu_{n}\to\nu
weakly if and only if
F_{\nu_{n}}\to F_{\nu}
in
\mathcal{C}([0,1]^{J},[0,1],\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
, by
11
Proposition 2.1, proving the finite dimensional case.

Now, suppose
J=\infty
.
Observe that as a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
, weak convergence of probability measures on
[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
already follows from the convergence of their finite-dimensional distributions.
The following defines a metric
d
on the product space
\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}([0,1]^{j},[0,1])

d((f_{j})_{J\in\mathbb{N}},(g_{j})_{J\in\mathbb{N}})=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-J}\|f_{j}-g_{j}\|_{\infty},

which induces the product topology. Thus, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\nu_{n}\to\nu

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall j\in J:\nu_{n}^{1\dots j}\to\nu^{1\dots j}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall j\in J:F_{\nu_{n}}^{1\dots j}\to F_{\nu}^{1\dots j}\text{ in }\mathcal{C},

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow d((F_{\nu_{n}}^{1\dots j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}},(F_{\nu}^{1\dots j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}})\to 0.

So far we considered the convergence of a given sequence of random permutations to some permuton. Next, we want to reverse this process.
Specifically, Let
J\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}([0,1]^{J})
be a
J
-permuton.
Our aim is to construct a sequence of random
J
-dimensional permutations
\sigma=(\sigma_{N}:\Omega\to\mathcal{S}_{N}^{J})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
such that the associated sequence of
J
-permutons converges to
\mu
.

To this end, consider an i.i.d. sequence
(U_{N}:\Omega\to[0,1]^{J})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
with
U_{N}\sim\mu
. In other words, we consider a (possibly infinite) matrix of random variables uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
:

\begin{pmatrix}U^{1}_{1}&U_{1}^{2}&\dots&U_{1}^{j}&\dots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\dots\\
U_{N}^{1}&U_{N}^{2}&\dots&U_{N}^{j}&\dots\end{pmatrix}

(61)

Here, the rows are independent realizations of a discrete-time permuton process, that is a process with uniform marginals
13
13

13

Here, the marginals are uniform on
[0,1]
. In some other works permuton processes have uniform marginals on
[-1,1]
. The difference is mostly cosmetic.
, with distribution
\mu
.

Given any
N\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
define
\operatorname{Perm}(v)\in\mathcal{S}_{N}
by

\operatorname{Perm}(v)(k)=1+|\{l\leq N:v_{l}<v_{k}\}|+|\{l<k:v_{l}=v_{k}\}|,\quad k\leq N.

(62)

In other words,
\operatorname{Perm}(v)(k)
is the (stable) rank of
v_{k}
in the vector
v
. Thus,
\operatorname{Perm}(v)
sorts the vector
v
from lowest to highest. Entries that are tied (equal) are ranked according to their original position. This ensures that
\operatorname{Perm}(v)
is indeed a permutation.

Now, set
\sigma^{j}_{N}:=\operatorname{Perm}(U^{j}_{1},\dots U^{j}_{N}),j<J
.
Because
\mathbb{P}(U^{j}_{l}=U^{j}_{k})=0
for all
l,k<N
, we have

\sigma^{j}_{N}(k)=1+|\{l\leq N:U_{l}^{j}<U_{k}^{j}\}|,\quad a.s.,j<J,k\leq N.

Given
v\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
we write
v_{(k)}=v_{\operatorname{Perm}(v)(k)}
. Then
v_{(1)}\leq\dots\leq v_{(N)}
. Consider independent
U_{1},\dots,U_{N}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
. Then, because the sequence is exchangeable, we have
\mathbb{P}(U_{1}=U_{(n)})=\dots=\mathbb{P}(U_{N}=U_{(n)})
for all
n\leq N
. Thus,
\mathbb{P}(U_{m}=U_{(n)})=\frac{1}{N}
for all
m,n\leq N
. In other words,
\operatorname{Perm}(U_{1},\dots,U_{N})\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathcal{S}_{N})
.

Consequently,
\sigma^{j}_{N}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathcal{S}_{N})
for all
j<J
. Aside from the marginals, the joint distribution of
(\sigma^{j}_{N})_{j<J}
is determined by
\mu
. In fact, the random
J
-dimensional permutations
\sigma_{N}
approximate
\mu
in the following sense.

Proposition 44
.

Let
J\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mu
be a
J
-dimensional permuton,
(U_{N}:\Omega\to[0,1]^{J})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
an i.i.d. sequence with
U_{N}\sim\mu
and let
\sigma=(\sigma_{N}:\Omega\to\mathcal{S}_{N}^{J})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
be defined by

\sigma^{j}_{N}:=\operatorname{Perm}(U^{j}_{1},\dots U^{j}_{N}),\quad j<J,N\in\mathbb{N}.

Then

\mathbb{P}(\|F_{\mu(\sigma_{N})}-F_{\mu}\|_{\infty}>4JN^{-1/4})\leq e^{-\sqrt{N}},

for large
N
.

Proof 20.4
.

This was proven by
11
. More precisely, this exact estimate is found at the end of their proof of that proposition.

Proposition 45
.

Let
J\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
,
\mu
be a
J
-dimensional permuton,
(U_{N}:\Omega\to[0,1]^{J})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
an i.i.d. sequence with
U_{N}\sim\mu
and let
\sigma=(\sigma_{N}:\Omega\to\mathcal{S}_{N}^{J})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
be defined by

\sigma^{j}_{N}:=\operatorname{Perm}(U^{j}_{1},\dots U^{j}_{N}),\quad j<J,N\in\mathbb{N}.

Then

\mu_{\sigma_{N}}\to\mu,\quad a.s.,

as
N\to\infty
.

Proof 20.5
.

Consider the case
J<\infty
first. For some sufficiently large
N_{0}
we have

\sum_{N=N_{0}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\|F_{\mu(\sigma_{N})}-F_{\mu}\|_{\infty}>4JN^{-1/4})\leq\sum_{N=N_{0}}^{\infty}e^{-\sqrt{N}}<\infty.

Thus, by Borel-Cantelli

\|F_{\mu(\sigma_{N})}-F_{\mu}\|_{\infty}\leq 4JN^{-1/4}\text{ for large }N,\quad a.s.

In particular,
\mu_{\sigma_{N}}\to\mu,a.s.
, as
N\to\infty
.

In the case
J=\infty
the convergence
\mu_{\sigma_{N}}\to\mu,a.s.
follows from the convergence of the finite-dimensional distributions
\mu_{\sigma_{N}}^{1\dots J}\to\mu^{1\dots J},a.s.,J\in\mathbb{N}
, as
N\to\infty
.

20.2
Constructing examples

Lemma 46
.

Let
Z:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be a random variable and
\varphi
be an increasing function. Then
\varphi(Z)\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
if and only if

\varphi(t)=F_{Z}(t):=\mathbb{P}(Z\leq t),\quad\mathbb{P}_{Z}\text{-}a.s.

Similarly, if
\psi
is decreasing, then
\psi(Z)\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
if and only if

\psi(t)=1-F_{Z}(t)=\mathbb{P}(Z<t),\quad\mathbb{P}_{Z}\text{-}a.s.

Proof 20.6
.

Assume
\varphi(Z)\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
. Since
\varphi
is increasing, the generalized inverse exists
\varphi^{-1}(t)=\inf\{z\in\mathbb{R}:\varphi(z)\geq t\}
and is increasing. We have

t=\mathbb{P}(\varphi(Z)\leq t)=\mathbb{P}(Z\leq\varphi^{-1}(t))=F_{Z}(\varphi^{-1}(t)),\quad t\geq 0.

This implies
\varphi^{-1}=F_{Z}^{-1}
, Lebesgue almost everywhere, and so
\varphi=F_{Z}
,
\mathbb{P}_{Z}
-almost surely.
The converse is well known. The statement for decreasing
\psi
is proven similarly (by duality).

Lemma 47
.

Let
U_{1},\dots,U_{m}
be random variables with
U_{1},\dots,U_{m}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

U_{1},\dots,U_{m}
are independent,

(ii)

\mathbb{P}_{(U_{1},\dots,U_{m})}=\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])^{\otimes m}
,

(iii)

\mathbb{P}(U_{1}\leq t_{1},\dots,U_{m}\leq t_{m})=\prod_{j=1}^{m}t_{j},\quad t\in[0,1]^{m}
,

Proof 20.7
.

Straightforward.

Lemma 48
.

Let
U_{1},\dots,U_{m}
be random variables with
U_{1},\dots,U_{m}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

U_{1}=\dots=U_{m},a.s.
,

(ii)

\mathbb{P}(U_{1}\leq t_{1},\dots,U_{m}\leq t_{m})=t_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge t_{m},\quad t\in[0,1]^{m}
,,

Proof 20.8
.

We apply Theorem 2.5.7 by
16
. It implies that (ii) is equivalent to the following statement: there exists a probability space
(\Omega^{\prime},\mathcal{F}_{\Omega^{\prime}},\mathbb{P}^{\prime})
, a random variable
Z:\Omega^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
and increasing functions
\varphi_{j}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{E},j=1,\dots,m
, such that

\mathbb{P}_{(U_{1},\dots,U_{m})}=\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{(\varphi_{1}(Z),\dots,\varphi_{m}(Z))}.

Since
\varphi_{j}(Z)
is uniform, we must have
\varphi_{j}(t)=\mathbb{P}(Z\leq t),\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{Z}\text{-}a.s
by Lemma
46
. Hence, (ii) is equivalent to
U_{1}=\dots=U_{m},\mathbb{P}\text{-}a.s.

Lemma 49
.

Let
U,V
be random variables with
U,V\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

U=1-V,a.s.
,

(ii)

\mathbb{P}(U\leq s,V\leq t)=\mathcal{W}(s,t):=(s+t-1)\vee 0
.

Proof 20.9
.

We apply Theorem 2.5.13 by
16
. It implies that (ii) is equivalent to the following statement: there exists a probability space
(\Omega^{\prime},\mathcal{F}_{\Omega^{\prime}},\mathbb{P}^{\prime})
, a random variable
Z:\Omega^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
an increasing function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and a decreasing function
\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, such that

\mathbb{P}_{(U,V)}=\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{(\varphi(Z),\psi(Z))}.

Since
\varphi(Z),\psi(Z)
are uniform, we must have

\varphi(t)=\mathbb{P}(Z\leq t)\text{ and }\psi(t)=\mathbb{P}(Z>t)=1-\mathbb{P}(Z\leq t),\quad\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{Z}\text{-}a.s,

by Lemma
46
. Hence, (ii) is equivalent to
U=1-V,\mathbb{P}\text{-}a.s.

Example 20.10
.

(a)

Consider
\mu
given by the comonotonicity copula

F_{\mu}(t)=t_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge t_{m},\quad t\in[0,1]^{m},m<J

Given a random variable
U\in\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
we have
(U,\dots,U)\sim\mu^{1\dots m}
. Conversely, if
(U_{1},\dots,U_{m})\sim\mu^{1\dots m}
, then
U_{1}=\dots=U_{m}
almost surely by Lemma
48
. Thus, up to almost sure equality, the matrix (
61
) is given by

\begin{pmatrix}U_{1}&U_{1}&\dots&U_{1}&\dots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\dots\\
U_{N}&U_{N}&\dots&U_{N}&\dots\end{pmatrix}

Then
\sigma_{N}^{1}=\sigma_{N}^{2}=\dots=\operatorname{Perm}(U_{1},\dots,U_{N})\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathcal{S}_{N})
, and so

\mu_{\sigma_{N}}=\mu_{(\sigma_{N}^{1})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}}=\mu_{(\operatorname{id}_{N})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}}

by Lemma
41
.
Thus,
\mu_{(\operatorname{id}_{N})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}}\to\mu,a.s.,
as
N\to\infty
, by Proposition
45
.

(b)

Consider
\mu={\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])}^{\otimes J}
, i.e. the
J
-fold product of the Lebesgue measure on
[0,1
] with itself. Its distribution function is the independence copula

F_{\mu}(t)=\prod_{j=1}^{m}t_{j},\quad t\in[0,1]^{m},m<J.

Suppose we are given
U_{1},\dots,U_{m}\in\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
. Then
(U_{1},\dots,U_{m})\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])^{\otimes m}
if and only if
U_{1},\dots,U_{m}
are independent by Lemma
47
.
Thus, the matrix (
61
) consists entirely of i.i.d. random variables. Hence,
(\sigma^{j}_{N})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is i.i.d. with
\sigma^{j}_{N}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathcal{S}_{N})
and
\mu_{(\sigma^{j}_{N})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}}\to\mu,a.s.,
as
N\to\infty
, by Proposition
45
.

(c)

Consider random variables
U,V\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
and the countermonotonicity copula

\mathcal{W}(s,t)=(s+t-1)\vee 0,\quad s,t\in[0,1].

We have
(U,V)\sim\mathcal{W}
if and only if
U=1-V,a.s.
by Lemma
49
. Thus, for
J=2
the matrix (
61
) is almost surely given by

\begin{pmatrix}U_{1}&1-U_{1}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
U_{N}&1-U_{N}\end{pmatrix}

Since
(1-U_{1},\dots,1-U_{N})
is almost surely in reverse order compared to
(U_{1},\dots,U_{N})
, we have
\sigma^{1}_{N}=\tau_{N}\circ\sigma^{0}_{N},a.s.
, where
\tau_{N}(k)=N-k+1
is the
reversal permutation
of size
N
. By Proposition
45
we have

\mu_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\tau_{N})}=\mu_{(\sigma^{0}_{N},\tau_{N}\circ\sigma^{0}_{N})}\to\mathbb{P}_{(U,1-U)},a.s.,

as
N\to\infty
, where
U\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
.

Reversing the order twice leaves you with the original order. So there is no direct higher-dimensional analogue to
\mathcal{W}
. Next, we look at two simple generalizations of
\mathcal{W}
to infinite dimensions.

(d)

Given
U\in\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
, the sequence
(U,1-U,U,1-U,\dots)
has the distribution
\mu
with

F_{\mu}(t_{1},\dots,t_{2m})=\mathcal{W}(t_{1},t_{2})\wedge\dots\wedge\mathcal{W}(t_{2m-1},t_{2m}),\quad t\in[0,1]^{2m},m\in\mathbb{N},

the matrix (
61
) is almost surely given by

\begin{pmatrix}U_{1}&1-U_{1}&U_{1}&1-U_{1}&\dots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\dots\\
U_{N}&1-U_{N}&U_{N}&1-U_{N}&\dots\end{pmatrix},

\sigma^{2j}_{N}=\sigma^{0}_{N},a.s.
and
\sigma^{2j+1}_{N}=\tau_{N}\circ\sigma^{0}_{N},a.s.
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence,

\mu_{(\operatorname{id}_{N},\tau_{N},\operatorname{id}_{N},\tau_{N},\dots)}=\mu_{(\sigma^{0}_{N},\tau_{N}\circ\sigma^{0}_{N},\sigma^{0}_{N},\tau_{N}\circ\sigma^{0}_{N},\dots)}\to\mu,a.s.,

as
N\to\infty
.

(e)

Given an i.i.d. sequence
(U^{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
with
U^{j}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
, the sequence
(U^{1},1-U^{1},U^{2},1-U^{2},\dots)
has the distribution
\mu
with

F_{\mu}(t_{1},\dots,t_{2m})=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{W}(t_{2j-1},t_{2j}),\quad t\in[0,1]^{2m},m\in\mathbb{N},

the matrix (
61
) is almost surely given by

\begin{pmatrix}U_{1}^{1}&1-U_{1}^{1}&U_{1}^{2}&1-U_{1}^{2}&\dots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\dots\\
U_{N}^{1}&1-U_{N}^{1}&U_{N}^{2}&1-U_{N}^{2}&\dots\end{pmatrix},

and
\sigma^{2j+1}_{N}=\tau_{N}\circ\sigma^{2j}_{N},a.s.
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence,

\mu_{(\sigma^{0}_{N},\tau_{N}\circ\sigma^{0}_{N},\sigma^{2}_{N},\tau_{N}\circ\sigma^{2}_{N},\dots)}=\mu_{(\sigma^{j}_{N})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}}\to\mu,a.s.,

as
N\to\infty
.

The set of
\infty
-permutons is much richer than the examples so far indicate. To illustrate this point, we discuss
Archimedean copulas
next.

A function
\varphi:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
is called an
(additive) generator
if it is continuous, decreasing with
\varphi(0)=1,\lim_{t\to\infty}\varphi(t)=0
, and strictly decreasing on
[0,t_{0}]
with
t_{0}=\inf\{t>0:\varphi(t)=0\}
. The
pseudo-inverse

\varphi^{(-1)}:[0,1]\to[0,\infty)
of a generator
\varphi
is defined by

\varphi^{(-1)}(t)=\begin{cases}\varphi^{-1}(t),&t\in(0,1],\\
t_{0},&t=0.\end{cases}

Note that
\varphi^{(-1)}(\varphi(t))=t\wedge t_{0},t\geq 0
.

A function
\varphi:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
is
completely monotone
if it is continuous with
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((0,\infty))
, and
(-1)^{k}f^{(k)}(x)\geq 0
for all
x>0
.

Proposition 50
.

Let
\varphi:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
be an additive generator. Then
\varphi
is completely monotone if and only if

C(t)=\varphi\left(\sum_{k=1}^{|t|}\varphi^{(-1)}(t_{k})\right),\quad t\in[0,1]^{*}

defines an
\infty
-copula.

Proof 20.11
.

This is immediate by Corollary 6.5.14 by
16
.

In the setting of Proposition
50
, the
\infty
-copula
C
is called
Archimedean
with generator
\varphi
.

Example 20.12
.

The following are well-known examples of (families of) completely monotone generators and their associated Archimedean
\infty
-copulas.

Family

\varphi^{(-1)}(u)

\varphi(v)

C(t)

\theta

Clayton

\frac{u^{-\theta}-1}{\theta}

(1+\theta v)^{-1/\theta}

\left(\sum_{k=1}^{|t|}(t_{k}^{-\theta}-1)+1\right)^{-1/\theta}

>0

Gumbel

(-\log u)^{\theta}

\exp(-v^{1/\theta})

\exp\left(-\left(\sum_{k=1}^{|t|}(-\log t_{k})^{\theta}\right)^{1/\theta}\right)

\geq 1

Frank

-\log\left(\frac{e^{-\theta u}-1}{e^{-\theta}-1}\right)

-\frac{1}{\theta}\log(1-(1-e^{-\theta})e^{-v})

-\frac{1}{\theta}\log\left(1+\frac{\prod_{k=1}^{|t|}(e^{-\theta t_{k}}-1)}{(e^{-\theta}-1)^{|t|-1}}\right)

>0

To reiterate, any of these examples define an
\infty
-permuton
\mu
and Proposition
45
defines a sequence of random infinite-dimensional permutations
(\sigma_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\mu_{\sigma_{N}}\to\mu
almost surely. After picking such a family one can conceivable tune the parameter
\theta
, such that the limiting process in main result Theorem
40
has whatever properties one desires more.

There are many other types of examples, including extreme-value copulas and elliptic copulas. Moreover, Sklar’s theorem lets us turn
any
joint distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{J}
with continuous marginals into a unique
J
-copula. Many of these examples and Sklar’s theorem can be potentially generalized directly to the case
J=\infty
. Alternatively, one can combine finite-dimensional examples by independence (just putting independent vectors together) or using the Markov product of copulas (essentially the gluing operation from optimal transport theory). For more information we refer to
16
.

There are also numerous examples that come from studying large permutations, such as the limits of sequences of square permutations. Other examples, such as the limit of the Baxter permutations, cannot be used directly because the limit
\mu
is
random
. However, in principle one can always take the intensity measure

\mathbb{E}\mu:\mathcal{F}\to[0,1],A\mapsto\mathbb{E}[\mu(A)]

as an example instead. Although note that the sequence of permutations converging to
\mathbb{E}\mu
is in general different from the one for
\mu
. See
12
for more on these topics.

Finally, note that even
\mu_{\tau}
for some
J
-dimensional permutation
\tau\in\mathcal{S}_{N}^{J}
is a legitimate example for the limit
\mu
in (A
40
). The so called
shuffles of Min
provide another
“finite-permutation”
example
(see again
16
, for more information)
.

Remark 51
.

Even though the limiting process
W
in Theorem
40
depends only on the bivariate marginals
F_{\mu}^{ij}
, we insisted on discussing
J
-copulas also for
J>2
. This is because not all families of
2
-copulas
(C^{ij})_{i<j<J}
are
compatible
. We call a family
(C^{ij})_{i<j<J}
of
2
-copulas compatible if there exists a
J
-copula permuton
\mu
such that
F_{\mu}^{ij}=C^{ij},i<j<J
, that is if they are genuinely the bivariate marginals of some
J
-permuton or
J
-copula. For, the family

C^{12}(s,t)=\mathcal{W},C^{23}=\mathcal{W},C^{13}=\mathcal{W}

consisting of only the countermonotonicity copula is
not
compatible, that is there exists no
3
-permuton with these bivariate marginals. The reason is if
U_{1},U_{2},U_{3}
are uniform, such that
(U_{1},U_{2})\sim\mathcal{W}
and
(U_{2},U_{3})\sim\mathcal{W}
, then
U_{1}=1-U_{2}=U_{3},a.s.
, so
(U_{1},U_{3})\nsim\mathcal{W}
.
From the viewpoint of permutations the idea is: if you reverse the order twice, you end up with the original order (the reversal permutation satisfies
\tau_{N}\circ\tau_{N}=\operatorname{id}_{N}
).

21
Proof of the main theorem

First, let us note some consequences of Assumption (A
40
).

Lemma 52
.

Assume (A
40
) holds true. Then

\|F_{\mu_{N}}^{a}-F_{\mu}^{a}\|_{\infty}\to 0,\text{in probability},

as
N\to\infty
, for all
a\in\{j\in\mathbb{N}:j<J\}^{*}
.

Proof 21.1
.

Because
\mu_{N}\to\mu
in probability we also have

\sum_{j=1}^{J}2^{-j}\|F^{1\dots j}_{\mu_{N}}-F^{1\dots j}_{\mu}\|_{\infty}\to 0,\text{in probability},

as
N\to\infty
, using Lemma
43
.

Corollary 53
.

Assume (A
40
) holds true. Then

\|\hat{F}_{\mu_{N}}^{a}-F_{\mu}^{a}\|_{\infty}\to 0,\text{in probability},

as
N\to\infty
, for all
a\in\{j\in\mathbb{N}:j<J\}^{*}
.

Proof 21.2
.

Combine Lemma
42
(b) with Lemma
52
.

In the following we also denote distributions with the more traditional notation
\mathbb{P}_{Z}:=Z\mathbb{P}
and we write
\mathbb{P}^{N}_{X}:=\mathbb{P}_{X_{N}}
.
To prove the convergence result in Theorem
40
it is sufficient to show two things:

(a)

The family
\left(\mathbb{P}^{N}_{X}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight.

(b)

The finite-dimensional distributions of
\mathbb{P}^{N}_{X}
converge to those of
\mathbb{P}_{W}
, that is, given
t_{1},\ldots,t_{M}\in[0,1]
and
j<J
we have

\begin{pmatrix}X_{t_{1}}^{N,0}&\cdots&X_{t_{1}}^{N,j}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
X_{t_{M}}^{N,0}&\cdots&X_{t_{M}}^{N,j}\end{pmatrix}\Rightarrow\begin{pmatrix}W_{t_{1}}^{0}&\cdots&W_{t_{1}}^{j}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
W_{t_{M}}^{0}&\cdots&W_{t_{M}}^{j}\end{pmatrix}

as
N\rightarrow\infty
.
Here and in the following,
W
is the stochastic process introduced in Theorem
40
.

Our approach for showing tightness is similar to the proof of Theorem 14.1. in
8
.

Lemma 54
.

The sequence
\left(\mathbb{P}^{N}_{X}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight.

Proof 21.3
.

Given
j<J
the tightness of
(\mathbb{P}_{X_{N}^{j}})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
follows from the usual argument for Donsker’s theorem. Here it is for completeness:
For
0\leq s\leq t\leq 1
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|X_{t}^{N,j}-X_{s}^{N,j}\right|^{2}\mid\sigma_{N}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\sum_{k=\lfloor Ns\rfloor+1}^{\lfloor Nt\rfloor}Z_{\sigma^{j}_{N}(k)}\right|^{2}\mid\sigma_{N}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{k=\lfloor Ns\rfloor+1}^{\lfloor Nt\rfloor}Z_{\sigma^{j}_{N}(k)}^{2}\mid\sigma_{N}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}(\lfloor Nt\rfloor-\lfloor Ns\rfloor).

Fix
0\leq s\leq u\leq t\leq 1
. Note that
X^{N,j}
has independent increments conditional on
\sigma_{N}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|X_{u}^{N,j}-X_{s}^{N,j}|^{2}|X_{t}^{N,j}-X_{u}^{N,j}|^{2}|\sigma_{N}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}(\lfloor Nu\rfloor-\lfloor Ns\rfloor)(\lfloor Nt\rfloor-\lfloor Nu\rfloor)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\lfloor Nt\rfloor-\lfloor Ns\rfloor}{N}\right)^{2}.

For
t-s\geq\frac{1}{N}
the RHS is bounded by
4(t-s)^{2}
. Otherwise
\lfloor Nu\rfloor=\lfloor Ns\rfloor
or
\lfloor Nt\rfloor=\lfloor Nu\rfloor
and the LHS of the inequality vanishes. By Theorem 13.5. in
8
(with
\alpha=\beta=1
and
F(t)=2t
) the sequence of projected measures
(\mathbb{P}_{X_{N}^{j}})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
on
\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
is tight, for all
j<J
.

Suppose
J<\infty
and let
\varepsilon>0
. Then the tightness of
(\mathbb{P}_{X_{N}^{j}})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
for all
j<J
implies there exist compacts
K_{1},\ldots K_{J}\subseteq\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
, such that
\mu_{1}^{N}\left(K_{1}^{c}\right)\ldots\mu_{J}^{N}\left(K_{J}^{c}\right)\leq\varepsilon/J
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
. By Tychonoff’s theorem, the product
K_{1}\times\dots K_{J}
is compact in
\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})^{J}
, and

\mathbb{P}_{X}^{N}((K_{1}\times\cdots\times K_{J})^{c})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{P}_{X}^{N}(\mathbb{R}\times\cdots\times K_{j}^{c}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R})\leq\varepsilon

for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})^{J}
is homeomorphic to
\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{J})
, the sequence
\left(\mathbb{P}^{N}_{X}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight as well.

Now, suppose
J=\infty
. For every finite sequence
a\in\mathbb{R}^{j}
we define the continuous function

\Pi_{a}:\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})\to\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})

by

(\Pi_{a}x)_{t}=\sum_{k=1}^{j}a_{k}x^{k}_{t},\quad t\geq 0.

and by abuse of notation we denote the map
\Pi_{a}:\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{j})\to\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
given by the same formula by the same symbol.
Given
a\in\mathbb{R}^{j}
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\Pi_{a}\mathbb{P}_{X}^{N}=\Pi_{a}\mathbb{P}_{X^{1\dots j}}^{N}
. Hence,
the family
(\Pi_{a}\mathbb{P}_{X}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
consists of pushfowards of a tight family of measures along a continuous map. Therefore, it is tight as well. By Mitoma’s criterion
(see
34
, Theorem 4.1)
we conclude
14
14

14

For the application of their theorem, we let
E
be the countable nuclear Hilbert space of finite sequences. Then its strong dual
E^{\prime}
is the space
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
(up to linear homeomorphism)
that the family
(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight.

To prove the convergence of the finite-dimensional distributions we use the following central limit theorem.

Proposition 55
.

Let
(\xi_{k}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N},k\leq N}
be a triangular array of centered
\mathbb{R}
-valued random variables. For every
N\in\mathbb{N}
define

V_{N}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\operatorname{Var}(\xi_{k}^{N}).

Assume

(a)

|V_{N}|\to\infty
, as
N\to\infty
,

(b)

\mathbb{E}[\xi_{k}^{N}\sum_{l\neq k}^{N}\xi_{l}^{N}|\sum_{l\neq k}^{N}\xi_{l}^{N}]\geq 0,a.s.
, for all
k\leq N\in\mathbb{N}
,

(c)

\sum_{k,l}\operatorname{Cov}((\xi^{N}_{k})^{2},(\xi^{N}_{l})^{2})=o(|V_{N}|^{2})
,

(d)

\sum_{k,l}\operatorname{Cov}((\xi^{N}_{k}),(\xi^{N}_{l}))=o(|V_{N}|)
.

Then
V_{N}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{k}^{N}\to\mathcal{N}(0,1)
in distribution, as
N\to\infty
.

Proof 21.4
.

See
15
Corollary 1.

In the proof of the next lemma we work with random arrays. Given
d,e\in\mathbb{N}^{*},X:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{\Pi d}
and
Y:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{\Pi e}
we define the (cross-) covariance
\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)\in\mathbb{R}^{(\Pi e)\times(\Pi d)}
by

\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)_{ij}=\operatorname{Cov}(X_{i},Y_{j}),\quad i\leq d,j\leq e

provided
\mathbb{E}[|X_{i}Y_{j}|^{2}|]<\infty
for all
i\leq|d|,j\leq|e|
.
In similar vein one defines conditional (cross-) covariance. Note that given
A\in\mathbb{R}^{\Pi(df)}
and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{\Pi(eg)}
, we have

\operatorname{Cov}(\langle X,A\rangle,\langle Y,B\rangle)=\langle\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y),A\otimes B\rangle\in\mathbb{R},

(63)

provided all relevant terms are defined. In particular, if
X,Y
are real-valued we have

\operatorname{Cov}(XA,YB)=\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)(A\otimes B).

Analogous properties hold true for conditional covariance.

Lemma 56
.

For all
t_{1},\ldots,t_{M}\in[0,1]
and
j<J
we have convergence

\begin{pmatrix}X_{t_{1}}^{0}&\cdots&X_{t_{1}}^{j}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
X_{t_{M}}^{0}&\cdots&X_{t_{M}}^{j}\end{pmatrix}\Rightarrow\begin{pmatrix}W_{t_{1}}^{0}&\cdots&W_{t_{1}}^{j}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
W_{t_{M}}^{0}&\cdots&W_{t_{M}}^{j}\end{pmatrix}

as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

Proof 21.5
.

Fix
t_{1},\ldots,t_{M}\in[0,1]
. We fix
J\in\mathbb{N}
and write
\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2},\dots,\sigma^{J}
instead of
\sigma^{0},\sigma^{1},\dots,\sigma^{J-1}
(and similarly for
X
and
W
) in this proof to simplify the notation.
By a variation of the Cramér-Wold theorem (cf.
24
Corollary 6.5) it suffices to prove

\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{J}u_{m}v_{j}S_{\left\lfloor Nt_{m}\right\rfloor}^{N,\sigma^{j}}\Rightarrow\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{J}u_{m}v_{j}W_{t_{m}}^{j}

as
N\rightarrow\infty
, for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{J}
. To this end we want to apply Proposition
55
.
Fix
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{J}
and assume wlog
|u|=|v|=1
. We have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{J}u_{m}v_{j}S_{\left\lfloor Nt_{m}\right\rfloor}^{N,\sigma^{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{J}u_{m}v_{j}\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt_{m}]}(k)\right)Z_{k}.

Given
k\leq N\in\mathbb{N}
define the matrix
A^{N}(k)\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times J}
by

A_{m,j}^{N}(k)=\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt_{m}]}(k),\quad m\leq M,j\leq J

and set

\xi_{k}^{N}=\langle A^{N}(k),u\otimes v\rangle Z_{k},\quad k\leq N\in\mathbb{N},

so that we may write

\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{J}u_{m}v_{j}S_{\left\lfloor Nt_{m}\right\rfloor}^{N,\sigma^{j}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{k}^{N}.

By Equation (
63
)

\operatorname{Cov}(\xi_{k}^{N},\xi_{l}^{N})=\langle\operatorname{Cov}(A^{N}(k)Z_{k},A^{N}(l)Z_{l}),u\otimes v\otimes u\otimes v\rangle

and by the law of total (cross-) covariance

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}(A^{N}(k)Z_{k},A^{N}(l)Z_{l})=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Cov}(A^{N}(k)Z_{k},A^{N}(l)Z_{l}|\sigma_{N})]

\displaystyle+\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbb{E}[A^{N}(k)Z_{k}|\sigma_{N}],\mathbb{E}[A^{N}(l)Z_{l}|\sigma_{N}])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 2}]\delta_{k,l}.

Hence, the sequence
(\xi_{k}^{N})_{k\leq N}
is pairwise uncorrelated for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 2}]_{m,j,m^{\prime},j^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt_{m}]}(k)\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{j^{\prime}}_{N}[Nt_{m^{\prime}}]}(k)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt_{m}]\cap\sigma^{j^{\prime}}_{N}[Nt_{m^{\prime}}]}(k)],

and so

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{k}^{N}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sum_{m,m^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}}u_{m}u_{m^{\prime}}v_{j}v_{j^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt_{m}]\cap\sigma^{j^{\prime}}_{N}[Nt_{m^{\prime}}]}(k)]

\displaystyle=
