\displaystyle\sum_{m,m^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}}u_{m}u_{m^{\prime}}v_{j}v_{j^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\hat{F}^{jj^{\prime}}_{N}(t_{m},t_{m}^{\prime}).

By Corollary
53
and since any family of joint CDFs is bounded by
1

\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{Var}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{k}^{N}\right)=\sum_{m,m^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}}u_{m}u_{m^{\prime}}v_{j}v_{j^{\prime}}F^{j,j^{\prime}}_{\mu}(t_{m},t_{m}^{\prime}).

(64)

Using the identity

\langle A,B\rangle\langle C,D\rangle=\langle A\otimes C,B\otimes D\rangle,\quad A,B,C,D\in\mathbb{R}^{\Pi d}

we further have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}((\xi_{k}^{N})^{2},(\xi_{l}^{N})^{2})=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}(\langle A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 2}Z_{k}^{2},(u\otimes v)^{\otimes 2}\rangle,\langle A^{N}(l)^{\otimes 2}Z_{l}^{2},(u\otimes v)^{\otimes 2}\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\operatorname{Cov}(A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 2}Z_{k}^{2},A^{N}(l)^{\otimes 2}Z_{l}^{2}),(u\otimes v)^{\otimes 4})\rangle,

where

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}(A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 2}Z_{k}^{2},A^{N}(l)^{\otimes 2}Z_{l}^{2})=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Cov}(A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 2}Z_{k}^{2},A^{N}(l)^{\otimes 2}Z_{l}^{2}|\sigma_{N})]

\displaystyle+\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbb{E}[A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 2}Z_{k}^{2}|\sigma_{N}],\mathbb{E}[A^{N}(l)^{\otimes 2}Z_{l}^{2}|\sigma_{N}])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Cov}(Z_{k}^{2},Z_{l}^{2})A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 2}\otimes A^{N}(l)^{\otimes 2}]

\displaystyle+\operatorname{Cov}(A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 2},A^{N}(l)^{\otimes 2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 4}]\mathbb{E}[Z_{1}^{4}]\delta_{k,l}+\operatorname{Cov}(A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 2},A^{N}(l)^{\otimes 2}).

Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l=1}^{N}\operatorname{Cov}((\xi_{k}^{N})^{2},(\xi_{l}^{N})^{2})=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z_{1}^{4}]\langle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}[A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 4}],(u\otimes v)^{\otimes 4}\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle\operatorname{Cov}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 2},\sum_{l=1}^{N}A^{N}(l)^{\otimes 2}\right),(u\otimes v)^{\otimes 4}\rangle.

(65)

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the estimates

\displaystyle|A^{N}(k)|=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{m,j=1}^{M,J}\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt_{m}]}(k)\right)^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{MJ},

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}[A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 4}]|\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|A^{N}(k)|^{4}]\leq M^{2}J^{2},

we see that the first summand in (
21.5
) is bounded by
\mathbb{E}[Z_{1}^{4}]NM^{2}J^{2}
. Regarding the second summand, observe that

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 2}_{m,j+1,m^{\prime},j^{\prime}+1}=\frac{1}{N}|\sigma^{j}_{N}[Nt_{m}]\cap\sigma^{j^{\prime}}_{N}[Nt_{m^{\prime}}]|=\hat{F}_{N}^{jj^{\prime}}(t_{m},t_{m^{\prime}}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 2},\sum_{l=1}^{N}A^{N}(l)^{\otimes 2}\right)_{m_{1},j_{1},m_{2},j_{2},m_{3},j_{3},m_{4},j_{4}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N^{2}\operatorname{Cov}(\hat{F}_{N}^{j_{1}j_{2}}(t_{m_{1}},t_{m_{2}}),\hat{F}_{N}^{j_{3}j_{4}}(t_{m_{3}},t_{m_{4}})).

Note that
\hat{F}_{N}^{ij}
converges to a deterministic function in probability by Corollary
53
, for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, the covariance
\operatorname{Cov}(\hat{F}_{N}^{j_{1}j_{2}}(t_{m_{1}},t_{m_{2}}),\hat{F}_{N}^{j_{3}j_{4}}(t_{m_{3}},t_{m_{4}}))
vanishes as
N\to\infty
and we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\operatorname{Cov}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}A^{N}(k)^{\otimes 2},\sum_{l=1}^{N}A^{N}(l)^{\otimes 2}\right)=0,

Hence, we can conclude

\sum_{k,l}\operatorname{Cov}((\xi_{k}^{N})^{2},(\xi_{l}^{N})^{2})=o(N^{2}),\quad N\to\infty.

Finally, recall that if
X
and
Y
are random variables and
\mathcal{F}
a
\sigma
-algebra, such that
X
is independent of
\sigma(Y,\mathcal{F})
, then

\mathbb{E}[XY|\mathcal{F}]=\mathbb{E}[X]\mathbb{E}[Y|\mathcal{F}],\text{a.s.}

Hence,

\mathbb{E}\left[\xi_{k}^{N}|\sum_{k\neq l}^{N}\xi_{l}^{N}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{k}\langle A^{N}(k),u\otimes v\rangle|\sum_{k\neq l}^{N}\xi_{l}^{N}\right]=0,\text{a.s.}

Thus, by Proposition
55
we have

\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{k}^{N}}{\left(\operatorname{Var}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{k}^{N}\right)\right)^{1/2}}\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}(0,1),\quad N\to\infty.

With (
64
) we conclude further

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{k}^{N}=

\displaystyle\left(\operatorname{Var}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{k}^{N}\right)\right)^{1/2}\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{k}^{N}}{\left(\operatorname{Var}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{k}^{N}\right)\right)^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\left(0,\sum_{m,m^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}}u_{m}u_{m^{\prime}}v_{j}v_{j^{\prime}}F^{jj^{\prime}}_{\mu}(t_{m},t_{m}^{\prime})\right)

weakly, as
N\to\infty
.
On the other hand, we also have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{J}u_{m}v_{j}W_{t_{m}}^{j}\right)=

\displaystyle\sum_{m,m^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}}u_{m}u_{m^{\prime}}v_{j}v_{j^{\prime}}\operatorname{Cov}(W_{t_{m}}^{j},W_{t_{m^{\prime}}}^{j^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m,m^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}}u_{m}u_{m^{\prime}}v_{j}v_{j^{\prime}}F^{jj^{\prime}}_{\mu}(t_{m},t_{m}^{\prime}).

Consequently, the result follows.

Proof 21.6

(Proof of Theorem
40
)
.

Lemmas
54
and
56
imply the weak convergence of
X
to
W
. The converse statement follows from Proposition
45
.

22
Epoched Brownian processes as scaling limits

Our main motivation for the theory in this chapter is to construct an analogue to Brownian motion in the approximation of SGD by an SDE for the finite-data without replacement case.
Specifically, SDEs driven by Brownian motion are weak approximation to one-pass SGD, where the data is an infinite i.i.d. sequence drawn from the population. If we are instead given a finite i.i.d. sequence, then the corresponding SDE driver is an
epoched Brownian motion
. The significance of the limiting process
W
in Theorem
40
is that its components are essentially the epochs of an epoched Brownian motion. Thus, epoched Brownian motions arise as limits of random walks with finitely many increments. After being used up in the first epoch, the are used again, perhaps (randomly) permuted.

In this section we want to show how epoched Brownian bridges arise as scaling limits as an application of Theorem
40
. More precisely, we want to prove the following statement.

Theorem 56
.

Suppose
(Z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is a sequence of i.i.d. random variables with
\mathbb{E}Z_{n}=0,\operatorname{Var}Z_{n}=1
and
\mathbb{E}[Z_{n}^{4}]<\infty
. Further, suppose we are given a sequence
(\sigma_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
of random infinite-dimensional permutations, independent of
(Z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, (where
\sigma_{N}^{j}
is defined on the set
\{0,\dots,N-1\}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
) such that (A
40
) holds true.
Define
15
15

15

We set
\sum_{k=a}^{b}\dots:=0
for
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
with
a>b
.

\tilde{X}_{t}^{N}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{k=0}^{\left\lfloor Nt\right\rfloor-1}Z_{\sigma_{N}^{\left\lfloor k/N\right\rfloor}(k\operatorname{mod}N)}-\frac{t}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}Z_{k},\quad t\geq 0,N\in\mathbb{N}.

Then there exists a jointly Gaussian family of Brownian bridges
(B^{j}:\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
from
0
to
0
, with

\operatorname{Cov}(B^{i}_{s},B^{j}_{t})=F_{\mu}^{ij}(s,t)-st,\quad i\neq j,s,t\in[0,1],

such that
\tilde{X}
converges in distribution to the centered Gaussian process
\tilde{B}:\Omega\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\tilde{B}_{t}=B_{\{t\}}^{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor},\quad t\geq 0.

Let
b>a>0
. The
J_{1}
-metric, which induces the topology of the Skorokhod space
\mathcal{D}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
, is given by

d_{J_{1}}(f,g)=\inf_{\lambda}(\|\lambda-\operatorname{id}_{[a,b]}\|_{\infty}\vee\|f-g\circ\lambda\|_{\infty}),\quad f,g\in\mathcal{D}([a,b],\mathbb{R}),

where the infimum is taken over all homeomorphisms
\lambda:[a,b]\to[a,b]
with
\lambda(a)=a
and
\lambda(b)=b
. We call these
time changes
for simplicity.
Thus, we can induce the topology on
\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
by equipping this space with the metric

d_{\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})}(f,g)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}(1\wedge d_{J_{1}}(f^{j},g^{j})),\quad f,g\in\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}).

Denote the subspace of
\mathcal{D}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
of all functions
f
with
f(a)=f(b)=0
by
\mathcal{D}_{0}([a,b])
. We call these functions
càdlàg loops
.
We can equip
\mathcal{D}_{0}([0,1])^{\mathbb{N}}
with the metric sharing the same formula as before

d_{\mathcal{D}_{0}([0,1])^{\mathbb{N}}}(f,g)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}(1\wedge d_{J_{1}}(f^{j},g^{j})),\quad f,g\in D_{0}^{\mathbb{N}}.

For
M\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
we define
\Phi_{M}:\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{M})\to\mathcal{D}_{0}([0,1])^{M}
by

(\Phi_{M}f)(t):=f(t)-f(0)-tf(1),\quad f\in\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{M}),t\geq 0,

(66)

and write
\Phi:=\Phi_{\infty}
.

Lemma 57
.

The function
\Phi:\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})\to\mathcal{D}_{0}([0,1])^{\mathbb{N}}
is Lipschitz.

Proof 22.1
.

First, note that
\Phi f=(\Phi_{1}f^{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. Certainly,
\Phi_{1}f^{j}
is càdlàg if
f
is, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. For any time change
\lambda:[0,1]\to[0,1]
we have

\displaystyle|\Phi_{1}f(t)-\Phi_{1}g(\lambda(t))|=

\displaystyle|f(t)-f(0)-tf(t)-(g(\lambda(t))-g(0)-tg(1))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|f(t)-g(\lambda(t))|+|f(0)-g(0)|+|f(1)-g(1)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 3d_{J_{1}}(f,g),\quad t\in[0,1].

Hence,
\Phi_{1}
is Lipschitz with constant
3
with respect to the
d_{J_{1}}
metric. Now, consider
\Phi
. We have

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})}(\Phi f,\Phi g)=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}(1\wedge d_{J_{1}}(\Phi_{1}f^{j},\Phi_{1}g^{j}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 3\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}(1\wedge d_{J_{1}}(f^{j},g^{j}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3d_{\mathcal{D}_{0}([0,1])^{\mathbb{N}}}(f,g),

for all
f,g\in\mathcal{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
.

Lemma 58
.

Let
a,b,c>0
with
a<b
, and define the shift operator

T_{c}:\mathcal{D}([a,b],\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{D}([a+c,b+c],\mathbb{R}),

by

T_{c}f(t)=f(t-c),\quad t\in[a+c,b+c].

Then
T_{c}
is an isometry, that is

d_{J_{1}}(f,g)=d_{J_{1}}(T_{c}f,T_{c}g),\quad f,g\in\mathcal{D}([a,b],\mathbb{R}).

Proof 22.2
.

Let
\lambda:[a,b]\to[a,b]
be a time change. Define

\lambda_{c}:[a+c,b+c]\to[a+c,b+c],t\mapsto c+\lambda(t-c).

Then
\lambda_{c}
well-defined, a homeomorphism,
\lambda_{c}(a+c)=a+c
and
\lambda_{c}(b+c)=b+c
.
Then

\sup_{t\in[a+c,b+c]}|\lambda_{c}(t)-t|=\sup_{t\in[a,b]}|c+\lambda(t)-(t+c)|=\sup_{t\in[a,b]}|\lambda(t)-t|,

and for all
f,g\in\mathcal{D}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[a+c,b+c]}|T_{c}f(t)-T_{c}g(\lambda_{c}(t))|=

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[a+c,b+c]}|f(t-c)-g(c+\lambda(t-c)-c)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[a,b]}|f(t)-g(\lambda(t))|.

Since
\lambda\mapsto\lambda_{c}
is a bijection between the sets of time changes, taking infima over
\lambda
and
\lambda_{c}
yields the isometry property.

Given
0\leq a\leq b\leq c
and functions
f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R},g:[b,c]\to\mathbb{R}
with
f(b)=g(b)
, we define their
concatenation

f\ast g:[a,c]\to\mathbb{R}
by

(f\ast g)(t)=\begin{cases}f(t),&t\in[a,b],\\
g(t),&t\in(b,c].\end{cases}

The condition
f(b)=g(b)
ensures that
f\ast g
is continuous at
b
.

Lemma 59
.

Let
0\leq a\leq b\leq c
. Then the concatenation operator for càdlàg loops

\ast:\mathcal{D}_{0}([a,b])\times\mathcal{D}_{0}([b,c])\to\mathcal{D}_{0}([a,c]),

is continuous.

Proof 22.3
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{0}([a,b])
and
g\in\mathcal{D}_{0}([b,c])
.
Consider time changes
\lambda_{f}:[a,b]\to[a,b]
and
\lambda_{g}:[b,c]\to[b,c]
. Then
\lambda_{f}\ast\lambda_{g}:[a,c]\to[a,c]
is a time change as well (continuity is preserved since the endpoints are fixed). Suppose
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are sequences in
\mathcal{D}_{0}([a,b])
and
g\in\mathcal{D}_{0}([b,c])
respectively, such that
f_{n}\to f
and
g_{n}\to g
,
n\to\infty
. Then

\displaystyle\|\lambda_{f}\ast\lambda_{g}-\operatorname{id}_{[a,c]}\|_{\infty}\vee\|f_{n}\ast g_{n}-(f\ast g)\circ(\lambda_{f}\ast\lambda_{g})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\lambda_{f}-\operatorname{id}_{[a,b]}\|_{\infty}\vee\|f_{n}-f\circ\lambda_{f}\|_{\infty}\vee\|\lambda_{g}-\operatorname{id}_{[b,c]}\|_{\infty}\vee\|g_{n}-g\circ\lambda_{g}\|_{\infty}.

Hence,

\lim_{n\to\infty}d_{J_{1}}(f_{n}\ast g_{n},f\ast g)=0,

as desired.

Lemma 60
.

The function
\Psi:\mathcal{D}_{0}([0,1])^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathcal{D}([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
given by

(\Psi f)(t)=f_{\{t\}}^{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor},\quad t\geq 0,f\in\mathcal{D}_{0}([0,1])^{\mathbb{N}}

is continuous.

Proof 22.4
.

Firstly, given
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, define
\Psi_{j}:\mathcal{D}_{0}([0,1])^{j}\to\mathcal{D}([0,j],\mathbb{R})
by

(\Psi_{j}f)(t)=f_{\{t\}}^{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor},\quad t\in[0,j],f\in\mathcal{D}_{0}([0,1])^{j}.

Then

\Psi_{j}=f^{0}\ast T_{1}f^{1}\ast\dots\ast T_{j-1}f^{j-1},

and so
\Psi_{j}
is continuous by Lemmas
58
and
59
.

Consider
f\in\Omega^{\mathbb{N}}
. Since
f^{j}
is càdlàg for all
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, so is
\Psi f
. No jumps occur at integer points
t\in\mathbb{N}
, since
f^{j}(0)=f^{j}(1)=0
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Note that the topology on
\mathcal{D}([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
is induced by the metric

d_{\mathcal{D}([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})}(f,g)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j}(1\wedge d_{J_{1}}(f|_{[0,j]},g|_{[0,j]})),\quad f,g\in\mathcal{D}([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}).

Note that
(\Psi f)|_{[0,j]}=\Psi_{j}f|_{[0,j]},j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
. There exists an
M\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}2^{-j}<\varepsilon/2
. Recall the definition of
\Phi_{M}
(Equation (
66
)). Since
\Phi_{M}
is continuous, there exists a neighborhood
V\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{0}([0,1])^{M}
of
f|_{[0,M]}
, such that

d_{J_{1}}(f|_{[0,M]},g)<\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\quad g\in V.

Further,
V
is the projection of a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{0}([0,1])^{\mathbb{N}}
. Then, for all
g\in U
we have

d(\Psi f,\Psi g)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j}(1\wedge d_{J_{1}}(\Psi_{j}f|_{[0,j]},\Psi_{j}g|_{[0,j]}))\leq\sum_{j=1}^{M}2^{-j}\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}2^{-j}<\varepsilon.

Hence,
\Psi
is continuous.

Proof 22.5

(Proof of Theorem
56
)
.

First, apply Theorem
40
to get the convergence
X^{N}\to W,N\to\infty
in distribution. Consider Lemma
57
. We have

\displaystyle\Phi(X^{N})_{j}(t)=

\displaystyle X^{N,j}_{t}-X_{0}^{N,j}-tX_{1}^{N,j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{k=0}^{\left\lfloor Nt\right\rfloor-1}Z_{\sigma_{N}^{j}(k)}-\frac{t}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}Z_{\sigma^{j}_{N}(k)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{k=0}^{\left\lfloor Nt\right\rfloor-1}Z_{\sigma_{N}^{j}(k)}-\frac{t}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}Z_{k},\quad j\in\mathbb{N}_{0},t\in[0,1],

Further,

\Phi(W)_{j}(t)=W_{t}^{j}-tW_{1}^{j},\quad j\in\mathbb{N}_{0},t\in[0,1].

defines a Brownian bridge from
0
to
0
.
The continuous mapping theorem applied with the continuous function from Lemma
57
implies

\Phi(X^{N})\to\Phi(W)=:B,

in distribution, as
N\to\infty
, where
B=(B^{j}:\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R})
is a jointly Gaussian family of Brownian bridges.
Next, we calculate

\displaystyle\sqrt{N}(\Psi\circ\Phi)(X^{N})(t)=

\displaystyle\sqrt{N}\Phi(X^{N})_{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor}(\{t\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\left\lfloor N\{t\}\right\rfloor-1}Z_{\sigma_{N}^{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor}(k)}-\{t\}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}Z_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=N\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor}^{\left\lfloor N\{t\}\right\rfloor-1+N\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor}Z_{\sigma_{N}^{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor}(k-N\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor)}-\{t\}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}Z_{k}\quad t\in[0,1],N\in\mathbb{N},

Using the identity
\left\lfloor Nt\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor N\{t\}\right\rfloor+N\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\geq 0
, we further obtain

\displaystyle\sqrt{N}(\Psi\circ\Phi)(X^{N})(t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=N\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor}^{\left\lfloor Nt\right\rfloor-1}Z_{\sigma_{N}^{\left\lfloor k/N\right\rfloor}(k\operatorname{mod}N)}+\sum_{k=0}^{N\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor-1}Z_{\sigma_{N}^{\left\lfloor k/N\right\rfloor}(k\operatorname{mod}N)}

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=0}^{N\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor-1}Z_{\sigma_{N}^{\left\lfloor k/N\right\rfloor}(k\operatorname{mod}N)}-\{t\}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}Z_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\left\lfloor Nt\right\rfloor-1}Z_{\sigma_{N}^{\left\lfloor k/N\right\rfloor}(k\operatorname{mod}N)}-\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}Z_{k}-\{t\}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}Z_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\left\lfloor Nt\right\rfloor-1}Z_{\sigma_{N}^{\left\lfloor k/N\right\rfloor}(k\operatorname{mod}N)}-t\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}Z_{k},\quad t\in[0,1],N\in\mathbb{N}.

On the other hand,

(\Psi\circ\Phi)(W)(t)=\Psi(B)(t)=B_{\{t\}}^{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor},\quad t\geq 0.

Applying the continuous mapping theorem for the continuous function from Lemma
60
yields

(\Psi\circ\Phi)(X^{N})\to\Psi(B),

in distribution, as
N\to\infty
.

Chapter 8
Appendix

23
A Remark on Kurtosis

The
kurtosis
of a distribution is its standardized fourth central moment. That is, given a random variable
Z
with
\mathbb{E}Z^{4}<\infty
it is defined by

\Kurt Z=\frac{\mathbb{E}[(Z-\mathbb{E}[Z])^{4}]}{(\operatorname{Var}Z)^{2}}.

Note that
\Kurt Z\geq 1
by Jensen’s inequality. Further, kurtosis is invariant under affine transformations, that is

\Kurt(aZ+b)=\Kurt(Z).

This property is of great importance in regards to machine learning, because this means that the typical pre-processing steps of centering and dividing by the standard deviation do not affect the kurtosis of the features (or labels).
In other words, the presence of
\Kurt\bm{x}
in the expression for
\Sigma(\theta)
cannot be explained away by a standardization of
\bm{x}
.

For convenience, here is a list of common distributions and their kurtosises.

Dist.

\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda)

\operatorname{Poi}(\lambda)

\chi^{2}_{n}

\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})

\mathcal{U}[a,b]

\operatorname{Lognormal}(\mu,\sigma^{2})

Kurt.

9

3+\frac{1}{\lambda}

3+\frac{12}{n}

3

\frac{9}{5}

e^{4\sigma^{2}}+3e^{3\sigma^{2}}+3e^{2\sigma^{2}}-3

Further, if
p\in[0,1]
and
Z\sim\operatorname{Bin}(1,p)
, then

\Kurt Z=\frac{3p^{2}-3p+1}{p(1-p)}

which has minimum
1
at
\frac{1}{2}
. That is, a symmetric Bernoulli attains the smallest possible Kurtosis of
1
.

If
\Kurt Z=3
, then we say
Z
(or its distribution) is
mesokurtic
. If
\Kurt Z>3
, then
Z
is called
platykurtic
and we call
Z

leptokurtic
for
\Kurt Z<3
. These terms also delineate the settings for the error expansions in Section
9.0.1
.

Finally, we remark that the common interpretation of kurtosis as heaviness of the tails of a distribution is somewhat misleading. Let us suppose the distribution of
Z
is unimodal, for simplicity. Then, according to
7
, kurtosis is
“vaguely […] the location- and scale-free movement of probability mass from the shoulders of a distribution into its center and tails […]”
, that is higher kurtosis implies
both
higher peakedness as well as heavier tails. The term
shoulders
refers roughly to the area between the tails and the center.
For multimodal distributions, the interpretation of kurtosis is a lot more involved or perhaps not even well understood. We will restrict our attention to unimodal distributions only (which includes all previous examples).

24
Extension of
\mathcal{C}^{l}
maps

Consider bounded intervals
I_{1},\dots,I_{m},\Theta=I_{1}\times\dots I_{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-m}
and a Fréchet space
F
.
In this Section we demonstrate why functions
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(\Theta,F)
space can be smoothly extended to an open set containing
\Theta
.

Lemma 61
.

Let
F
be a Fréchet space,
d\in\mathbb{N},U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open, and
f:U\to F
a function. Then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,F)
if and only if
\ell\circ f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,\mathbb{R})
for all continuous linear functionals
\ell\in F^{\prime}
.

Proof 24.1
.

Omitted.

Lemma 62
.

Let
(F,(\|\cdot\|_{p})_{p\in\mathbb{N}})
be graded Fréchet space,
l,m,d\in\mathbb{N},I_{1},\dots,I_{m}
be bounded intervals, and define

\Theta=I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Let
f:\Theta\to F\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(\Theta,F)
.
Then there exist an open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\Theta\subset U
and a map
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,F)
such that
\tilde{f}|_{\Theta}=f
.

Proof 24.2

(Proof sketch)
.

We first treat the case

\Theta=[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1},

Given
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
1
construct a linear extension operator

\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{a}:W^{l,p}(\Theta,\mathbb{R})\to W^{l,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

using the reflection formula

(\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{a}g)(x):=\begin{cases}g(x),&x>0,\\
\sum_{j=0}^{l+1}(-1)^{a}\lambda_{j}g(-j,x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}),&x<0,\end{cases}

for suitably chosen constants
(\lambda_{j})_{j=0,\dots,{l+1}}
so that the derivatives up to order
l
match at
0
. Write
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}=\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
.
They show that given
g\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(\Theta)
we have

\mathcal{E}g\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ and }\partial^{\alpha}\mathcal{E}g=\mathcal{E}_{\alpha(1)}\partial^{\alpha}g,\quad|\alpha|\leq l.

(67)

Now, let
f:\Theta\to F\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(\Theta,F)
. Note that by definition
\partial^{\alpha}f
extends continuously to
\Theta
for
|\alpha|\leq k
.
Define
\mathcal{E}f
be the same reflection formula as above, i.e.

(\mathcal{E}f)(x):=\begin{cases}f(x),&x>0,\\
\sum_{j=0}^{l+1}\lambda_{j}f(-j,x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}),&x<0,\end{cases}

for suitably chosen constants
(\lambda_{j})_{j=0,\dots,{l+1}}
.
For
\ell\in F^{\prime}
, we have

(\ell\circ\mathcal{E}f)(x)=\begin{cases}(\ell\circ f)(x),&x>0,\\
\sum_{j=0}^{l+1}\lambda_{j}(\ell\circ f)(-j,x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}),&x<0,\end{cases}

that is
\ell\circ\mathcal{E}f=\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(\ell\circ f)
, where
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
is the extension operator for
\mathbb{R}
-valued functions.

Note that
\ell\circ f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(\Theta,\mathbb{R})
by Lemma
61
. Property (
67
) implies
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(\ell\circ f)\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
. Since
\ell\in F^{\prime}
was arbitrary, we conclude
\mathcal{E}f\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d},F)
by Lemma
61
.

The case
\Theta=[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
whith
a\leq b
can be reduced to the case
\Theta=[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
using a smooth partition of unity of
\mathbb{R}
. By iterating this construction we can treat the case
\Theta=I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-m}
for closed intervals
I_{1},\dots,I_{m}
.

Finally, consider

\Theta=I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
I_{1},\dots,I_{m}
are arbitrary bounded intervals. We can enlarge
\Theta
to the closure

\overline{\Theta}=\overline{I_{1}}\times\cdots\times\overline{I_{m}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-m}.

Since
f
and its derivatives can be continuously and uniquely extended to
\overline{\Theta}
, we can use the extension property for closed intervals we have deduced.
Thus, there exists an open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\overline{\Theta}\subseteq U
and a function
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{C}^{l}(U,F)
which restricts to this extension of
f
on
\overline{\Theta}
. In particular,
\tilde{f}|_{\Theta}=f
.
